#6960. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Governance of the Open Secure AI Alliance has moved to the Linux Foundation, giving the initiative what the organizations describe as a neutral home for developing open source tools, shared standards and defensive practices. The Alliance was launched by NVIDIA in July to develop open security technologies for AI systems and agents.

The federal Education Freedom Tax Credit (EFTC) is a new source of funding for education that could benefit 90% of students in states that have opted in to the program. However, some confusion and misconceptions exist surrounding details of the program, including which programs may qualify, how parents can apply for scholarships, and how public school students can benefit.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

AI security is entering a new stage as enterprise organizations look beyond protecting models from prompt injection, unauthorized access, and data exposure.

#6956. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

#6953. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6946. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6932. Presidents, Bullfighters, and California—Elaine de Kooning Painted Whatever She Wanted

And you can see it all at a new retrospective at Berry Campbell gallery in New York

In the history books, Elaine de Kooning is often eclipsed by her husband, the famed painter Willem. This fate befell the women in the abstract-expressionist movement, including Lee Krasner, Grace Hartigan, Joan Mitchell, and Helen Frankenthaler, who became known as the “Ninth Street Women,” all of whom received their flowers after decades of being overshadowed by their male counterparts. But you simply cannot tell the story of mid-century art—and abstract expressionism—without Elaine.

De Kooning, who died in 1989 at 70, sat at the epicenter of the art world for five decades. She deftly balanced two parallel practices in portraiture and abstract expressionism, unafraid to allow these genres to intermingle. Beyond her painting, she founded the artist collective the Club in Greenwich Village and wrote art criticism for Art News, celebrating artists like Arshile Gorky and Jackson Pollock. President John F. Kennedy, New York artist Toni Ross, former Art News editor in chief Thomas Hess, and American painter Alex Katz are among the artist’s impressive roster of sitters. A number of these portraits currently hang at New York’s Berry Campbell gallery. Each image frames its subject with expressive brushwork and vivid, bright colors. Among these works, most featuring starry figures, is a young boy in a cowboy hat, featured in the painting Jemp #1 (1963). This little kid is De Kooning’s nephew and the reason the show exists.

Gallerists Martha Campbell and Christine Berry connected with Chris Luyckx, the nephew, who had preserved De Kooning’s paintings for decades and felt her work deserved renewed attention. By the time they met, roughly a decade had passed since De Kooning’s last notable show, a retrospective at the National Portrait Gallery in 2015, followed by smaller shows that focused on specific periods of her life. “By doing these individual shows, you miss the incredible through line of her work from the late 1940s until the day she died in 1989,” says Campbell. “It goes with our ethos of always believing in the artist from the very beginning until the very end.” Berry Campbell’s show, simply titled “Elaine De Kooning” and on view through December 19, celebrates the artist in her entirety, featuring around 30 works sourced directly from the family’s collection.

De Kooning’s oeuvre retains its wonder by evading classification, collapsing boundaries between portraiture and abstract expressionism to forge a distinct visual sensibility. Part of her practice fit firmly in the world of abstract art. Paintings like Untitled (1960) reveled in obscuring the world around her; here, she depicts New York’s verticality. “Elaine de Kooning was an abstract expressionist, but as a woman artist, she didn’t have dealers breathing down her neck saying, ‘Paint more of what we see as abstract expressionism,’ ” Campbell says, noting that this provided the artist with wiggle room.

This should not overshadow her exceptional command of pure abstraction. The 11-foot-wide California (1961), a barrage of vivid colors underscoring De Kooning’s compositional mastery, sits at the very back of the gallery. Berry affirms “it is the most important piece in the exhibition,” noting that “there is no other painting of that scale or magnitude or from that time period on the market, because every other painting that is like that is in a museum collection.” Doubling down on its consequence, Campbell recalls, “There’s a great note that we found from [legendary art dealer] Joan Washburn that says that when she was her dealer, she said, ‘This is the best painting that Elaine de Kooning has ever painted.’ ”

Elements of her abstract work often defined her portraiture. Energetic bursts of bright paint consumed her subjects. Tom Hess (1963), for instance, features the titular journalist among flurries of orange, yellow, and red paint, leaving most of the canvas untouched. “She was an abstract expressionist, but within her own definition of what that was, so she used these abstract expressionist brushstrokes to glorify what she loved.”

De Kooning loved her subjects, occasionally obsessing over one topic and painting it repeatedly, ranging from bullfighting and baseball to the painter Aristodimos Kaldis and President Kennedy. This new show highlights all of these hyperfocused periods, including a standout from her paintings of the former president. Her connection to him started in 1962, when Kennedy sat for De Kooning in Palm Beach to complete a commission for the Harry S. Truman Presidential Library & Museum. The fixation intensified after the president’s assassination in 1963. JFK #7 (1963), on view at the gallery, is only one example of a year’s worth of works.

“She did get fixated on JFK, because she was painting him and then he was shot,” Berry explains. “That was pretty shocking. She painted him for a full year without stopping. People only focus on JFK. But she did portraits from the beginning, in the 1940s, all the way through. She did self-portraits, as well as portraits of her neighbors and her boss, Tom Hess. The portraits are a through line of her career.”

A sophisticated command of color is evident across De Kooning’s entire body of work. Bright colors clash in her abstract works, and in her portraiture, color builds texture. Perhaps most surprising, however, is that De Kooning often avoided looking at color when creating her images. For instance, she preferred to use black-and-white newspaper clippings as references for her basketball paintings, imbuing them with color guided by her personal sensibility. “Color is key to what she does,” Berry explains, pointing to the portrait of poet Frank Lima, a friend with a drug problem whom De Kooning rendered in muted greens.

One painting, Bacchus #20-G (1983), an image of Jules Dalou’s The Triumph of Silenus painted six years before her death, epitomizes what’s so striking about De Kooning’s paintings. The lush painting features the sculpture’s twisted bodies, rendered in an opulent violet, with a backdrop of verdant green. It demonstrates her capacity to seamlessly balance portraiture and abstract expressionism, a testament to her unwavering devotion to her distinct visual language. This vision, after years on the fringe, finally receives the flower it deserves, alongside, rather than below, her husband.

#6931. Designers Say These Are the 10 Best Antiques to Find at Estate Sales

Thrift stores and flea markets are popular spots for finding unusual vintage pieces, but they’re not the only options. Estate sales let you browse antiques in the very homes where they’ve been used and collected over the years. And since many items haven’t passed through a dealer, they may cost less than similar finds at an antique shop.

That combination makes estate sales a favorite among interior designers. They’re a source of characterful pieces for both client projects and personal collections. Krystal Reinhard, founder and principal designer of Old Soul Design Studio, likes being able to see items in the settings where they’ve long belonged. “It makes it easier to spot quality, scale, and patina,” she says.

For Valerie Helgeson, founder and lead designer at Design Directions, the appeal of estate sales is the fast-paced nature of the hunt. “There’s an immediacy to them that I like. You have to make a decision and trust your instincts,” she shares.

Whether you’re after a bargain, an authentic antique, or the fun of finding an unexpected treasure, estate sales are worth exploring. Ahead, interior designers reveal the 10 antiques they always look for—and explain why each belongs on your estate sale wish list.

Paige Loperfido, founder and principal designer of Decor & More Design Studio, is always game to grab hand-painted porcelain, midcentury ceramics, and antique glassware, like ice buckets and goblets.

“These collectibles bring color, pattern, and personality to kitchens and dining rooms,” she says. “Beyond their practical use, they easily transition into art, with vintage plates mounted on walls for a dynamic display, or grouped on open shelves. In dining spaces, glassware and tableware act as statement pieces, highlighting one-of-a-kind treasures that feel timeless, sophisticated, and thoughtfully curated, adding both charm and character to special gatherings.”

Reinhard calls antique candlestick holders “an instant layer,” with their warmth, height, and “a little romance with a surprisingly big impact for a small spend.” She adds that she often styles them as matched pairs on mantels and consoles, or as loose trios at varying heights on dining tables and coffee tables.

Going into estate sales with an open mind, Helgeson typically finds herself buying something she “could have never imagined looking for.” But frequently, one item tops her wish list, and that’s copper kitchenware. She displays these antique pieces in kitchens alongside other estate sale–found items, such as turned-wood bowls, old cutting boards, and bread bowls.

Pottery is a designer darling, since these pieces add texture and even imperfection (in a good way) to spaces, as Reinhard puts it. She tracks down pottery at estate sales, then showcases it on open shelving, sideboards, and tablescapes. She prefers to pair pottery with “something refined,” like brass, stone, or glass, to keep the mix elevated.

Loperfido’s favorite estate sale gems are what she calls “culturally rich pieces” that have been collected by homeowners from around the world. Think: Chinese antiques like foo dogs and ginger jars, and vintage ceramics such as majolica fish candlesticks, figurative pieces, or midcentury glazed pottery.

Oftentimes, she says that these particular pieces “showcase incredible craftsmanship and character,” adding that they’re perfect to layer on surfaces throughout the house, from bookshelves to nightstands to end tables.

At estate sales, Helgeson is scanning the spaces for “furniture with a strong silhouette.” Acting as anchors in rooms, Helgeson believes in accessorizing the rest of these spaces with vintage finds, which she says makes even new pieces appear like collected treasures.

“Vintage rugs bring color, pattern, and age instantly,” Reinhard remarks. She decorates with them “everywhere”: in powder rooms, mudrooms, and under seating areas to anchor the furniture and add depth.

“Original art can feel inaccessible at gallery pricing, but estate sales are one of the best places to find something truly special for a fraction of the cost,” Reinhard points out. She goes for single statement pieces, small collected clusters, or creates a full gallery wall that combines vintage art with modern pieces and personal photos.

Continually on the lookout for leather-bound collections and cocktail books, Reinhard snaps up antique books at estate sales.

“They’re one of the easiest ways to add personality and story—especially art, history, and travel titles that feel timeless and intentional,” she says. “I use them everywhere—stacked on coffee tables, tucked onto consoles, layered into shelves, or used as risers for objects to create height and visual rhythm.”

Loperfido says that these “finely detailed etched artworks” are often overlooked at estate sales, but they can instantly add sophistication and historical texture to any space.

“Small but rich in visual storytelling, they work beautifully against neutral or patterned backgrounds,” she notes.

#6929. A Definitive Guide to the Best Black Boots

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Out of all the fall fashion purchases you make this season, there’s one item that is guaranteed to give you maximum mileage in the months ahead: a pair of black boots.

For those who favor a sleek silhouette to elevate and anchor your outfits, a classic knee-high boot complements dresses and skirts across the board. If you’d like your boots to serve as more of a personality piece, why not test drive a Western-inspired pair or one detailed with moto hardware? Of course, timeless shapes like square-toe booties and Chelsea boots will always be reliable go-tos; trendier styles like the slouchy boot still feel just as versatile as they did decades ago.

Ahead, we break down nine different black boot styles, from equestrian-inspired riding boots to all-weather lug-sole boots. Find the right one for you in our guide to the best black boots of the season.

A quintessential knee-high black boot can be worn layered beneath dresses and skirts or styled over slim trousers and denim. Let your personal preference inform the height. Flat or block heels are best for commuters, while a higher heel can transition from the workday to evening seamlessly.

If you’re not yet a Western-boot convert, the easiest way to style these is to contrast them with wardrobe staples that don’t feel cowboy-adjacent. Whether you’re letting your boots peek out from beneath a pair of wool trousers or opting for a pair with subtler motifs, there’s always a way to make them feel your own.

Saint Laurent Dylan 45 Patent Leather Boots

A traditional Chelsea-boot silhouette is the workhorse of many wardrobes. While a classic take on this style makes a great investment, don’t miss modern interpretations, like the Row’s, which are cut extra low.

Manolo Blahnik Chelsa Leather Chelsea Ankle Booties

Whether you’re going for moto-inspired hardware or something with a bit of downtown edge (Coach and Vince, for example), a silver buckle is the kind of detail that adds dimension while staying versatile.

Manolo Blahnik Calica Buckled Biker Boots

For instant polish, slip into a pair of black riding boots. This equestrian-inspired silhouette is both classic and distinct, ideal with preppy separates like pleated skirts and boxy blazers.

The Row Nobilis Leather Knee-High Riding Boots

Khaite, Chloé, and several other designers included the slouchy boot in their fall 2026 collections, highlighting how this style has shifted away from its ’80s associations and in a much more compelling direction (particularly when rendered in classic black).

Preparing for the winter means being ready for the storms ahead, and a pair of lug-sole boots is a must. The season’s best styles offer all-weather protection (and are a stylish snow-boot alternative) while still feeling versatile enough to wear on glorious days when the weather is agreeable.

Not quite an ankle boot or a knee-high either, the mid-calf boot is shaping up to be one of the season’s most interesting shapes. Need proof? Just look to the designers embracing it, from cool-girl favorite Maryam Nassir Zadeh to buzzy newcomer Jude.

There’s a time and place for a pointed boot, but a sleek square toe always manages to feel a touch cooler. Look for styles with a slimmer, subtler square toe so you can style them for any occasion.

#6928. These 7 Storage Solutions for Small Spaces Look Too Good to Hide

Display-worthy baskets, glass-fronted cabinets, and colorful tins can make organization part of your decor.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From a Brooklyn studio to a narrow Baltimore rowhouse, two decades of small-space living have taught me how to make every square foot count. Add in my Type A Virgo tendencies and an appreciation for good design, and I have strong opinions about storage: Namely, it should be as good-looking as it is hard-working. My first stop when hunting for products that deliver just that is always IKEA. The Swedish retailer has an unmatched selection of storage furniture and decor. Here are seven pieces that offer both function and form.

I have never known the pleasure of a linen closet, but I’ve gotten so many compliments on this metal-and-glass cabinet in my bathroom that I no longer mind. The reeded glass offers an unexpected texture while obscuring what’s inside to reduce visual clutter, and each interior shelf is roomy enough to hold a stack of towels, plus a small bin for toiletries and medicine bottles. I like to use the bamboo UPPDATERA boxes for material contrast; the larger style works great on the lower shelves to tame my mess of serums, makeup, lotions, and the like.

There is no such thing as too many bins or baskets when you live in a small space. They are the unsung organizational heroes, giving you a way to stash all the miscellaneous things that would typically be tucked away in a closet, attic, basement, or even a spare room in a larger home. IKEA sells a huge variety, but the woven styles like this one are particularly lovely to keep on display. I use them on my EKET living room shelves for games, in my bedroom for scarves and bags, and in the kitchen for tea towels and napkins.

When square footage is at a premium, closets tend to get eliminated. A free-standing wardrobe is the solution to creating clothes storage in a small bedroom. This chic new design features sliding doors—a space-saver itself since you won’t need to account for door clearance—adjustable shelves, and two drawers.

If your front door, like mine, opens directly into your living space, you need to fake a foyer as best you can. The key to that is shoe storage—otherwise, guests will be staring at muddy boots the minute they walk into your home. This cabinet hides 14 pairs behind a traditional design, and sticks out from the wall less than a foot. Two drawers make space for keys and sunglasses while four hooks give bags and dog leashes a home.

All kitchens can benefit from more storage, but it’s the small cook spaces where you really need to get creative. A mobile cart offers that utility, and this one features three shelves for anything from spices and baking supplies to serveware or a coffee bar.

Another simple small kitchen storage idea: Keep your dry goods in stackable containers, like the BETTHAJ tins, to maximize your pantry shelf space. They’re far easier to handle than flimsy paper containers or plastic bags, plus, they look great on a countertop or open shelving. They come in five different sizes, from 19 oz. to 3.3 qt., so you can mix and match to suit your needs.

Why choose between extra seating or more storage when you can have both? This sturdy little pouf hides a compartment that I use for an extra throw blanket, but it could also fit a few games, your knitting supplies, or remote controls. The cushioned top is taut enough that it can also function as a side table, making this a small space triple threat.

Head to IKEA to find storage that maximizes space without sacrificing style.

#6921. Safe Word: The Best Jewelry Launches of November 2026

Safe Word is a series highlighting the latest trends in the jewelry market, keeping you up to date on all things that sparkle and shine.

November’s jewelry launches are looking both backward and forward, with designers revisiting their roots, reworking archival ideas, and stepping into entirely new territory. First up, Pomellato reaches back to 1997 to revive “Moneta Orientale” as a colorful modern talisman; David Yurman celebrates the magic of a New York holiday with Michael B. Jordan, Yerin Ha, and Inde Navarrette; Vhernier revisits its iconic “Trottola” design with new proportions and contrasting materials; Di Volpe reinterprets the classic tennis bracelet with customizable gemstones in “DiSegno”; and Brent Neale transforms the classic bow into sculptural fine jewelry with “Wrap It Up.” From archival revivals and meaningful motifs to innovative designs and new creative directions, November’s launches prove that sometimes the best way forward starts with looking back. For the rest of the month, we’ve got you covered on all the jewelry-related happenings worth knowing about.

Pomellato Revives Its 1997 Moneta Orientale Pendant as a Colorful Talisman

Pomellato is looking to its archives for its latest good-luck charm. For the newest chapter of “Memorie d’Archivio,” the maison revives Moneta Orientale, a 1997 pendant inspired by the ancient symbolism of a circle surrounding a square, representing the harmony between heaven and earth. Made in 33 stone-and-pavé combinations, the design pairs colorful hard stones including black jade, amazonite, sodalite, rhodochrosite, and labradorite with Pomellato’s signature irregular pavé of diamonds, sapphires, rubies, and more. Offered in two proportions, each pendant transforms the archival motif into a modern talisman for prosperity and protection.

Bibi van der Velden Makes Her High Jewelry Debut With ‘Eclipse’

Bibi van der Velden is entering a new era with “Eclipse,” the designer’s first high jewelry collection and most refined exploration of diamonds to date. Inspired by the fleeting moment when light gives way to darkness, the collection includes two six-piece suites: one in white diamonds and another defined by an ombré of diamonds into gray spinels deepening into black. Van der Velden’s signature alligator motif is abstracted into fluid, sculptural silhouettes, with scales engraved into the white gold on the reverse of each piece. “With Eclipse, I wanted to take those familiar elements and transform them into something more abstract and sculptural,” she says. “It’s a collection about transformation, which has always been at the heart of my work.”

Aflalo and Leandra Medine Cohen Turn Everyday Indulgences Into Fine Jewelry

Aflalo is finding the humor in our obsession with wellness with “The Vanities,” a new two-piece fine jewelry capsule designed exclusively by Leandra Medine Cohen. Playing with the contradiction between longevity and life’s little indulgences, the collection transforms two unlikely everyday accessories into precious objects. The Memento Mori is a 14-karat yellow gold cigar band set with more than four carats of diamonds, designed to slip over an Oura Ring, while The Libertine is a sterling silver pendant case adorned with diamonds and a wild-haired woman outlined in gold, designed to hold nicotine pouches or other small essentials. Equal parts extravagant and irreverent, the collection makes a playful case for enjoying life’s pleasures without sacrificing personal style.

Julia Berolzheimer’s Hart Jewelry Collection Draws on Childhood Memories

Hart is teaming up with Julia Berolzheimer for a new jewelry collection that celebrates the art of the stack. Inspired by Berolzheimer’s childhood memories of her grandmother’s California ranch and her collection of Art Deco minaudières, the collaboration brings together whimsical charms, colorful gemstones, and meaningful talismans designed to be mixed, layered, and restyled. Botanical motifs take shape in Tree of Life coins, daisies, bees, and fruit-inspired charms, while carnelian, turquoise, and pink opal add playful pops of color. One standout is the Perfume Vesta Case, a modern interpretation of a Victorian-era accessory that can hold a sample-sized fragrance and hang alongside other charms. With interchangeable chains, cords, and pendants, the collection makes a case for jewelry that feels just as personal as the memories behind it.

David Yurman Captures the Magic of a New York Holiday

David Yurman is celebrating the magic of the holidays in New York City with a new campaign featuring global brand ambassador Michael B. Jordan alongside actresses Yerin Ha and Inde Navarrette. Set against a snow-covered Washington Square Park, the campaign captures a series of quintessential New York moments, from Jordan and Ha facing off in a spirited card game to Navarrette hailing a cab in search of the perfect holiday gift. Throughout the campaign, the cast wears some of the house’s most recognizable designs, including 18-karat gold Cable bracelets and pieces from the “Starburst” and “Armory” collections. Directed by Bradford Young and photographed by Drew Vickers, the campaign brings together the spirit of the city, the joy of togetherness, and the timeless appeal of David Yurman’s jewelry.

Vhernier Reintroduces Its Iconic ‘Trottola’ Design With New Proportions

Vhernier is revisiting one of its most recognizable designs with a new interpretation of “Trottola,” the sculptural collection originally introduced in 2003. Inspired by the continuous motion of a spinning top, the latest designs play with proportions, contrasting materials, and asymmetrical shapes across rings, bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Polished rose gold meets glossy black jet, with select pieces featuring diamond pavé for an added contrast of light and dark. The collection also focuses on versatility, from rings designed for stacking to convertible earclips that can be worn as studs or dangling earrings. With its fluid forms and unexpected material combinations, “Trottola” brings a fresh perspective to Vhernier’s signature sculptural approach to jewelry.

Di Volpe is rethinking the classic tennis bracelet with “DiSegno,” a new customizable fine jewelry system designed to change with its wearer. Using concealed, patent-pending connections, individual cultured diamonds and gemstones, called Elements, can be removed, rearranged, or added by hand, allowing wearers to transform bracelets into necklaces or earrings without compromising the seamless look of traditional tennis jewelry. The collection launches with 28 stone-and-cut combinations, including rubies, emeralds, sapphires, and diamonds, alongside a limited-edition collaboration with Lebanese artist Ali Cha’aban featuring five sculptural designs. With new cuts, colors, and artist collaborations planned for future releases, “DiSegno” makes the case for fine jewelry that’s never quite finished.

Brent Neale Riffs on the Bow in Fine Jewelry

Brent Neale is putting a bow on the holiday season with “Wrap It Up,” a new fine jewelry collection that transforms the familiar ribbon motif into sculptural works of art. Drawing inspiration from Old Hollywood glamour and the femininity of the 1960s, the designer plays with the contrast between rigid precious metals and the soft movement of fabric, crafting bows that twist, curl, and unfurl in 18-karat gold and platinum. Colored gemstones sit nestled within the ribbon-like forms, while pavé diamonds accentuate every curve and fold. Equal parts playful and precious, the collection brings a little gift-wrapped glamour to the jewelry box.

Grace Winfrey’s ‘Poppy Fortuna’ Collection Reimagines Good-Luck Charms

Grace Winfrey is bringing a little extra luck to the jewelry box with “Poppy Fortuna,” a new fine jewelry collection inspired by the natural beauty of the Italian landscape and symbols of good fortune. Scarlet poppies, sunflowers, bees, four-leaf clovers, horseshoes, and ladybugs come to life through vibrant gemstones and intricate pavé, creating miniature mosaics of color and texture. Highlights include a Lucky Bangle combining three good-luck charms, sculptural sunflower earrings with tiny ladybugs tucked into their petals, and layered charm necklaces designed to feel like modern keepsakes. Handcrafted in New York City, each piece transforms familiar symbols of prosperity, protection, and happiness into playful, colorful works of art.

Robinson Pelham Channels ’90s Glamour for Its Anniversary

Robinson Pelham is closing out its 30th anniversary celebrations with “Suite 96,” a new campaign and collection launch inspired by the glamour of late-’90s London. Set inside a private suite at St. James’s Hotel & Club in Mayfair, the campaign considers jewelry the ultimate room service, with diamonds arriving on trays, gold spilling out of wardrobes, and plenty of pieces to layer before a night out. At the center is “Studio 30,” a four-piece anniversary collection featuring diamond necklaces and statement earrings, including the sculptural Via and Hira necklaces, cascading diamond drops, and the bold Solstice Supreme. Alongside the anniversary designs, the house introduces new collections and additions to existing favorites, from chunky gold links and graphic heart rings to colorful enamel and gemstone combinations. Together, the launches celebrate three decades of Robinson Pelham’s bold, playful approach to fine jewelry.

Le Vian and Jared Revive Georgian-Era Romance

Le Vian is bringing a little old-world romance to Jared Jewelers with its new exclusive “Georgian Chocolate & Nude Diamonds” collection. Inspired by the candlelit jewels of the Georgian era, when jewelry was designed to capture and reflect the warmth of natural light, the collection rethinks historic buttercup settings in polished Honey Gold, with eight rounded prongs framing each natural diamond. The designs pair Le Vian’s signature Chocolate and Nude Diamonds with warm gold detailing across solitaire rings, anniversary bands, tennis bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Select styles introduce colorful gemstones including Chocolate Quartz, Green Apple Peridot, and Grape Amethyst, giving the vintage-inspired silhouettes a contemporary update. With its sculptural settings and warm-toned stones, the collection brings the romance of antique jewelry into the present.

Wempe’s ‘Au:thentic’ Celebrates the Art of Goldsmithing

Wempe’s new gender-inclusive fine jewelry collection, “Au:thentic,” puts craftsmanship front and center, finding inspiration in the foundational skills taught to aspiring goldsmiths. The collection takes its shape from the jump ring, one of the first techniques Patrick Sieroslawski learned as an apprentice more than 22 years ago, translating its form to necklaces, bracelets, rings, earrings, and pendants. Handcrafted at Wempe’s atelier in Germany, the pieces come in 18-karat yellow, white, and rose gold, with select styles set with diamonds. “Au:thentic” also marks the beginning of a multiyear rollout, with three additional chapters set to expand on its design language through twists, wraps, and interlocking links.

#6918. 7 Celebrity-Approved Ways to Wear Flats With Jeans This Fall

Stylish celebrities confirm that the timeless duo is also one of the coolest.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Today, the humble flat has become just as much of a wardrobe staple as a trusty pair of jeans. And styling them together? That’s practically a no-brainer. Everything from classic ballet flats and loafers to ornate Mary Janes looks especially sharp with structured, dark-wash denim and can instantly elevate baggy or light-wash jeans. With a seemingly infinite selection of chic designs in both categories, it’s an easy-to-repeat formula for all kinds of occasions: strolling through your city on the weekend, exploring a new one, heading into the office, or dressing for a night out.

Some of the most fashionable celebrities are making the combo look cooler than ever, which is why we’ve spotlighted a few of our favorite Hollywood-approved jeans-and-flats getups to inspire your upcoming outfits. From supermodels like Bella Hadid and Kendall Jenner to Zoë Kravitz, find seven impeccable flats-and-jeans ensembles worth copying this fall.

Offset simple black ballet flats and a matching cropped cardigan with a pair of no-waistband jeans, à la Zoë Kravitz. Topped off with a pear-cut tennis necklace, the look is the epitome of elegance with an edge.

Aritzia HomeStretch Rib Spectrum Cami Tank

Schutz Arissa Leather Bow Ballerina Flats

Pointed-toe flats and a sweeping trench make for a flawless French girl-inspired fall outfit. Kendall Jenner wore the duo last year in Paris with crisp, ankle-length mom jeans and a sleek top-handle bag for a chic business-casual vibe.

Michael Kors Hamilton Large Leather Tote Bag

Striped pullovers are trending this season, as are croc-embossed bags to top off the look. For a touch of elevated Y2K nostalgia, mirror Bella Hadid’s approach and add a grommeted belt in rich leather.

From bejeweled to feathered and embroidered, embellished shoes had a huge moment on fall 2026 runways. For milder fall days—and to wear again next spring—style a delicate, adorned sheer flat with a timeless, supermodel-worthy trio: a white button-down, bootcut jeans, and a timeless leather belt. Finish with a retro-inspired zigzag headband the It girls are also loving right now.

Designers, street style stars, and A-list celebrities agree: burgundy flats—a more versatile take on bright red styles—belong in your closet this autumn. During a recent press event for Verity, Dakota Johnson wore Khaite’s Jane glove flats with distressed light-wash jeans, a black top, and a cropped plaid Nili Lotan blazer—proving that, with the right styling, a jeans-and-flats combo can be an equally effortless alternative to a polished dress you might otherwise reach for. Reformation’s Blaine flat and A.L.C.’s Cass jacket offer somewhat more accessible ways to recreate Johnson’s look.

Good American Vintage Straight Ankle Jeans

Eva Chen undoubtedly has a penchant for them, having been spotted wearing them with baggy jeans twice this year. Textured flats might actually be a bit groundbreaking for fall. Katie Holmes proves the traditionally summer-ready style can add eclectic flair to voluminous jeans and a chunky sweater, especially when teamed with thin crew socks for added warmth. Thrown over her shoulder? A spacious, sumptuous suede tote in a deep ink-blue shade.

#6907. Under the Surface

Canali gives a behind-the-scenes look at sartorial excellence: the time, precision and mastery that transform raw materials into a jacket made to last

Under the surface, unseen layers lie. They stay there, silent. They don’t demand the spotlight. They don’t seek glory. And yet, like a beating heart, they form the innermost foundation of any real achievement. Where passion, enthusiasm, and discipline meet, the logic of excellence takes shape. Such is the story of Under the Surface, the newest creative collaboration from Canali.

Under the Surface is a look at the unseen architecture of a sartorial jacket by Canali. Every Canali jacket is born from a complex system of about 200 steps, each executed with meticulous care. Around 15 specialists are involved in the process—one that sees more than 520 feet of thread sewn into 22,000 stitches, both machine- and hand-made. Unseen when looking at the finished garment, the process informs each piece’s quality, comfort, shape, and durability.

It’s not just a matter of talent: rather, it’s about staying faithful to one’s goals, pursuing them with dedication even when no one is watching. It’s then that quality truly makes a difference, and the end result becomes the visible manifestation of a long creation process that demands great effort, experience and willpower.

That commitment of excellence unites the sartorial world of Canali and Ethan Hawke, the brand’s new Global Ambassador. The actor, writer, and director has dedicated almost four decades to perfecting his art, with endless, ever-learning curiosity. For their new Fall/Winter 2026 campaign, Canali has decided to capture what goes on behind the scenes. In the film, Hawke pores over his scripts, rehearses lines, refines each motion: a painstaking introspective process made of research, study, and exercise, preceding each of his performances.

The behind-the-scenes of a Canali jacket starts with selecting the finest fabrics, sourced from the best Italian wool mills. Then, each textile is examined and interpreted. While technology now allows extreme precision and control, some assessments are still left to the eye and expertise of human specialists. Even tiny details such as slightly different shades of color or variations in texture can influence the final product.

The fabric is then stabilized via steaming cycles and specific treatments, to ensure its behavior remains consistent over time. Then it’s time for digital mapping, guiding every cutting room decision: here, each of the jacket’s pieces is carefully positioned to also help keep waste to a minimum. It’s the first irreversible step.

The garment’s three-dimensional structure is then shaped upon the canvas. Canali crafts theirs in-house, adapting it to each model’s fit and fabrics. Made from horsehair, plant fibers, and natural reinforcements, the canvas is modeled on custom-made concave shapes, known as Schiena d’Asino (Donkey’s Back), which replicate the chest’s anatomy. The canvas is crucial for determining how a jacket drapes over the body, avoiding stiffness. It’s here that the volume of the chest, the balance of the silhouette and ease of wearing begin to be defined.

Then it’s time to craft the front, lapel, padded shoulder, and the sleeves. From planning to execution, nothing’s left to chance. The tailor observes, corrects, iterates. It’s a patient process, requiring time, knowledge, and skill. Lastly, the various layers are united through basting, stitching, and ironing processes. As these details interact, the garment takes on its structure and sophisticated character.

Something special also takes place behind the scenes with Hawke: day after day, the actor’s preparation subtly transforms his thoughts and behavior until, almost by instinct, he takes on each role’s intimate characteristics.

Time underpins the whole process. After being assembled and pressed, the jacket is allowed to rest, to dissipate the steam and stabilize the cloth. This is how the fabric finds its balance, and the garment gains a sort of memory: the ability to naturally find its shape again, even after having been worn, folded, or packed into a suitcase.

In over 90 years of heritage, Canali has refined their techniques through repetition, experience, and experimentation. Each garment is built upon the time-tested values of care, know-how, precision. The Canali style is not one made of grand gestures, but daily practice, intimate and subtle: the constant research into a shape that lasts. One hero of the Under the Surface project is the vicuña jacket, available exclusively at selected flagship boutiques by Canali.The campaign, and the collaboration between Canali and Ethan Hawke, speak to the art of shaping oneself, guided by an ambition to do things well with patience and self-awareness. Both in tailoring and in cinema, there is so much quiet work that happens behind the scenes to make creativity possible. Discipline and rigour do not constrain creative freedom; they make it possible. For Canali, excellence takes shape through the craftsmanship with which each element is made. For Ethan Hawke, it is the preparation that allows him to approach each role with confidence.And, as the Under The Surface campaign reveals, each of these steps adds something essential to the finished product. In the case of the Canali cashmere and vicuña blazer (the hero style of the FW26 campaign), it is structure, movement, character, durability, and a lightness that can only be achieved through the combination of expertly-chosen materials and complex fine tailoring. The knowledge required to execute this is almost tangible, felt in the quality of the piece.

The finished result only has value because of the strong, unseen structure underneath. It is a story we can all resonate with. Little by little, layer by layer, day by day, we each build ourselves in pursuit of excellence. Our actions create the people we are – even, or especially, when nobody is watching – in the same way that the apparent simplicity of a piece of clothing hides the depth of work required to craft it. This is the essence of Under the Surface.

#6893. An Everyman’s Guide to Wearing a Necklace

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You already have a watch you like. This is the next step in your jewelry journey.

Men’s jewelry, I’ve learned, can be a slippery slope. I’ve been a watch guy since elementary school, if we’re generous enough to count a dual-sided compass/watch on a carabiner as a watch. In college I added a bracelet. I’ve been a ring guy for over ten years, thanks mostly to being married and partially to getting lunch drunk in Albuquerque, going ring shopping for my wife, and finding a sterling silver bear signet ring that I’ve worn nonstop since. I’ve had an earring for two years, thanks to a quick weekend in Miami and a well-timed Harrison Ford interview clip crossing my algorithm about getting his ear pierced in a Claire’s.

Recently, I’ve been curious about my personal final frontier of men’s jewelry—the necklace. The other listings above are all fairly low entry points, safe experiments in the world of men’s jewelry. The earring may be the exception, but this is 2026; one small silver hoop isn’t that daring, even in the Midwest. A necklace has never really been my thing. I haven’t felt the pull. That is, until this year.

For an unrelated project, I was breaking down some of the signature stylistic calling cards of Robert Redford. And there it was, across paparazzi pictures and movie stills and in memoriam roundups. In almost every photo, Redford has some kind of necklace, usually peeking from a precariously unbuttoned western shirt.

Further study of what we can safely call Hollywood’s golden age of off-duty style shows the same: McQueen and Newman and their contemporaries, swanning around on and off film, always wearing some sort of necklace or chain or pendant.

This is, of course, a common trap: to look at a famously handsome Hollywood star and think, yeah, all that’s separating me from THAT is a necklace. And sure, a good cheekbone and clavicle will get you pretty far in life. There’s no magic amulet here, nothing that will shazam you into leading-man looks. But there are a few lessons on styling from guys whose literal job it was to present themselves favorably to the world. And a few lessons on what necklaces to choose to help get there.

So, I put my professional online shopping style writing gig into overdrive, going deep into the Getty Images archives and confusing Google’s settings by repeatedly searching “Insert Hollywood Icon + shirtless” to glean some jewelry 101 tips from the professionals.

Instagram and (theoretically, since I don’t indulge) TikTok are full of maximalizing style trend chasers. Why wear one necklace when you can wear four? Why two rings when each finger could have two rings on it? Why dip your toes in the water when you can cannonball into the deep end? And sure, there’s some je ne sais quoi about just committing to something fully and completely out of the gate. But there’s also a lot of try-hard to it.

We’re channeling a more reserved, less #sponsoredpost era of dressing here that cares less about link clicks and more about how things look in the real world. So start simple and start with a chain. My favorite, and currently on daily rotation, is the Curb Chain from L.A.-based Bloedstone. Mine is sterling silver and in the inch length, which hits closer to the sternum than the neck. That’s the prescribed Hollywood A-lister way to wear it, but chart your own path if you want to. Miansai has a thicker 2mm Cuban chain that’s great too. And while we’re covering the M’s, Mejuri has one too. To my slight surprise, my home state watchmaker Shinola makes one, and less surprisingly so, Esquire-approved David Yurman has this giant one featuring a marketing campaign with Michael B. Jordan, who is picking up McQueen’s (and subsequently Brosnan’s) torch by adapting The Thomas Crown Affair.

There’s a whole vocabulary on link types and thickness and clasps out there. Don’t worry about that for now. Find something that calls to you and click add to cart. We’re starting slowly here.

Parting note. There are a lot of chains worn over T-shirts these days. To each their own, but the leading man’s move is to keep it under your shirt. If you want to show it off, just ditch the buttons.

A chain is simple, and sure, the greats wore them. But more often than not, they had something dangling from that chain. Medallion, pendant, a bottle opener in Cool Hand Luke. McQueen was often photographed, including in the pages of Esquire, wearing a St. Christopher medallion. Seeing as he’s the patron saint of travelers, St. Christopher medallions are having a bit of a cultural resurgence, partially because of their traditional association as a good-luck charm for travelers. There are some fun enamel ones on eBay, including these surfing-specific ones. Every once in a while, Nashville-based Americana experts Imogene + Willie will pull together a vintage edit of St. Christopher medallions that always sell out in around 24 hours. Speaking of Nashville, if you’re going to go new, go with Yearly Co. The gold jewelry experts have a 14k version of the iconography with a pitch-perfect 24-inch chain. I can attest to the quality—I gave my wife the brand’s diamond huggies and she wears them almost every day. As the grandson of a Protestant minister, I steer away from the saints out of genetic loyalty to Luther’s 95 Theses, but I recently picked up this Perseverance medallion from the brand, inspired by a vintage signet ring. It scratches the McQueen itch with flying colors.

Secondhand gold is also an option. Alloy Market recently came onto my radar, and I’m browsing it daily for myself and gifts for my wife. They’ve got a small but mighty collection of certified men’s items with pendants and medallions you can mix and match. All of the quality without any of the markup.

Don’t limit yourself to just a medallion, though. Get a little funky. Redford did for a while with this turquoise number that somehow looked even cooler hitting under the sternum. Shinola’s got an option there too. Bali-based Salty Dagger has an Odysseus-approved Greek mythology piece. And why not try a locket, like this one from Pattaraphan. Miansai has a great pendant. Get fancy with the Prevaut diamond ring deal. When in doubt, steal from the girls. I got this With Clarity birthstone necklace for my wife that I might take a swing at myself.

Peyote Bird High Desert Sterling Silver Necklace

Shinola 21mm Turquoise Inlay Tag Necklace

From the abbreviated/slightly bastardized Shakespeare: “All the world’s a stage and one man in his time plays many parts.” Jewelry can feel like a risk, even if you’re following some sort of contrived guide to styling and buying it. Sometimes we pretend until we’re comfortable actually being. Redford and McQueen and company were actors. Pretending was their legacy, and they were good at it. Take a leaf from their book and get dressed for the curtain call.

#6888. The Quiet Rise of DeMellier Handbags

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why royals and fashion editors alike love this brand

The Margaux, the Birkin, the Baguette: All are familiar bag styles associated with major fashion houses. Very few handbag-first brands can break through and compete. Enter DeMellier, a British label favored by royals, street-style stars, and fashion editors alike for its understated designs that are big on functionality and eminently reasonable in price.

While many luxury labels are raising prices on handbags, DeMellier’s range hits that sweet spot. Most bag styles ring in between $500 and $600 and capping off well under $1,000. “We believe luxury should be priced with integrity,” says Mireia Llusia-Lindh, creative director and founder of DeMellier London. “This means offering luxury quality and ethical manufacturing without inflated prices by having lower, more reasonable margins than traditional luxury houses.”

Before we hit peak quiet luxury, DeMellier built its foundation on that timeless, minimalist look years ago. (Llusia-Lindh launched Milli Millu in 2010 and rebranded as DeMellier in 2017.) “It’s a philosophy we’ve held since the beginning,” says Llusia-Lindh. “I have always believed in creating pieces that will stand the test of time, avoiding large logos or unnecessary embellishments. For us, luxury is about creating something personal, meaningful, and enduring.”

DeMellier’s New York tote has a minimal, architectural silhouette and is one of the most popular styles from the brand. It’s a bit reminiscent of Celine’s Trapeze, and for Phoebe Philo fans who are nostalgic for the designer’s past artful designs for the label, it fills that void.

The New York tote, which comes in two sizes and different hues and textures, is popular among many fashion editors, including those at Bazaar. “DeMellier has been able to understand that great style and substance are more valuable than trends and fashion. The bags are so chic and provide luxury at a great price point,” says Harper’s Bazaar’s accessories director, Miguel Enamorado.

Plus, the bag can haul a lot: a laptop, a notebook, beauty essentials, headphones, and more. As a New Yorker, I can vouch that it’s aptly named, though it suits any harried commuter’s lifestyle. The long straps are critical when carrying it over a thick jacket or coat. It’s lightweight from the start, so it’s not a drag when filled to the brim. It often prompts compliments and inquiries from friends, curiously asking, “What is that bag?” For me, it instills a sense of confidence and assurance when carrying it.

Earlier this year, DeMellier launched a new counterpart to the New York, called the Brooklyn, which features a similar shape but is equipped with a few design changes, like an interior zipper and a foldover buckle flap.

And for fall, DeMellier introduced new silhouettes like the Sloane (in celebration of its first store opening in London’s Sloane Square) and the Icons 26 collection, a limited-edition capsule where five stylish tastemakers, from Taylor Hill to Romee Strijd, reimagined some of the brand’s signature styles.

DeMellier has a royal fan club too, including Kate Middleton, Meghan Markle, Pippa Middleton, and Princess Beatrice. The Princess of Wales has been spotted carrying the Nano Montreal, a micro toffee-colored bag, on more than one occasion, as well as the small Hudson in mocha suede.

The brand has struck a chord with women searching for elegant, professional handbags that can accompany them during whatever life may throw at them in a given day—an important meeting, a parent-teacher conference, a gym class, a rush to make a bus home, a coffee with friends. Llusia-Lindh emphasizes versatility, noting that she aims for her designs to “transition effortlessly between work, travel, and leisure.” Each bag is named after a metropolitan city, like New York, Tokyo, and Paris, or a more vacation-worthy spot like Capri and Santorini (where you’ll find a range of raffia totes and resort-friendly carryalls).

“DeMellier has demonstrated that they have a true understanding of what the modern handbag customer wants,” shares Marissa Galante Frank, Bloomingdale’s fashion director. “They continue to evolve their silhouettes, update their colorways and use playful new materials like shearling or pony hair. Their handbags have a sophisticated aesthetic at a price point that can appeal to a wide range of customers. Their belted tote styles are ideal for our customers who are looking for chic handbag options that can be worn from day to night.”

Although the brand is based in London, Llusia-Lindh was born in Barcelona and boasts an impressive resumé: an MBA from Harvard Business School and advisory posts at Burberry and LVMH. She is also a mother of three, so she is well-versed in the day-to-day needs of a working woman. “DeMellier reflects my personal values and has always aimed for something more, redefining luxury by putting meaning, female empowerment, ethical craftsmanship, and sustainability at its core,” she says. And at the end of the day, don’t we all just want a bag that gets us from point A to point B and helps us feel powerful getting there?

Below, shop DeMellier’s greatest hits and new fall offerings.

#6887. I've Had This Modular Sofa for Four Years, and Its Secret Twin "Bed" Is a Hosting Game-Changer

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The first couch I ordered for my basement was a bust. No matter how hard they tried, the delivery men couldn’t angle it to get it down the narrow stairs. Because I live in a 1930s home, all the charm turns to chaos when it comes to fitting new furniture. I was literally stuck because a family of five was visiting for the holidays and they had nothing to sit (or sleep) on. Plus, what even is a basement without a comfy couch?

Ideally, I wanted a sleeper sofa to accommodate my guests and let my kids have sleepovers. But there was absolutely no way I would risk buying another couch that couldn’t come apart and was stuck in a corner. For round two, I knew that I needed a modular sectional. After a bit of research, I came across the Burrow Range sectional and was elated that it could be assembled like a building-block puzzle that can grow over time if I wanted to add more sections.

Since the basement isn’t huge, my selection was a four-piece model that included two arm pieces as well as an ottoman. Because my dreams of a sleeper were no more, I liked that the Range has flippable top cushions that, when pulled back, could make a twin-size bed. Happily, four years later, the sectional has survived many, many sleepovers and now resides in my bedroom. Read on to see how it transitioned from child-hopping to a peaceful nook two floors up.

Best For: A growing household or someone who likes to change the layout of their space often.

Not ideal for: Someone who is very tall. If you are taller than 6 feet, the sofa may sit too low for you.

Price at review (4-piece sectional lounger): $1,271 (originally $2,119)

Bottom Line: The modular sectional is comfortable and won’t sag or end up with flat cushions, even after years of use.

Because I needed the sectional before the winter holidays, I was glad that I could order by early December and still get my sofa before Santa came to town. The four big boxes for the cushions and a little box for legs came to my front door within a week.

My husband and I brought down all the boxes to the basement where we started to unbox and assemble. The overall process took no more than 30 minutes. In a nutshell, you need to line up the holes with the metal braces and secure those with large plastic knob screws. The cushions have two strips of Velcro that stick to the black fabric at the base to keep them from sliding out from beneath.

The sofa pieces are easy to secure together.

Compared to my living room couch, which is a mid-century modern style West Elm reversible sectional, the Burrow has a bit more bounce to the cushion. While the seats are softer, I would still describe it as medium-firm. As soon as I sit down, it feels relaxing. Because it is only 28 inches high, it is a little lower than I am used to. (This still works out because I can slouch down and stretch my feet up on the ottoman when I watch a movie.)

Later, when I moved the sectional up to my bedroom, I lie across all three seat cushions, with my head resting on the arm cushion, as if I am reading in bed. The arm cushions are slightly angled, with more padding at the bottom to create a gentle incline that supports my head.

I chose the Stone Gray upholstery, an almost salt-and-pepper look, in performance flatweave. This is my third woven upholstery sofa, and it is the softest. It feels similar to a worn-in tweed jacket. I think it actually got a bit softer with age. I am happy to report there is absolutely no pilling or loose threads.

My biggest pet peeve is saggy couch cushions. I cannot stand it when the fabric looks crumpled on the seats, and there are sloppy valleys on the back cushions. After having this sectional for four years, I can vouch that it still looks the same as the day we assembled it. Which is a small miracle because when the Range lived in the basement, it was base camp for elementary-age boys playing video games and jumping around. If for some reason you wanted to fluff, you can unzip the underside of the back cushions and take out the filling to give it more shape.

In terms of stains and spills, if any kids spilled something on the sofa, I don’t know about it because it has no markings. Come to think of it, I don’t think I’ve ever had to clean the sofa beyond brushing off crumbs.

I did catch my cat trying to stretch (scratch) on the back of the sectional a few times, but I shooed him away, and he did not make it a habit. When I checked, there were never any loops or snags. (I cannot say the same about another woven sofa that is a victim of his claws.)

The cushions are still in terrific shape.

The flexibility of the Burrow Range sectional is everything. That’s the main reason I would highly recommend this couch. Not only do the pieces come in individual boxes that can fit through narrow doorways of older homes, but you can customize the sectional to your exact needs.

Originally, I needed something to help with hosting my out-of-town family, but this sectional has proven again and again that it’s a high-quality piece that can hold up for years to come. I still can’t believe that the cushions are in tip-top shape.

Pricewise, it’s a solid buy for mid-range furniture, especially because I don’t see a reason to replace it any time soon. Even when I ended up getting a new green modular sectional to go with the new wallpaper that I installed during my basement makeover, I found a spot for it in my bedroom. The Range now serves as a reading nook or a makeshift WFH station when I don’t want to stay in bed but feel too lazy to walk down the stairs to my home office. I added a Häti pouf with a scalloped EtúHome board next to the Burrow ottoman, and it serves as a perch for my coffee.

All in all, I am very happy with this purchase. It is durable, comfortable, and boasts clean lines and a silhouette that is approachable, but a bit more interesting than a standard traditional sofa.
