#6884. Inside the Lavish Estates Where The Gilded Age Was Filmed

These locations are straight out of 1880s high society.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why should the Brits have all the fun? The writer of Downton Abbey, Julian Fellowes, went stateside with the HBO series The Gilded Age, which tells the story of New York City high society in the 1880s amid the halcyon days of the industrial boom. The series explores the tension between old-money elites and their escalating feud with the nouveau riche neighbors who threaten the established social order. Meanwhile in real life, powerhouse families like the Vanderbilts and the Carnegies left their opulent footprints on Newport, Rhode Island, in the form of their famously ostentatious and absolutely gargantuan “summer cottages.” Circumstances which lend themselves to set-design magic.

In honor of season four of The Gilded Age, which premiered November 1, we’re revisiting the real-life settings that have helped bring the series to life. The social alliances may shift, but the architecture remains a dependable scene-stealer. Below, tour 21 properties that rank among the East Coast’s architectural crown jewels, from historic house museums to lavish mansions.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s ballroom and George Russell’s bedroom, as well as the billiard room.

These so-called dollar princesses and their families lived in Manhattan and spent their summers in Newport, Rhode Island. Of course, these industry magnates didn’t consider their infrequent visits (some only came a few weeks in a year) as a reason to exercise restraint. The mammoth Breakers mansion is one of four so-called summer “cottages” (read: castles) that were completed just blocks from each other in a building boom between 1892 and 1902. The mansion was designed by Richard Morris Hunt in the Renaissance Revival style, as a less-than-subtle symbol of the Vanderbilt family’s social and financial preeminence in the Gilded Age. It was architectural one-upmanship that is considered the grandest of all the manses, featuring ornate ballrooms, extensive art collections, manicured gardens, and the latest (then) in modern technology, including electric lights. It has 70 rooms, an interior using marble imported from Italy and Africa, and a library mantel purchased from a château in France.

“When you walk into the dining room of The Breakers, with its painting of the goddess Aurora on the ceiling 50 feet above you or see 500,000 cubic feet of marble in a variety of different hues, you can’t help but be awestruck by the incredible wealth of these Gilded Age tycoons,” says Trudy Coxe, the chief executive officer and executive director of The Preservation Society of Newport County, which has under its stewardship six of the grand mansions featured in the series.

The preservation society worked closely with the filming crew to ensure the safety of the objects and spaces. “We had long discussions about the billiard table at The Breakers,” she explains. “It could not be removed, but it was important to the scene being filmed. Rather than moving it, we worked with their props department to put an alternative covering on the table to simulate an actual game of pool.”

In the series, the Breakers’ neo–Italian Renaissance great hall and music room are featured as Bertha Russell’s (played by Carrie Coon) ballroom, which we see in the final ball scene. George Russell (Morgan Spector) also discusses business while playing billiards in the mansion’s massive billiard room.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s bedroom in the Russell residence

Marble House was built from 1888 to 1892 as a summer cottage for Alva and William Kissam Vanderbilt. It was also designed by Richard Morris Hunt, but in the Beaux-Arts style. For an American house, it was unparalleled in opulence, with a temple-front portico resembling that of the White House, which had been completed 92 years earlier in 1800 (lofty, no?).

Mrs. Russell’s bedroom, built on a soundstage in Bethpage, New York, was inspired by real-life socialite Alva Vanderbilt’s bedroom in the Marble House. The French Louis XV bed was painted and reupholstered in salmon-pink velvet, and the custom bedding used 300 yards of fabric that have been draped, cinched, and swagged.

And that’s just the textiles. Most of the room’s furniture is refurbished antique and everything in the room has been painted to match. Sometimes, Shaw notes, it was difficult to paint a lovely wooden antique piece. “The scenic artists would look at us and say, ‘You really want me to paint over the chestnut? Are you sure?’” says Shaw. “Yes.”

Consuelo Vanderbilt’s (Alva and William’s child) monochrome red bedroom in the mansion also serves as George Russell’s bedroom in the show.

Features as: The exterior of Sylvia Chamberlain’s house

Rosecliff was built for Nevada silver heiress Theresa Fair Oelrichs, whose father, James Graham Fair, was one of the four partners in the Comstock Lode. The famed architectural firm McKim, Mead & White was commissioned to design the mansion, which was modeled after the Grand Trianon, the garden retreat of French kings at Versailles. The French Baroque Revival gem was designed for entertaining at a grand scale—and it certainly fulfilled its purpose. Fair Oelrichs would become known as one of the three great hostesses of Newport.

The white front facade of this mansion, which recently got a $7.4 million renovation, is a stand-in for Sylvia Chamberlain’s (played by Jeanne Tripplehorn) house. You’ll see it in Episode 7, when Tom Raikes (Thomas Cocquerel) pays a visit.

Features as: The servant kitchen and the real-life Sarah Berwind’s bedroom as parts of the Russell residence

The Elms is a Classical Revival dwelling that was completed in 1901. Architect Horace Trumbauer designed it for the coal baron Edward Julius Berwind, taking inspiration from the 18th-century d’Asnières French château. It also houses an impressive collection of paintings and tapestries, as well as the latest technology of its age.

In The Gilded Age Seasons 1 and 2, The Elms stands in as part of the Russells’ lavish residence, including scenes in the kitchen and Bertha Russell’s bedroom. But it truly steps into the spotlight in Season 3, when it hosts a dazzling ball. And as a long-awaited confession of love looms (we won’t spoil from whom), the opulent Newport mansion proves to be the perfect setting for high drama and high society.

Each guest is granted a cinematic entrance, illuminated by a golden glow that bathes the grand façade—a new addition this season, as electricity is now becoming available. “The house’s interiors would not have been electrified yet, but people were beginning to use it for parties,” Shaw says. “That would mean they would have to have their own generator installed somewhere on the property to light up those lights.” To accomplish this, the crew went to specialty shops that make reproduction bulbs. “The original bulbs were much bigger than what we have in a household today,” Shaw adds. “Some of the lightbulbs we used were 10 inches in diameter, which is how big they were then.”

Features as: The exterior is used for Caroline Astor’s Beechwood house; the boardinghouse is used for a scene with Oscar van Rhijn; and another room serves as Agnes van Rhijn’s bedroom

Completed in 1852, Château-sur-Mer is one of the first grand Bellevue Avenue mansions of the Gilded Age in Newport, built by architect Seth C. Bradford for William Shepard Wetmore. Its interiors were designed by Ogden Codman Jr., who co-authored Edith Wharton’s Decoration of Houses. The mansion was host to many major parties, including the Fête Champêtre, an elaborate country picnic for more than 2,000 guests in 1857, as well as a debutante ball for Edith Wetmore in 1889. Wetmore’s son, George Peabody Wetmore, went on to have a distinguished political career as the governor of Rhode Island and, later, as a U.S. senator.

In The Gilded Age, the home’s exterior was used for Caroline Astor’s (Donna Murphy) Beechwood House. The hallway, dining room, and ballroom were used during Mamie Fish’s (Ashlie Atkinson) doll tea party. Several bedrooms serve as Agnes van Rhijn’s (Christine Baranski) bedroom, Oscar van Rhijn’s (Blake Ritson) bedroom, Anne Morris’s (Katie Finneran) bedroom, and Patrick Morris’s (Michel Gill) office. There’s also a scene filmed at the boardinghouse with Oscar van Rhijn, according to Patch.

Features as: The office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes

Hunter House was constructed in 1748 at the harborfront near the north end of the Newport Historic District at a time when the city was a bustling seaport in the British empire. The house was owned by wealthy merchants and overlooked the wharves that serviced their trading ships. It’s one of the finest examples of Colonial architecture in Newport, according to the Preservation Society. Inside, its resident families furnished the house with decorative arts produced by expert craftsmen, much of which has been preserved today. Such a luxurious lifestyle, however, depended upon the labor of enslaved people of African descent; recent research by the Preservation Society has identified at least 13 enslaved individuals who lived and worked at Hunter House. Today’s Hunter House honors their perseverance and incalculable contributions to Newport’s past.

One of the sitting rooms in Hunter House functions as the office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes, and is set fictitiously in Doylestown, Pennsylvania.

Features as: The Astor family summer home

Belcourt was completed in 1894 for Oliver Hazard Perry Belmont, a prominent American socialite and coachman who served in the U.S. House of Representatives. Like many of the Newport mansions, the Château-esque structure was completed by architect Richard Morris Hunt; it was designed to mimic the hunting lodge of Versailles, built for Louis XIII of France, but with a special ask to accommodate Oliver’s collection of horses and automobiles. In 1896, Oliver gave the house to his second wife, the former Alva Vanderbilt, who designed the neighboring Newport mansion Marble House. Alva used Belcourt as a gathering place for the elite of society, hosting lavish parties and balls and attracting notable figures in politics, business, and the arts.

In The Gilded Age Season 1, Belcourt shows up in Episode 8 as Mrs. Astor’s lavish new Newport home, which Mrs. Russell sneaks inside and is forced to flee to avoid a less-than-pleasant meetup.

Features as: The social gathering scene where Caroline Astor and George Russell meet

Built in 1867 by prolific Boston tea and china merchant Robert M. Cushing, the Ledges was originally the summer home of the Cushing family. This majestic oceanfront pile is perched on one of Newport’s most desirable peninsulas, overlooking Bailey’s Beach. It was built by architect John H. Sturgis, who designed a large, Stick-style cottage that was equal parts comfort and elegance. Today it features original furnishings, papered walls, and unique artwork, including a studio that was added to the side of the house for Robert’s artistic children.

Today, the estate remains in the hands of the Cushing descendants, who occasionally open the house up to film crews. It was featured a few years ago in the film Evening, starring Glenn Close and Meryl Streep. “Working at the Ledges was pretty extraordinary,” Gilded Age location manager Lauri Pitkus told The Providence Journal, “because I don't think I’ve ever been in a home with spectacular views out of every window.”

This historic house was built in 1864 for John Noble Alsop Griswold, an Old China Trade merchant and diplomat. The house is the work of Richard Morris Hunt; it was one of the earliest buildings in the American Stick style of architecture. The house has a complex roofline with a main mansarded section that is pierced by numerous gable and dormer sections. It boasts a number of balconies sheltered by deep eaves, with gable ends decorated with woodwork. Today, the house is a gallery location for the Newport Art Museum. In the series, the house features in Season 1 Episode 1, and again in Episode 5.

Features as: Mingling with the other elite scene

The International Tennis Hall of Fame and its perfectly preserved grass courts are in the Newport Casino, a National Historic Landmark property. The historic seven-acre estate was commissioned by New York Herald publisher James Gordon Bennett as a social club for the elite summer residents of Newport. The Newport News boasted three days after it opened in July 1880: “It is doubtful if a livelier place can be found.” Today, the space is recognized as the official hall of fame for the sport of tennis globally. Since 1955, 262 distinguished inductees representing 27 countries have been enshrined in Newport.

In the eighth episode of The Gilded Age Season 1, the characters are seen promenading around the piazza that overlooks what is known today as Horseshoe Court. A few characters are even playing tennis. “It was fitting to have that history depicted here, given The Gilded Age’s look into that time period,” the hall of fame’s Megan Erbes tells ELLE DECOR. “Several of our staff members got to work closely with the production team and actors, teaching everything from the history of our property to how to hit a proper forehand.” RI Monthly reported that crews returned to the courts last summer to film Season 2 of the show, so it’s possible that Newport Casino will make another appearance.

Features as: The parlor, library, entryway, and staircase of the Morris house, located on Fifth Avenue in the show; Mr. and Mrs. Morris’s bedrooms were filmed in the Newport mansions

William Smith Slater hired General William Walker to build a home as a wedding gift for his daughter. The result was this historic home, which was completed in 1877. The Victorian house is filled with treasures—the fireplace surrounds, for example, were carved by Charles Dowler, who came to the United States to produce arms for the Civil War. Today, the house is open to visitors and is known as the Clouds Hill Museum.

The Clouds Hill Museum is the only Rhode Island site outside of Newport that was used in the series. The crew scheduled filming for Season 1 in late February of 2021, when a few hundred people were on site during two days of filming, museum director Wayne Cabral told Warwick Online, noting that the taping finished around midnight on the second day of filming (after five days of work to prep the set). Almost all the parlor’s furniture was removed, and the head set designer brought in items sourced from local antiques stores in East Greenwich and North Kingstown, museum president Anne Holst added. The producers were adamant about removing the existing curtains, which were disintegrating, Holst added, so she negotiated to leave the replacements (which cost $10,000) with the museum after filming.

Features as: Visit to the home of Aurora Fane scene

The Gothic Revival–style Lyndhurst Mansion was designed in 1838 by Alexander Jackson Davis. Lyndhurst’s third owner, Jay Gould, is the real-life inspiration for the “new money” character George Russell. “He was fantastically ruthless in business, to an extent that Mrs. Astor would never let him in, he was never allowed into society, and she didn’t let his children in until he was dead,” creator, writer, and producer Julian Fellowes said in a podcast. “But he was an incredibly affectionate father and a very caring and faithful husband…and his children all had these nostalgic memories of Lyndhurst, their house on the Hudson.”

In Season 1 of The Gilded Age, Lyndhurst’s interiors are the home of characters Charles and Aurora Fane (Ward Horton and Kelli O’Hara), who are related to the van Rhijns and part of the “old money” side of society. The Lyndhurst lawns and grounds also doubled as Central Park and the Sheep’s Meadow in the opening sequence. The home boasts a bowling alley that dates back to 1894, which was re-created to be the ferry terminal that Marian Brook (Louisa Jacobson) and Peggy Scott (Denée Benton) take to Manhattan for the first time in Episode 1. And finally, the house’s carriage house was transformed into The New York Globe newspaper offices.

The Belvedere Estate, located between Lyndhurst Mansion and writer Washington Irving’s Sunnyside home, is a century-old mansion on 25 acres in historic Tarrytown. The estate was originally part of Philipsburg Manor, a 90,000-acre property built for Florence and Casper Whitney. “While the mansion dates from the 1920s, it has a much older European feel that could represent something from decades earlier,” says Belvedere Estate sales director Linda Fiorentino, who recalls the massive legwork required to film at the mansion.

“I will never forget the background actors descending from a yellow school bus, fully dressed in period costumes and felt hats,” she says. The Season 1 scene was filmed at the pond at the back of Belvedere Estate, acting as a stand-in for Central Park in the scene when Marian meets Mr. Raikes at Bethesda Fountain in Episode 1. The pond is a man-made feature, and the water level sometimes sinks to a mere foot or two, Fiorentino explains. “Before filming, they filled the pond with roughly 600,000 gallons of water to fill to capacity, giving the illusion of a much larger body of water and to allow them to have pleasure boats filled with passengers on the water,” she says. They also re-created Central Park’s Shakespeare Bridge from rough-hewn logs and brought in trees, shrubs, and foliage to cover the existing link fence because it was too modern.

Features as: The drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain

Toward the end of the 19th century, Colonel Elliott Fitch Shepard, a lawyer who founded the New York State Bar Association in 1876, purchased more than 500 acres on a hill high above the Hudson River north of Tarrytown. He engaged the renowned architecture firm McKim, Mead & White to design his family’s manor house here. The result was a 75-room Victorian structure that mimicked 18th-century Italian villa architecture. Think: hand-carved mahogany paneling, a grand stairway near the front entrance, high patterned ceilings, and elaborate cornice work. Unfortunately, Shepard died before it was completed in 1893, and it passed into the hands of William Rockefeller and Frank Vanderlip. These men were part of a group (including John Jacob Astor, who died on the Titanic in 1912) who went on to convert the building to the Sleepy Hollow Country Club, which remains a private members-only club today.

Scenes in the drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain were shot on location here. “The entire room was redressed by our team in the Rococo style,” set decorator Regina Graves told 1st Dibs.

Features as: Part of Mrs. Astor’s residence

Glenview was built in 1876 by John Bond Trevor, a highly successful stockbroker, who engaged New York City architect Charles W. Clinton to build his estate. The mansion was a paragon of luxury and modern comfort, featuring indoor plumbing, gas illumination, and a coal-burning furnace to circulate heat throughout its 37 rooms. The exterior had elements of Victorian Gothic and French Second Empire design, complete with a mansard roof, a gray stone facade constructed of blocks hewn from a Hastings-on-Hudson quarry, and decorative sandstone elements brought from Indiana.

The mansion makes an appearance as Caroline Astor’s residence in Season 1. “They were all so complimentary about Glenview, and of course we did a lot of polishing and waxing,” Laura Vookles, chair of the museum’s curatorial department, told The Rivertown Enterprise. “They focused on our exquisite interiors and filmed in six rooms, including the dining, library, and sitting rooms, and used all but one painting that is normally on view.”

The John Paine Mansion, also known as the Castle, was built in 1896 and was deemed the grandest house in Troy, New York. It has since served as a private residence, a college administration building, a movie set (see: Martin Scorsese’s 1993 drama The Age of Innocence), and, currently, a fraternity house. In the show, one of the bedrooms in the Castle features as Tom Raikes’s office.

This residence, the home of Troy locals Bill and Sue Comiskey, stands in for the Brooklyn residence of the well-off Scott family. At one point early in the series, Arthur Scott (John Douglas Thompson) and his daughter, Peggy (Denée Benton), sit on carved-oak chairs around a damask-covered dining table. The home’s elaborate details really shine in this historic home, which dates back to the turn of the 19th century. Stained-glass windows and an elegant china cabinet are memorable architectural features that are original to the location and transport the viewers back in time.

While filming what would be the Scott family residence, Bill, the homeowner, told News10 that a few prized family possessions actually made the cut. “My desk was in it, front and center…you [also] saw the whole front and back parlor into the dining room where they were having a meal,” he explained. “[There were] great shots of this room catching the stained glass, the fireplace, [and] this bookcase my grandfather made.”

Features as: The interior is used as George Russell’s workspace

Hempstead House was built for Howard Gould by architects Hunt & Hunt in 1912 on the north shore of Long Island. The mansion was eventually sold to art mogul Daniel Guggenheim (yes, that Guggenheim). Hempstead House is one of four mansions on the grounds of the Guggenheim Estate—which includes Castle Gould, Falaise, and Mille Fleur. The Tudor-style castle was the embodiment of Gold Coast opulence, a place where the esteemed family hosted grand parties, performances, and exhibitions by gatherings of the elite of the time. To say it is massive would be an understatement: 50,000 square feet, with 40 rooms and a 60-foot-tall entry foyer. In The Gilded Age, the mansion’s interior was used as George Russell’s workspace.

Features as: The late General Henry Brook’s house

Rock Hall Museum was initially an 18th-century plantation. The two-and-a-half-story Georgian-style home was built in approximately 1767 and later restored to its original appearance in the 1950s. In Season 1 Episode 1, the house’s white facade is featured within the first 15 minutes as a backdrop for the late General Henry Brook’s house. Marian, his daughter, is seen walking out of the home on her way to the train station.

The Hart Cluett Museum is a work of Federal architecture designed by Martin E. Thompson in 1827. He was hired by William Howard. Thompson brought the design of the Hart Cluett House back to New York City, where many examples of the 1827 Federal-style townhouse were copied throughout Manhattan. This house, a museum open to the public today, was featured in The Gilded Age in Episode 2 as Anne Morris’s home.

Rachel Silva is a freelance writer and editor covering design, architecture, and culture. She previously served as an editor at ELLE Decor, and her work has appeared in Harper’s Bazaar, TIME, and Veranda, among others. A graduate of Columbia Journalism School, she is based in New York City.

#6883. Jesse Jo Stark on What It Means to Be a Muse

The singer’s new Gothic country album, Muse, releases on November 6

Jesse Jo Stark wrote her latest album, Muse, in the wake of heartache. She left Los Angeles to find reprieve in the Colorado Rocky Mountains, where she found sanctuary among friends, a bottle of rosé, and a pink-halter-top-and-sweatpants combo. The melancholic album flowed out of her for three weeks, during which she transferred her emotions onto the page. “I am no longer holding on to a version of myself,” she tells Bazaar on a call from Los Angeles, on her way to a photo shoot. “There’s a freedom in moving forward.”

Stark keeps pressing on, no matter what. Outside of songwriting, she plays a key role in Chrome Hearts as the daughter of founder Richard Stark. She spent her formative years surrounded by musicians, artists, designers, and every kind of creative person imaginable, all of whom inspired her to pursue her own vision. That world endlessly inspires her to create, even when she feels low. “I’ve worn things that are absolutely horrible, and I’ve written songs that I don’t necessarily love,” she says, “but when you grow, you go back to a less constructed version of yourself.”

This more authentic version of herself fuels Muse. She shed some of the macabre edge of her debut album, 2022’s Doomed, in favor of a softer reflection on her life, love, femininity, and the natural world that inspired these songs. But above all, Stark offers a reflection on her life being put on a pedestal, a narrative that deeply considers the women in her life, from friends to her mother, Laurie Lynn Stark. This 13-track Gothic country album appropriately conveys Stark’s energy, with visuals described as “somewhat of a horror film, but I’m wearing a lace skirt.” She wrote on her Instagram, announcing the album, that the theme is “fragile but fierce femininity,” best to “enjoy with a glass of red.”

A few weeks before the release, Bazaar spoke to Stark about the women who inspired her, Napoleon’s poems to Josephine, writing songs in Colorado, and what it means to be a muse.

I was looking at a lot of mythology and history and the muses of rock ’n’ roll—women of rock ’n’ roll. If you break down the word itself, you get “used,” “amused,” “music.” I think women are admired and desired but not always heard. For me, women have this pivotal point in their life—hopefully, at one point—where they regain their strength and voice, and this album was that for me. The muse, throughout history, could also have a negative connotation and feel lonely. But I felt like putting emphasis on the reciprocity of it—then owning that and allowing it to feel beautiful. It’s a powerful word. It felt fitting for this time in my life.

There’s so much serious crossover. Being able to develop the way I feel my best on stage and design, what I wear enables me to embody that version of myself, to gather the strength to perform. Women like Daphne Guinness, Isabella Blow, and Jane Birkin have really inspired me. I’ve always wanted to not just completely mirror something from the past but re-create it as my own. I wear a loincloth on stage; it’s not that it hasn’t been done before, but the way I wear it or the way it’s designed, the accessories and tops that are paired with it... I do feel confident in me extending this already living thing but making it my own.

When I was little, I always had an attitude about the two not going hand in hand. I think that was incorrect. They’re pretty much the same expression: One’s a vocal cord and playing guitar, and the other’s a boot that you walk in.

It’s weird. My last album was highly visual. There was a lot of fantasy involved, and this is very stripped back, [both] vocal-production-wise and stylistically. I just did my first acoustic show, and I was wearing a vintage silk dress with a heel, and my hair was straight, and I had minimal makeup. Because there was such a rawness to it, especially where I recorded, which was amongst nature and very secluded. With this record, anytime I tried to put emphasis on creating a specific vibe, I rejected that.

The space and the quietness and the snow—I’ve never been one to be completely influenced by nature. I don’t love green or grass, but I love to sit in it and lie in it. I love being with animals and the snow and the sun. All these natural tones, I’m embracing the color brown like I haven’t before. It sounds so silly, but they represent a softness that I didn’t have for a while. The visuals have become somewhat of a horror film, but I’m wearing a lace skirt.

My main single from the record, “Age of Being Honest,” has been visually stimulating and powerful. I can feel every emotion through that visual. It’s rawness—in the vocals and everything. “Colorado” is the place that I literally went to and didn’t really know what was going to come from it. That set the tone for the rest of the record.

The shedding was happening as I was driving. The heartache was there, so it wasn’t difficult once I sat down to break through that human brick wall. I wanted to feel everything right away. I gotta be real, it was a white boa, a bottle of rosé, people I fuck with, the sun, and being in a really beautiful pink halter top and sweatpants half the time. I don’t think there was much going on except pain and not knowing where it was going and just sitting on the floor, living in a house with people for two weeks or a month. You can’t escape what you’re doing; I really leaned into that.

My dad had a lot to do with my love of music. I asked to play guitar at a young age. I was around a lot of rock ’n’ roll and glam—these really amazing artists who were way older than me. This desire to—and I was really shy—create something as beautiful as they had or stand as my parents could and make my own way. Music was always mine, so there was an independence to it. Finding yourself in writing, you don’t think you can write, and then you start writing poems. I loved Britney Spears. I loved the Spice Girls. But then my dad would play Merle Haggard, and he also let me listen to Christina Aguilera. I found punk rock when I was 13 because I fell in love with a punk boy. Then somehow you’re mad at everything, and you found your voice in that way. It just started to become this whole thing, and then I started singing covers because I wasn’t ready to write. And then when you write, you really write your first song. To create something out of nothing is really funny because the fact that we think we get to do that is funny to me, but also so freeing and amazing. You never want to stop.

#6880. A Look Inside WELL/BEINGS’s ‘Bound by Nature’ Gala

On November 6, cultural leaders, philanthropists, and conservation advocates braved the rain in New York City for a worthy cause. That evening, WELL/BEINGS hosted its annual benefit dinner at Hearst Tower, bringing guests together to support conservation efforts in the most vital and vulnerable ecosystems across the Caribbean and the Bolivian Amazon.

The event, titled “Bound by Nature” this year, was hosted by actor, entrepreneur, and philanthropist Oliver Trevena, and presented by the nonprofit’s co-founders Amanda Hearst Rønning and Breanna Schultz. Throughout the night, attendees enjoyed live music, conservation storytelling, an exclusive auction of fine art, jewelry, and luxury experiences, as well as a specially curated culinary experience by celebrity chef Eduardo Garcia.

As nods to the marine and terrestrial environments, the sea of guests wore shades of blue and green, respectively.

The benefit sought to raise funds toward more than $800,000 in programmatic commitments WELL/BEINGS has made to 15 conservation partners for 2026 and 2027. The selected organizations, which include local and underserved groups, are working to protect wildlife, restore critical habitats, and strengthen communities in targeted areas.

During her opening remarks, Hearst Rønning underscored the importance of investing in the people closest to the ecosystems in need of protection: “That is why we support and amplify local leaders,” she said. “They know what needs to be done. Too often, what stands between them and doing it is support and funding.”

Schultz echoed that sentiment, reminding attendees of the lasting impact of their contributions: “Our partners fight every day to protect what we love. Tonight, we can give them the resources to keep going…what we save now is what the next generation will inherit.”

Since its founding, WELL/BEINGS has invested more than $3 million in conservation. The organization also operates under a 100 percent donation model, with its co-founders and Board of Directors separately covering core operating expenses so that public donations go directly toward the mission at hand.

The evening also marked the launch of the Caribbean Bioregional Hub, a new initiative connecting nine partner organizations across the region to strengthen collaboration and advance coordinated conservation efforts. The sky is the limit!

Ecleen Luzmila Caraballo is the weekend editor at ELLE who covers celebrity and culture news. She was previously a senior editor at Complex and has contributed to other publications including GQ and Nylon. Born and raised in New York City to Panamanian parents, she’ll take book recs in English, Spanish (and soon, German). Her interests include music, fashion, beauty, film, earth science, and the study of love. On her time off, you can find her with loved ones at an odd-hour movie showtime, baking, or sitting in silence near a high-fidelity sound system.

#6875. 7 Desert Boots That Can Survive the Winter

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Despite the name, these chukkas are built to be worn in all conditions.

Winter weather can get ugly. If it’s not wind and rain, it’s slushy sidewalks and unexpected snow flurries. You obviously need shoes that can handle a little precipitation, but the key phrase here is a little. You don’t want to stomp around in heavy winter boots, especially if we want to look nice. Dress shoes are a tough fit in winter, as are dress boots. Funny enough, between December and March, you know what our go-to footwear becomes? Desert boots. Technically chukka boots, but a few desert boots make the cut here.

But that’s something to note. The Esquire office does not use the term interchangeably—but the Internet does, so we’re going along with it. We know some of you—nerds, mostly—have stopped reading this article because you don’t think we know the difference between the two. We’re well aware of the square and rectangle-ism: All desert boots are chukkas, not all chukkas are desert boots.

If you’re not a nerd and want to learn something new, chukkas are ankle-high boots with open lacing and two or three pairs of eyelets, often made with leather uppers—leather or rubber soled. Desert boots are a subsection of this silhouette that typically features a suede upper (nubuck, sometimes) with lighter-colored, more flexible crepe soles. They are not as winterized—suede, especially, is known to take a beating from moisture—hence the name. The details vary here and there, but both styles can work for winter if you choose wisely.

We found seven pairs of chukka boots—yes, some of which are desert boots—with weather-friendly features, from water-resistant uppers to sturdy rubber soles. Most have both, so you can find a pair that looks sharp without leaving you entirely at the mercy of the winter weather forecast.

These chukka boots might as well be dress boots. They look sharp no matter what outfit you pair them with, and the ultra-refined, premium calfskin leather upper is naturally water-resistant; to a point, that is—we wouldn’t wear them in the pouring rain.

They’re very comfortable thanks to a soft, leather lining on the inside and open-cell foam insoles that provide supportive cushioning. And the flexible outsoles made from XL Extralight injection molding are a nice touch that give the boot some lightweight flexibility.

For a classic pair of chukka boots that come at a good price and have a wide range of colors and sizes, Thursday Boot Co. is the way to go. These chukkas come in a variety of colors and materials, but our favorite has to be the Mocha colorway in weather-safe suede. It’s rare to find a pair of suede shoes you can actually get wet without ruining, but these ones can brave the weather while still looking great. And with studded rubber outsoles, you have some extra traction and durability. If you want even more traction and durability in the soles, try one of the Rugged & Resilient styles with anti-slip wedge rubber outsoles.

Here’s another suede option you can actually wear during the winter; Sabah treats the suede upper of this boot to be water resistant, but you can still add a suede treatment for extra protection if you want to. These boots are handmade in Monte San Giusto, Italy, and you can trust that they were made with lots of skill and care. The insole and midsole are made from vegetable tanned leather, so they end up forming to your feet once you wear them, making them extra comfortable.

If you like the look of a gum-sole sneaker, you might like how it looks on a boot, too. With these desert boots, SeaVees blends the classic gum-sole look with the more rugged desert boot style, and the result is a versatile boot that can withstand pretty much any weather. With water-resistant rugged oiled leather uppers and natural rubber soles, these guys can get you through quite a lot.

These are going to be the least weather-ready on this list. You don’t want to take them out in a snowstorm, but damn do they look good. Imagine these worn to a holiday party, worn with a nice pair of chinos and fair-isle sweater.

The suede upper is a true not-water-resistant suede. But they do have durable rubber soles. The stitched moc-toe is a fun detail that makes these chukka boots stand out from the rest of the pack. Drake’s notes that these boots run small, so they recommend sizing up by a half-size when ordering online.

We’re starting to get pretty far away from the chukka heart, but you can see the chukka-shaped vamp and the ankle-y shape. Plus, when we think of rugged workwear boots, we think of Timberland. The upper is made from waterproof leather and has a TimberDry waterproof membrane for extra protection, and you can see that Gripstick rubber outsole gives you some distance from the ground and provides great traction as well.

And finally, we can’t talk about chukka boots without mentioning Clarks. The original Clarks desert boot was designed in 1949 and inspired by the rough suede and crepe-soled boots worn by off-duty British soldiers during WWII (as well as the shoes worn by polo players in India and South African veldskoen shoes). Today, the brand’s original style is still going strong, and you can get a pair for under $100.

#6873. This Brand-New Illinois Farmhouse Was Designed to Look Hundreds of Years Old

The team at Buccellato Design and Architecture focused on incorporating heritage details and timeworn furnishings.

For Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, the best compliment they could receive about a project isn’t, “Wow, it looks so different in here.” It’s that a home looks like they haven’t changed a thing.

On a country road in Momence, Illinois, a beloved farmhouse stands as a timeworn landmark for the surrounding agricultural community. For the homeowners—a retired couple returning to their Midwestern roots—the property was as much a repository of memories as it was a place to create new ones. “Having grown up nearby, the property evoked a strong sense of memory and place,” says Aimee, who founded Buccellato Design and Architecture alongside her husband, Kevin. “At the outset of the project, and throughout, our clients wanted the new house to feel like it had always been there, so much so that perhaps the neighbors could be led to believe that the original house remained intact, yet amended by skillful renovation and discreet additions.”

It was an architectural sleight of hand rooted in a distinctly Midwestern philosophy: “Be good to the neighbors.” Alongside the Buccellatos, the couple approached the 6,000-square-foot rebuild with the restraint of preservationists and the imagination of storytellers. “Great care was taken to instill the home and outbuildings with a sense of history and rootedness, creating a home that feels as though it’s been part of this bucolic setting for centuries,” says Kevin of the project, which also included the restoration of the property’s original 19th-century barn and corn crib. “We drew inspiration from traditional farm complexes, where homesteads were added slowly over time, reconnecting the site with its Folk-Victorian-Farm-House and agricultural building roots.”

Inside, the narrative continues through antique white oak floors, salvaged beams, heritage florals, and rooms that borrow their palettes from the landscape. In the kitchen, a wall of windows replaces upper cabinetry, framing sunrise over the barn and walnut trees. The glass porch offers sweeping views of the Kankakee River, while a tucked-away speakeasy recalls the original farmhouse’s stone foundation.

For Kevin and Aimee, the guiding principle was a new construction home that felt “sensitive, not wasteful, polite, not ostentatious,” an exercise in knowing when architecture and interiors should defer to their surroundings. The result is a house with an invented past and a very real sense of belonging that does the Midwest—and the neighbors—proud.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Buccellato Design and ArchitectureStylist: Cate RaganLocation: Momence, IllinoisThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-and-a-half-bathroom home across 6,000 square feet

The entryway offers a fitting introduction to the home'’s carefully constructed sense of history, replete with nickel gap paneling, fumed and waxed antique white oak floors, and a light-filled staircase. Even the furnishings—including a vintage Persian rug and antique Jacobean gateleg table—suggest a collection acquired over generations.

Benjamin Moore’s Nelson Blue sets a serene backdrop for the sitting room, where traditional silhouettes and understated patterns make an easy pairing. “This room was designed to honor the history of this property and embody the love that our clients hold for this special place,” adds Aimee.

A tufted mohair sofa, realized in a rich umber, lends a sense of history, while an iconic feather lamp by Bunny Williams offers a reminder that even the most classic rooms benefit from a little irreverence.

In the kitchen, the Buccellatos traded upper cabinets for an expansive wall of windows overlooking the farmland, relocating the practical necessities to an adjacent dish room and larder. Salvaged white oak beams and barnboards overhead reinforce the home’s agricultural roots, while furniture-like cabinetry and leathered black quartzite countertops strike a timeless note.

With natural light pouring in from three sides, the dining room is what the Buccellatos describe as “peaceful, soft, and ethereal,” equally suited to both morning coffee and candlelit dinners. A dreamy mural wallpaper by Susan Harter references the property’s location along the Kankakee River, while formal seating lends a sense of occasion without sacrificing the home’s easygoing country charm.

Nestled among mature trees, the primary bedroom feels almost suspended in the surrounding landscape. The Buccellatos leaned into that sense of serenity with the palette. “It’s a dreamlike mix of earthy neutrals and pale blues that paints a serene backdrop for elegant details, vintage furnishings, and a delicate mix of subtle pattern,” adds Kevin.

A French country canopy bed, dressed in tailored Schumacher linen, takes center stage, while vintage-inspired furnishings and subtle pattern keep the room from feeling overly precious. An adjacent sitting room offers a place to read the morning paper or unwind with a cup of tea in the evenings.

In the vestibule, a little pattern goes a long way. A climbing floral from Schumacher in inky charcoal makes a charming counterpoint to the bedroom’s hushed palette, while a checked window bench is the ideal spot to read, giving the passageway purpose.

Pale blue walls and creamy millwork lend the primary bathroom an old-fashioned prettiness. A custom vanity introduces a tailored architectural element, while a Cowtan & Tout floral Roman shade softens the room’s more utilitarian details.

Designed as a playful respite for guests and future grandchildren, this bedroom plays off pattern and primary hues. “The red painted woodwork feels unexpected and cheerful against the blue-and-white wallpaper, striped rug, and patterned bedding,” says Aimee.

“The brick-clad main block echoes the footprint of the original farmhouse on this site, yet the story we wrote for the house begins with the folk Victorian wing, inspired by L-shaped farmhouses of that period,” says Kevin. “In our modern narrative, the clapboard-sided wing came first, followed by the brick-clad ’addition,’ which takes inspiration from the later Colonial Revival period in its scale and sophistication of detailing.”

Founded by Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, Buccellato Design & Architecture is a practice marked by twenty-plus years of close collaboration, in architectural practice and in life. With projects on both coasts and throughout the country, the firm’s award-winning work reveals a dedication to craft, artistry, and detail. Achieved in partnership with clients, their projects reflect a purposeful sense of rootedness in the landscape and abiding devotion to beauty and the comfort of home.

#6866. Why Kylie Jenner and Timothée Chalamet Didn’t Pose Together on the LACMA Art+Film Gala Red Carpet

Kylie Jenner and Timothée Chalamet both attended the fashionable annual LACMA Art+Film Gala on Saturday night, but they didn’t make a joint red carpet appearance.

Jenner arrived solo, posing for photographers in a fun Ashi Studio gown, and Chalamet skipped the red carpet altogether. He was snapped inside the event on his own.

The couple posing separately is unsurprising given their history. Chalamet and Jenner rarely do red carpets together. Their last big event was the Vanity Fair Oscar Party in March. (Chalamet arrived on his own at the Oscars earlier that night, and Jenner joined him inside the show.)

Chalamet offered a glimpse of his evening at the LACMA Art+Film Gala on his Instagram Story, though he kept it work-focused. He posted a photo of filmmaker and director Denis Villeneuve speaking onstage, with a still from Dune (in which Chalamet plays Paul Atreides) projected behind him. Dune: Part Three is set to release in theaters on December 18.

The actor was also photographed inside the gala seated alongside Villeneuve, one of the evening’s honorees; Villeneuve’s wife and producer Tanya Lapointe; and actress Nicole Kidman. The 15th annual event recognized Villeneuve alongside visual artist Vija Celmins. Chalamet was likely there to support the Dune director.

While it’s been months since Chalamet and Jenner made a joint red carpet appearance, one of their past outings inspired Lisa Rinna’s Halloween costume this year. Chalamet and Jenner wore matching custom Chrome Hearts ensembles at the Los Angeles premiere of Marty Supreme in December, and Rinna recreated Jenner’s look.

#6865. These 9 Brands Are Making the Best Dark-Wash Jeans on the Market

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Moody, rich, and sophisticated—everything you want to project with your dressing.

In the world of denim, think of dark wash jeans like a good Scotch. It exists within a broader umbrella, but it’s a little more weathered and more mature. In the grand scheme of jeans, you really can’t go wrong when it comes to different washes. For clarity, we use dark wash to describe lightly washed dark jeans, raw sanforized jeans, and the real nerdy loomstate shrink-to-fit stuff. Why? Because they are all, visually, dark wash. For us, it’s exactly how you should buy your jeans.

Jeans inherently skew casual. But the subdued color of dark wash allows it to pass for something a little more refined. Where other denim gets processed and faded after being dyed, dark wash hasn’t—letting it keep that subdued inky hue that veers slightly toward “polished trouser” territory. They’re more rugged than a pair of slacks, but just formal and sophisticated enough that you can probably wear them around the office as a sort of nonchalantly edgy work pant.

You won’t need to hunt far or hard for quality and stylish dark washes. Most, if not all, of the Esquire-editor-approved jeans and denim brands also offer darker colors—making it as easy as buying your favorite sweater in another color. Find your favorite below.

Rag & Bone knows what it’s good at and sticks to it. (It’s denim...they’re good at denim.) Our reviewers especially like how the brand approaches its sizing and fits. It’s a super simple and straightforward number system that starts at 1 and goes up to 4. Fit 1 offers a slimmer silhouette, while Fit 4 gives that classic straight look.

For their dark wash—which Rag & Bone calls “Solomon”—the brand uses its very own rb Selvedge, which it developed in partnership with one of Japan's premier denim mills. This will help the jeans naturally develop a nice patina over time to really show just how much you love your denim.

If you ask Esquire shopping editor Luke Guillory what jeans to buy, he’s going to tell you these. He’s already waxed poetic about Wrangler’s original Cowboy Cut in our denim buying guide, so we’ll just cut to the chase: they’re super flattering and at an ultra-competitive price (though he advises bigger guys should opt for the more relaxed cut).

If you’re looking for something under $100, Gap’s more than likely where it’s at. The retailer has spent a ton of time and effort in reupping its basics—jeans especially. The brand’s classic straight silhouette is, at this point in time, better than the offering from the big-name brand that starts with “L” and rhymes with WiFi.

People hunt for vintage Levi’s for a reason. These 505s are a reproduction of the brand’s iconic style for 1967. As Luke describes in our denim buying guide, the fit will “hug up top, drape down low.” “They're universally flattering and classic cut, so you don't have to worry about them going out of style,” he says. “As long as they're holding on, you'll look good.”

We’ve already rounded up a few Todd Snyder pieces we’d recommend buying, and these dark indigo worn wash jeans are one more thing to add to the list. Available in three different fits—slim, straight, and relaxed—it’s made from an all-cotton denim that balances comfort and durability.

This denim from Ralph Lauren’s Americana sublabel is an absolute classic. It’s got a distinctly Western feel with its deep 13.75 oz selvedge denim, while still offering that sort of rugged sophistication you want from a dark wash jean.

Little known fact, but Acne Studios actually started out with jeans. Over 20 years later, it’s still their bread and butter—with funky pieces that embody a “rock and roll edge,” as Luke describes them. This groovy wide-legged pair especially gives off a sort of “Lenny-Kravitz-meets-Steven-Tyler” air of vintage swagger.

We’re long-time fans of Buck Mason. As Esquire contributor Matthew Medendrop describes, the brand has been “quietly doing some of the most exciting things in contemporary menswear” for the last few years—and their denim is no exception. The brand’s “full saddle” jeans take inspiration from the classic vintage blue jeans, think Levi’s 501s circa like the ‘60s. Pair that with the deep dark wash, and you got one sexy pair of jeans.

3Sixteen is one of the premier brands for Esquire’s denim nerds. It gets into the nitty-gritty stuff we care about, but since it’s based here in North America—and not in Japan—it’s a lot easier to get your hands on. To break down the naming, RS means relaxed straight, what we think is the easiest to wear from the brand. The 100xk tag is the type of denim—custom woven for the brand by Kabuki Mills. It’s rough at first, be warned, but it softens and fades like no other.

#6859. All of Princess Kate’s Remembrance Sunday Looks Over the Years

The Princess of Wales first attended the event in 2011

Princess Catherine has joined the royal family for the National Service of Remembrance every year since 2011, the same year that she got married to Prince William. The somber annual ceremony, held on Remembrance Sunday at the Cenotaph, a war memorial in London, honors service members who have lost their lives at war. It takes place on the second Sunday of November, and it involves a church service, a wreath-laying ceremony, and a formal procession, all of which are attended by various members of the British royal family.

When it comes to her royal style, the Princess of Wales has long embraced a more sustainable approach, frequently rewearing dresses, jewelry, and accessories. Over the years, that style philosophy has been on full display at Remembrance Sunday, where Kate has made a tradition of recycling looks and adding sentimental touches through meaningful brooches and heirloom jewelry.

One of her signature accessories is the red poppy pin, a symbol that has come to be widely associated with the holiday. Kate is often seen wearing three poppies at once, and while the royal family has never confirmed the significance behind the number, one theory suggests it may be a personal nod to her great-grandmother’s three brothers, all of whom lost their lives during World War I.

Throughout the years, Kate has also worn pieces that once belonged to late royal family members like Queen Elizabeth II and Princess Diana, which she has styled with outfits from Catherine Walker, Alexander McQueen, Temperley London, Diane von Furstenberg, and more.

Ahead, take a look back at Princess Kate’s Remembrance Sunday style through the years, from her first appearance in 2011 to now. And be sure to check back for more updates once the Princess of Wales steps out for the 2026 ceremony, too.

For her first appearance with the royal family at the Cenotaph memorial, Kate wore a black Diane von Furstenberg coat adorned with a double poppy pin along with a Jane Corbett hat and Kiki McDonough earrings.

At the 2012 Remembrance Sunday ceremony, the then-Duchess of Cambridge rewore her Diane von Furstenberg coat from the previous year, this time paired with a Philip Treacy hat, Annoushka earrings, and a single sparkling poppy brooch.

Opting for a different outerwear look for her third appearance, Kate wore a single-breasted Temperley London coat, which she accessorized with a Sylvia Fletcher for Lock & Co. hat and Kiki McDonough hoop earrings with Annoushka pearl drops.

In 2014, Kate wore a black Alexander McQueen jacket over a black ruffled BCBG dress. She rewore the Jane Corbett hat she donned in 2011, and she added a floral brooch, a Mappin & Webb diamond necklace, and Princess Diana’s sapphire earrings.

Kate stepped out in another Alexander McQueen coat for the 2015 Remembrance Day service, which featured velvet trim along the collar, sleeves, and waist. The then-duchess topped it off with a Lock & Co. hat adorned with a sculptural butterfly and black veil, the Kiki McDonough hoop earrings and Annoushka pearl drops she’d worn in 2013, and a triple-poppy pin on her lapel.

In another rewear, Kate donned the same Diane von Furstenberg coat she wore to her first and second Remembrance Sunday services in 2011 and 2012. This time, she styled it with a John Boyd hat, diamond-and-pearl earrings, and three poppies on her lapel.

Kate switched up her look for the 2017 National Service of Remembrance, opting for a double-breasted, collarless Dolce & Gabbana coat with oversized gold buttons, statement Oscar de la Renta earrings in gold, and a Philip Treacy hat she wore in 2006, during her early days of dating Prince William. She also debuted a new hair look, pinning it up to create the illusion of a short bob.

The 2018 Remembrance Day Service marked a significant milestone: the 100th anniversary of the end of World War I. For the occasion, Kate wore a custom Alexander McQueen jacket with military-inspired details, including a high white collar, red shoulder embellishments, and crest-style buttons. She completed the look with a Lock & Co. hat, a triple red poppy pin, and a brooch honoring the women who served during World War I.

Opting for another military-inspired jacket, Kate wore a Catherine Walker coat and a Philip Treacy hat featuring a black veil. Forgoing her usual poppy pin, she instead wore a special pin known as the “Codebreaker’s Brooch” in honor of her grandmother who worked at Bletchley Park during World War II.

At the socially distanced 2020 Remembrance Day ceremony, held amid the COVID-19 pandemic, Kate once again opted for a military-inspired look. She wore a Catherine Walker jacket with fringe detailing at the shoulders, complete with a Philip Treacy fascinator, pearl drop earrings, and her signature triple-poppy pin.

Kate brought back her Alexander McQueen coat from the 2018 service for the 2021 Remembrance Day ceremony. She paired the repeat look with Princess Diana’s pearl earrings, a wide-brimmed Lock & Co. hat, and her signature triple-poppy pin, along with a crystal flower brooch from the Royal British Legion.

For her first Remembrance Day Service following Queen Elizabeth II’s passing, the Princess of Wales wore a black Catherine Walker coat, Princess Diana’s pearl-and-diamond earrings, and a wide-brimmed Philip Treacy hat. She completed the look with her signature trio of poppies, paired with an Art Deco-inspired diamond brooch.

The Princess of Wales opted for multiple rewears at the 2023 Remembrance Sunday service. She repeated the custom Catherine Walker coat she first wore in 2019 and the Philip Treacy hat she previously wore at the 2012 and 2017 services. Then, for some added sparkle, she wore Queen Elizabeth’s diamond-and-pearl earrings, and her signature three-poppy pin.

Kate didn’t make many public appearances in 2024 due to her cancer diagnosis, but she still attended the Remembrance Sunday service that year. She wore a Catherine Walker coat dress, featuring a velvet bow collar, and she accessorized with a Juliette Botterill hat, Queen Elizabeth’s earrings, and her triple-poppy pin.

Kate’s look at the 2025 Remembrance Sunday service featured a rewear with a new twist. She opted for the same Catherine Walker coat from 2022, but this time, she styled it with a lace navy-blue pie-crust collar blouse draping out of the top. She accessorized with a sculptural Lock & Co. fascinator, Princess Diana’s pearl-drop earrings, a single poppy brooch, and a Royal Air Force brooch.

#6850. Costco's New Porch Goose Feels So '80s Coded, and Fans Are Running to Stores to Find It

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Think of these backyard birds as your new mascot: They can sit on your porch, front yard, or terrace to offer cheerful charm for visitors and passersby. Though porch geese were once popular in the 1980s, they’re making a comeback in a big way—and are even available at Costco for $35. If you ask us, it’s a small price for so much joy.

When most people think about buying home products from Costco, they usually think of strictly practical purchases: Heaps of toilet paper rolls, a shed to conceal patio furniture, or even furniture that can turn a home office into a bedroom on demand. But make no mistake, even Costco is brimming with whimsy.

Clocking in at 24 inches tall, this porch goose from Evergreen Enterprises is the Goldilocks of outdoor decor: It’s large enough to make a statement but not as cumbersome as, say, a 12-foot-tall skeleton. The metal frame is sturdy enough to hold its own should a gust of wind sweep your neighborhood, while the powder-coated finish keeps rust and corrosion to a minimum. That said, if your goose gets a little dirty, you can disconnect the body from its webbed feet and give it a quick wipe-down.

You have to admit this porch goose is adorable on its own, but what makes this pick even cuter is that it comes with a pair of outfits: Shoppers can choose between a gardener ensemble and rain slicker or a “grill master” look, as well as a tropical shirt and lei. But, honestly, why stop there? A quick Amazon search yields clothes for Thanksgiving, Christmas, and even Valentine’s Day.

Sure, adding a few more costumes to your goose’s closet is an extra expense, but if you want to bring some smile-inducing quirk, it’s an investment that’s so worth it.

#6848. The 15 Best Beauty Minis That Fit Right in the Palm of Your Hand

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fun-size favorites from Prada, Rare Beauty, and more make perfect holiday stocking stuffers

The ideas that things can be “small but mighty” and that “good things come in small packages” are especially true for your favorite beauty products. There’s plenty of room, metaphorically and literally, to curate a collection of the best beauty minis, perfect for packing in your carry-on or gifting to friends and family for the holidays.

Itty-bitty, often limited-edition versions of rotation staples you know and love are simply adorable, like Rare Beauty by Selena Gomez Soft Pinch Liquid Blush and Prada’s tiniest set of stylish metal claw clips. Plus, these staples are not only aesthetically pleasing, but also won’t take up too much real estate on your counter or in a festive stocking. If you’re looking to expand your collection of hair, makeup, skincare, and fragrance goodies while keeping it small, I invite you to peruse our short list of the best, too-cute-for-words beauty minis.

Sure, your best friend could use regular bar soap, but why not gift them with this distinguished set of four minis instead? This second edition of Soft Services’s Buffing Bar Miniature Collection might be even better than the first and is entirely dedicated to gourmand scents. From the vanilla-coconut cream, espresso-, and citrus-scented Affogato L'Orange to their best-selling and extremely fresh Green Banana bar to try, and more, chances are your friend will be taking a bunch of calls from the bathtub.

Whether you’re between washes or just want extra volume, K18’s keychain-friendly AirWash Dry Shampoo is great to keep on hand. The non-aerosol shampoo absorbs excess scalp oil and eliminates odor thanks to translucent microbeads and the brand’s patented Odorbind technology.

Any Lip Butter Balm superfan will be thrilled to unbox this four-piece mini set of some of Summer Fridays’ best sellers. This limited edition includes Birthday Cake, Strawberry Soft Serve, Pink Guava, and Cherry flavors your friend can enjoy for weeks, plus supremely hydrated lips.

#6847. A Guide to The Gilded Age Families: the Scotts, van Rhijns, and Russells

Making sense of the cousins, fiancées, and adopted sons that keep us fascinated as viewers.

The Gilded Age is ultimately a story about society and class, so it’s unsurprising that family ties and marriages are central to the plot. That also means that it can be difficult to sort out who’s who and how they’re all related. As the fourth season continues to gather steam, here’s a guide to all the players in the Scott, van Rhijn, and Russell families.

Peggy grew up in a prosperous Black family but has a difficult relationship with her father, a pharmacist who is unhappy with her decision to become a writer. Peggy was previously married and had a son, Thomas, whom she believed was stillborn. She later learns that her father lied to her and that Thomas is alive and living with an adoptive family. She meets Marian Brook when she is traveling home to her family and begins living with the van Rhijn family. Peggy becomes a reporter for the prestigious Black newspaper, the Globe, and writes a novel. She becomes engaged to Dr. Kirkland, a physician from a prominent family who treats her when she is seriously ill.

Arthur is the father of Peggy and the husband of Dorothy. He was born into slavery but became the successful owner of a pharmacy in Brooklyn. He has a difficult relationship with his daughter.

Peggy’s mother, Dorothy, is a pianist from Maryland who meets her husband in New York City when he saves her from men who were following her. Peggy is her only child.

When Peggy learns that Thomas, the son she believed had died, is alive and living in Philadelphia with the Spring family that adopted him, he is 3 years old. Shortly before Peggy finds out about him, Thomas dies from scarlet fever along with his adoptive mother.

Dr. Kirkland is a physician whom Peggy’s parents bring to treat her when she has a serious respiratory illness. He knew Peggy’s father when he was a child, and Arthur’s pharmacy career inspired him to become a doctor. He and Peggy date and then become engaged at the end of season 3.

Mrs. Kirkland, Dr. Kirkland’s mother, is from a wealthy Newport family. She expects her son to marry someone from a more pedigreed family than Peggy, and when she meets Mr. and Mrs. Scott, she treats them poorly, emphasizing her family’s status and Arthur’s former enslavement. She doesn’t approve of Peggy’s political activism.

Dr. Kirkland’s father is a pastor in Newport who grew up in a wealthy and well-known family.

Agnes is a widow and mother to Oscar. She was married to Arnold van Rhijn, an investment banker from a wealthy, early New York family. She is an aunt to Marian and lives with her sister Ada in a beautiful home on East 61st Street.

Ada is a devoted sister to Agnes, whom she lives with, and aunt to Marian and Oscar. When she meets Reverend Luke Forte, they marry quickly, and Ada begins living with him in the church rectory, which upsets the balance between her and Agnes. When her husband dies, Ada unexpectedly inherits a fortune, making her now wealthier than her sister.

Marian is the niece of Ada and Agnes, the daughter of their estranged brother. After her father dies, Marian finds out she’s been left no money, and she comes to live with her aunts, from whom she had been estranged. She becomes engaged to the widower Dashiell, who is Agnes’s nephew by marriage and Oscar’s cousin from his father’s side, but she later ends it. She has an on-and-off relationship with Larry Russell, George and Bertha Russell’s son.

An Episcopal reverend, Luke meets Ada after moving to New York from Boston and assuming a position at the Episcopal Church of Saint Thomas. He dies shortly after marrying Ada, leaving her the textile fortune he inherited from his mother.

Aurora, who is a major figure on the society scene, is a cousin of Oscar and the niece of Agnes. She becomes close with Marian through their family connections. Aurora is devastated when her husband, Charles, asks for a divorce.

Oscar is a banker and the calculating young son of Agnes, who is gay and closeted but eager to marry a wealthy woman. He proposes to Gladys Russell, but he loses interest when he believes her family might go broke. Oscar has a secret relationship with John Adams IV, a descendant of the political family. In season 4, he marries Enid, a widow with a fortune who shares his interest in social climbing.

Enid was once a maid for the Russell family, but she was let go after trying to seduce George. Enid reappears in season 2 as the wife of Joshua Winterton, an older man, and inherits his money and his place in society when he dies. When Enid gets to know Oscar, they become close and eventually marry, making her the daughter-in-law of Agnes.

A Cavalier King Charles spaniel, Pumpkin is the constant companion of Ada. Larry Russell rescues Pumpkin from a scary run-in with a horse.

Bertha Russell and her husband, George, move to New York City after his work in the railroad industry makes them very wealthy. Bertha is calculating and desperate to be admitted into high-level Manhattan society. She’s eager for her daughter Gladys to marry someone with a pedigreed background. She is also the mother of Larry.

Bertha’s husband is a cutthroat, often cruel, railroad magnate with a distaste for union workers but is deeply loving toward his children, Gladys and Larry. He moves out of his home after believing that his wife’s interference in her children’s love lives is inappropriate.

Bertha and George’s oldest child is an aspiring architect and Harvard grad with a more modern approach to hierarchy and social norms than his mother. He has a flirtation with Marian Brook.

Gladys is the overprotected daughter of Bertha and George. Her mother is focused on finding the right kind of husband for Gladys. Gladys is proposed to by Oscar van Rhijn, but her father doesn’t allow her to marry him because he questions Oscar’s intent. Gladys wants to be with Billy Cotton, a man she met at a party, but her mother believes she should be with the Duke of Buckingham, Hector Vere, a royal figure. Gladys marries Hector and lives with him in England. They have a child.

Everyone is atwitter when the fancy Duke of Buckingham visits New York. Though he has royal lineage, he has no money. He marries Gladys, knowing that marrying her will bring him significant wealth.

The Marquess of Sidmouth is the son of Gladys and Hector. He is their only child.

Sarah is Hector’s sister and lives with him and Gladys in their castle.

Bertha has a tenuous relationship with her sister Monica, whom we first meet when she attends Gladys’s wedding.

#6846. Casio’s New Mechanical Field Watch Is a Game-Changer

The affordable titanium timepiece puts the Japanese brand squarely in the consideration set for budding collectors.

Since forever, Casio has dominated the market for affordable digital and quartz watches. Now it's making a play for the wallet-friendly end of the mechanical watch market, territory traditionally dominated by fellow Japanese manufacturers Seiko, Citizen, and Orient.

The new CMK-100 is Casio's first automatic watch to carry its mainline branding, following the introduction of mechanical models under its Edifice label last year. And on paper, it's a pretty compelling proposition.

Inspired by classic military field watches—with more than a passing resemblance to the Rolex Explorer and the Tudor Ranger—the CMK-100 has a 38mm titanium case, sapphire crystal, screw-down crown, and 100 meters of water resistance. Its textured dial features luminous Arabic numerals, while a Japanese automatic movement provides a 42-hour power reserve. The movement's manufacturer hasn't been disclosed, although its specifications suggest it could be a Miyota 8215, a calibre used by Timex, Spinnaker, and Invicta, among others.

There are five versions: one on a titanium bracelet, two on nylon straps, and two more on Italian leather straps—including a rather jazzy brown-dial model with a rose gold-colored case. Prices range from $330 to $440.

For Seiko, which has built a substantial business selling affordable mechanical watches through collections such as the Seiko 5 Sports, Casio's arrival in this category represents an interesting challenge. Titanium cases and sapphire crystals are hardly standard equipment at this price, and Casio already has an enormous following among younger watch buyers.

Whether those customers will embrace a mechanical from the home of G-Shock remains to be seen. But for anyone shopping for a good-looking, affordable automatic watch, there's now another Japanese name in the running for your attention.

#6841. With the Hunger Games Press Tour, Have We Reached the Final Frontier of Method Dressing?

The cast has offered a pointed take on the state of today’s red carpets

Another Hunger Games movie, a prequel to the initial series, premieres later this month, and with it comes another press tour full of fantastically far-fetched fashion. As the promotional events begin to stack up for Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, we are reminded of the familiar lore of this dystopian empire: In a fictionalized future America called Panem, the 12 districts that supply resources to the rich and powerful people of the Capitol are forced to send two children to fight to the death each year. The excess of the Capitol is signaled in the films via outlandish style, which the newest film’s cast members have been attempting to channel in their recent looks. The goal is to be extreme—but in an era of extremes on the red carpet, these over-the-top ensembles actually don’t look so out of place.

Elle Fanning plays a young Effie Trinket, who is essentially a Capitol stylist dressing the young “tributes” in all sorts of fanciful fashions—and doing the same for herself. (In the first film series, the character wore a lot of McQueen.) So far, Fanning’s press looks have deliberately channeled that outlandishness. For a London fan screening, she wore a gown from Robert Wun’s Fall 2026 couture collection that subverted ideas of childlike wonder. Her ensemble began in miniature with a music-box-style ballerina atop a bubblegum pink fascinator. The corresponding human-size gown features a doubled-up bodice, the outer layer appearing to peel off, above a full tulle skirt, all in that same dusty pink.

For her other appearances, Fanning has established a de facto uniform of precisely tailored skirt suits, each paired with some sort of coordinating fascinator tilted just so above her brow. She has selected a soft pink pleated skirt suit from Valentino complete with a ruffled collar, gloves, and a butterfly-trimmed chapeau; a sharp-shouldered version in a deep, almost reptilian satin green from Demna’s Gucci; and a floral, bow-embellished one from Vivienne Westwood. The looks are decidedly feminine in a manner that is at once both futuristic and retro in very Capitol fashion.

Fanning’s stylist Samantha McMillen has been vocal about the references to the character, with captions like “Gucci Effie | @demna puts “The Hunger” into @thehungergames,” and “Elle bringing 🦋 “Sunrise” 🦋 to the Academy Gala.” And it’s not just Fanning doing this either. Costar Mckenna Grace recently recreated a Katniss Everdeen Capitol tour look in which Katniss’s dress quite literally caught fire. Created by Vietnamese designer Cong Tri, who is known for his sculptural silhouettes, the recreation nixed the pyrotechnics but printed graphic flames onto the skirt and bodice. With the help of stylist Molly Dickson, Grace also revived Katniss’s wedding dress with a frothy white, silver-embellished gown by Monique Lhuillier.

I haven’t seen the most recent films, but all of them examine the same concept of this dystopian society propped up by the Hunger Games themselves at different points on a timeline. In the first film specifically, the older Trinket uses their version of a press tour to craft a narrative around Katniss and her fellow tribute Peeta Mellark and win favor among their audience through larger-than-life fashion. The aforementioned fire dress referenced Everdeen’s district’s coal mining industry, and the wedding dress was meant to create the illusion of a love story between the two of them. The series of looks is designed to be projected to the surrounding communities—and garner as much attention as possible. The characters can quite literally benefit later in the life-and-death-style games from the loyalties and sympathies the theatrics spawn.

It’s only in recent years that this idea of method dressing, or theming press tour looks around an actor’s current project, has become commonplace. It began in earnest with the work of Law Roach and Zendaya around the Dune and Spider-Man franchises, Challengers, and The Drama. It was perhaps commercially crystallized in 2023 by Andrew Mukamal and Margot Robbie with Barbie—a press tour that spawned so much digital attention it earned its own coffee-table book—and, more recently, Wuthering Heights. Now, it has proof of concept, attracting stars like Cynthia Erivo and Ariana Grande in their promotion of Wicked, the new cast of Sense and Sensibility, and Lili Reinhart’s rom-com references for The Love Hypothesis. At its most reductive, the tactic is a surefire way to drum up conversation on social media; at its best, a clever way to add depth to a project’s storytelling.

As press tours become more and more oriented towards social media, it’s difficult to isolate the influence of method dressing on the red carpet’s current proclivity towards extremes. Everyone is trying to capitalize on the sort of hype that the Met Gala ensembles can drum up. Your reference needs to be loud enough to cut through the noise—pyrotechnics optional. It’s perhaps why avant-garde, couture, and couture-level designers like Wun, who dressed a large handful of stars at this year’s Met Gala, are carving out more and more space among these sorts of promotional events. At the same time, brands like Standing Ground and Matieres Fecales—it’s only a matter of time before Fanning wears one of those skirt suits—are also gaining traction as stylists treat the red carpet more like a magazine editorial than an occasion to get dressed up.

Then last night, Grace added another layer. She and Dickson have begun to look outside the films to reference looks of Jennifer Lawrence’s own. Lawrence played Katniss in the first movies. For a Madrid premiere, she wore a Miss Sohee couture dress that paid homage to the Givenchy naked dress Lawrence wore for this year’s Golden Globes. It all feels very meta, calling into question the role of fashion in these promotional spaces, real or fictional. On the IRL Hunger Games red carpet, the reference is dystopian extravagance—but with the rise of method dressing and the corresponding celebration of larger, louder looks, has dystopian extravagance already become the norm? In this case, it may not be life and death, but it could make or break your movie.
