#6840. Allison Ellsworth Sold Poppi for $2 Billion. Now She’s Doing It Again.

The prebiotic soda co-founder is back with a new hydration drink called Jetty.

In ELLE’s Office Hours series, we ask people in powerful positions to take us through their first jobs, worst jobs, and everything in between. This month, we talk to Allison Ellsworth, the co-founder and former chief brand officer at Poppi, the prebiotic soda company she built from the ground up with her husband, Stephen, and then sold to PepsiCo in 2025 for an incredible $2 billion.

The Ellsworths could have sailed off into the sunset to enjoy their success, but instead, the entrepreneurial-minded couple is doing it again. Today, they are launching Jetty, a new hydration beverage for real people, not athletes. They say it’s better for you, too, with no artificial colors, dyes, or sweeteners.

Below, Allison shares the personal inspiration behind both of her companies, the riskiest move she made, and why she was eager to start Jetty.

My first job was working at a dance studio, where I did everything from cleaning the toilets in the locker room and sweeping the floors to teaching classes and selling shoes. I started working from a really young age; I think I was 14 when I started at the studio. I was blessed to learn to do something that I absolutely loved, and there was a great community there as well. But also, it is very humbling at that age to have to be cleaning toilets and taking out the trash. So, I learned the basics in life early on, which I think every kid needs.

One of the worst jobs I had, even though I was awesome at it, was at a fitness company. I was No. 5 in the nation in sales—I absolutely killed it. But I remember why it was a bad experience, and that was because I didn’t have a good boss. Even though I was No. 5 in the nation and No. 1 in Texas, he tried to tell me I shouldn’t dress the way I dress and shouldn’t speak the way I spoke. And I was like, “But it’s working.” It was just his ego; he had to be right. And he’s the one that drove me away and made me quit, because if you don’t have a good leader, it’s really hard to be in a job even if you’re great at it and making good money.

Before starting Poppi, I worked in oil and gas research. Unfortunately, it’s not like the show The Landman with Billy Bob Thornton, which seems much more exciting than what I did. I used to live in teeny little towns and work on multimillion-dollar projects for oil companies, figuring out who owns mineral and surface rights and permits and things like that. I would live in these small towns for years, and I just love that that was my foundation because it taught me how to be really independent. I was out there by myself. You don’t have a boss out there telling you what to do. You are making a real impact and getting to know people and learning how they work.

I was a self-starter; I was doing that job when I was like 20. And it’s a very male-dominated industry. But it prepared me for the beverage industry, which is also very male-dominated. I mean, I had the largest exit of any female founder ever in beverage history. I’ve always just taken it as a challenge to play the game better.

I was on the road all the time for my oil and gas job, and when you’re traveling constantly, you don’t feel good. I had horrible tummy problems, so I started drinking apple cider vinegar, but I hated the way it tasted. So I went to my kitchen because I love to cook and I was like, “I got to make this taste good.” That first version was awful—let me just tell you—it was like rocket fuel. But I learned through trial and error. I would give it to my husband and neighbors until it got to where everyone loved it. There was a lot of googling and asking to hop on calls with people who had done it before. And then I took it on Shark Tank and got a deal.

When I went on Shark Tank, it was with the first product I created, which was called Mother Beverage. We had to figure out how to get our ingredients, how we would manufacture it, but also, like with any startup, how we were going to make money to put food on the table. We sold our cars—we sold everything; we maxed out our credit cards to buy bottles and start manufacturing. After Shark Tank, we were like, “Let’s take a beat.” We took nine months off and did a total rebrand into Poppi, and then launched it in March 2020, literally the first week of COVID. At the time, it felt very devastating, but it was the first time everybody in the world was thinking about their health and wellness—some people for the first time. They were reading the ingredients and thinking about boosting their immunity, like, “Maybe I shouldn’t be drinking soda with 40 grams of sugar.”

So we had triple-digit growth year over year. We were one of the fastest-growing beverages in the history of beverages. We went from zero to $500 million in 4.5 years, and then sold to Pepsi for $2 billion in March 2025. So it’s not been normal, and I’m very blessed, but I also think sometimes hard work looks like luck, but it was years of sacrifice to get it to that point. Everyone’s like, “Oh my gosh, that seemed so quick and easy.” And I’m like, “It wasn’t.”

There were so many moments, but I think a really big one that changed the whole trajectory of Poppi was that we were one of the first brands to get on TikTok. It sounds crazy now because everyone’s on there now, but it was unusual back in 2020. I remember everyone told me I was stupid to do it, and that no brands could be on there because all it was was dancing kids, and I said, “Yeah, but I think there’s something here.” So I said, “I will take my weekends and my nights, and I will do this by myself.” I did that for like three months, and no one cared because it wasn’t working. But then I sat down one night, and I told my story of how I started Poppi in my kitchen, and how I was nine months pregnant on Shark Tank, and it blew up and we did $100,000 on Amazon overnight. Grocery store shelves were cleared. We were trending on Google. So then everyone was like, “Oh, Alison, I guess your TikTok idea wasn’t the worst.” We ended up moving 80 percent of our digital advertising budget over to TikTok; it changed the whole trajectory of how we marketed. Now, being digital-first is a no-brainer, but it was pretty new six years ago; no brands were really doing it.

A really big career moment was when I realized that we were growing really fast at Poppi and that I might not be the right person to run our marketing anymore. As an entrepreneur, you don’t start a company to have people tell you what to do. But we were growing so fast and we were looking at buying a Super Bowl ad, and I was like, “I’ve never done any of this before.” So I went to the board and our CEO at the time and I said, “I think we should bring someone in to run marketing.” It’s a really vulnerable place to be, to ask for help. And not a lot of people will do that; they let their ego get in the way of growth. But they heard me and we brought someone in who ended up doing all the stuff that I wasn’t good at. It really was one of the best decisions, but it was really hard. I cried. It felt like a loss of control at the time. But it was a bold decision to ask for help, and I think a lot of people downplay how powerful that can be.

I really think it was really when we sold to Pepsi. When we were starting out, we always knew we wanted to sell the company. I know it can sound really harsh to be like, “We started a company to sell it,” but that’s not what I’m saying. In the beverage industry, we wanted to grow, as our mission was to revolutionize soda for the next generation. If you want to get your product everywhere, you have to sell to one of three companies: Keurig Dr. Pepper, Coca-Cola, or PepsiCo, because they have huge distribution. We would never be able to go everywhere on our own. We couldn’t be on college campuses; we couldn’t be in stadiums. We could never be at Subway, which we’re in now. We’re in Starbucks. All of these things would never have been options if we hadn’t sold. And we knew that day one, so it was always the goal. So before we sold, any time something big would happen, like we’d work with a celebrity or something, I’d say, “We’re not done yet.” I’m one of those people who never stops to smell the roses. I’m a Capricorn. I work 80 hours a week. I’m so hardcore, but finally when we sold, I was like, “Oh wow, we really built something incredible.”

It was an incredible moment of “I’ve made it,” but it was also a time of immense loss. Which everyone says to founders who exit, like, “Oh, poor baby,” but we worked for years to build something that involved so much sacrifice, blood, sweat, and tears that it’s hard to just hand it over like, “Here you go!” So, it was the highest high, like “Oh my gosh, we’ve made it. We created a generational brand.” But also, “Now what?”

Before you get acquired, you date for years. At first, all the big beverage brands were like, “You’re cute, but you will never disrupt us.” They were not paying attention to us. But when we got into Target, Kroger, and Walmart, they noticed us. When you hit $500 million in revenue, that is a really good time to start thinking about being acquired before you are too big to be bought.

Pepsi made an initial offer the year before we sold, but it didn’t feel right, so we said no. They offered $1 billion, but we knew with our numbers and our growth, it wasn’t a good deal. We knew we had something special—we were one of the fastest-growing beverages in history. You have to know your worth, but it was still so stressful to say no to $1 billion. But they watched us continue to grow; they watched our household penetration increase; we were gaining market share. And when they came back and gave us the second offer a year later, we knew it was really good, and it was easy to say yes. We talked about it for about three days and then accepted. And we closed six weeks later. So it was very quick in the end, because we’d been talking to them for years.

What I have learned since selling Poppi is that being retired at 39 is not a thing. My husband and I absolutely adore working together. He’s everything I’m not, and I’m everything he’s not when it comes to business and in life. I missed the build. I missed the team. I missed creating something culturally relevant and building a legacy—all of the above. I think it’d be crazy not to try again.

And what’s fun about doing it this time is I can do it with all the knowledge that I’ve gained. Also, the pressure to sell is off. But I do think I would still give everybody equity in the new company, with the goal of monetizing it, because that is still the American Dream. I saw it happen at Poppi. We made more than 44 people millionaires and changed people’s lives. They bought houses, paid off their cars, bought their mom’s houses, so many things. So continuing to be an entrepreneur is absolutely in the cards.

Poppi started because of my health, and Jetty is the same. Sometimes I will get to 11 A.M. or noon and be like, “Huh, I haven’t had any water today.” Maybe I will have had coffee or matcha, but I was just really struggling to hydrate. So we have created the first-ever “anti-hydration” hydration company. Traditionally, people have created hydration drinks for athletes; they call them sports drinks. Nobody has created approachable, everyday hydration for normal people. You’re not running a marathon; you didn’t just go to the gym for two hours; you need something not crazy overloaded with sugar and electrolytes. Everything’s just so intense in that space. Other products have dyes, artificial ingredients, 1,000 milligrams of sodium—they were giving me tummy problems. My husband said he would make me a better one, because he’s like, “We know how to do that.” And so he worked with one of our previous food scientists to develop it. One day he brought samples to the house and I was like, “Oh my gosh, this is so good.” I was drinking it every day, and he was like, “Do we need to share this with people?” And everyone’s like, “Guys, hello, you need to do this again.” And then I was like, “We can build again. We know what we’re doing. We know the recipe for success.”

So we created Jetty, a better-for-you electrolyte drink and powder. We’re going to have five flavors: fruit punch, watermelon, orange mango, black raspberry, and white cherry, and we will launch nationwide in Target, Whole Foods, and Sprouts. With Poppi, we had this mission to revolutionize soda for the next generation. And if we can disrupt soda, we can disrupt hydration too.

#6838. Dakota Johnson Does High-Low Dressing in a Hoodie and Tons of Diamonds

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Diamonds—and hoodies—really are a girl’s best friend. On Thursday night, Dakota Johnson offered up a dose of high-low dressing for a date night with Machine Gun Kelly.

#6837. This Colorful California Mountain Home Gives Classic Ski House Style a Fresh New Look

Using the surrounding landscape as palette inspiration, designer Alden Fletcher brought 70s retro flair to this family retreat.

When most people think of a classic ski chalet, they picture log-lined walls, dark corners, a raging hearth, plenty of plaid, and maybe even a taxidermied deer or two. But a certain kind of magic strikes when high-altitude architecture hits the right notes, and this Truckee, California, retreat is a masterclass in making the mountains sing.

Built almost 20 years ago, this family holiday home boasted all the 90s mountain classics: an abundance of knotty pine, traditional slate floors, and a minimalist beige palette. Undoubtedly stylish for the time, the retreat now felt out of step with the Bay Area family looking to make it into a personalized winter escape. The owners knew updates were imminent (necessary, even), but they feared losing the home’s classic California soul. Fighting modernism’s white-box aesthetic, they called in Alden Fletcher of the eponymous San Francisco-based design firm to meld the home’s retro architecture with a fresh, forest-inspired brief.

Noting the owner’s concerns and dedication to sustainability, the project’s goal was not to remodel for grandeur, but to restore the home’s charm. Fletcher’s task was to design around storied memories, the family’s obvious love for winter sports, and their passion for entertaining—all while combining the crisp aesthetic of the alpine wilderness with cozy textiles and natural details to create the ultimate mountain sanctuary. By pulling saturated hues from the surrounding landscape and sourcing perky patterns inspired by the local culture, the redesign breathes new life into the slope-side abode without sacrificing sentiment.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Alden Fletcher Interiors Stylist: Beth Protass Location: Truckee, CAThe Space: A five-bedroom, four-bathroom home across 3,000 square feet

The home's hallways set the tone for the entire design scheme. On the first floor, just beyond the main door, wall-mounted ski runners double as decor. Upstairs, Fletcher redefined the boundaries of the primary suite to carve out a nook for a vignette displaying the home’s most bespoke decor piece: a vintage cherry-red chairlift. “The home’s location is unique,” adds Fletcher. “It sits on a ski slope, and you can view chairlifts out the back windows!”

A true gathering space, the living room was designed around the original stone-faced fireplace, though Fletcher did replace the former pine mantel with white oak, connecting the floor details with the exposed oak beams running along the ceiling.

Swiveling burnt orange armchairs accompany an inky indigo sectional. Both gather around a button-tufted ottoman, which doubles as a coffee perch or game table. Shaded sconces flank the fireplace to give the room a glow, even when the hearth is unlit, while a pair of vintage ski shoes hang on the wall, crowning the design.

In the playroom, the deep green hue of the wallpaper paired with the natural texture of knotty pine borrows inspiration from the forest-like landscape just beyond the window. Vertical lines carried through both the wallpaper and the wainscoting create the illusion of height, while playful ski motifs bring the local sport’s culture into the play space.

“The clients are sustainably minded and didn't want to get rid of what was working,” says Fletcher. With conservation in mind, she opted to keep the original perimeter cabinets, refreshing them with paint, new hardware, and Virginia Black Satin Granite countertops, a sturdier alternative to show-stopping soapstone.

To optimize the space, she nixed the two existing islands, reimagining the room to fit a larger iteration that boasts a sink and easily seats five. The design team then added upper cabinets, transforming the space into a storage haven. Later, she placed a library ladder and golden hardware for easy access and a touch of whimsy. Then, she turned her attention to making sure the lighting was up to par. Fletcher firmly believes a beautiful space should be equally as functional. To ensure no detail fell short, she installed discreet recessed lights alongside under-cabinet lighting, and two large glass pendants to adorn the space. All dimmable for ambiance—of course. “There’s nothing worse than a home kitchen that is just flooded with bright light everywhere. A kitchen should still feel cozy and warm, and good lighting is the key to accomplishing that,” says Fletcher.

Fletcher compensated for this former powder room’s lack of windows with vibrant wallpaper to energize the space and make the most of interior lighting. Borrowing space from an adjacent closet, she converted this former two-piece bath into a full retreat with a shower, vanity, and storage, finishing the space with gold hardware for an additional touch of luxury.

Perhaps the most customized room in the home, the mudroom boasts bold color and smart storage solutions for a space as functional as it is chic. The custom cabinet is an ode to smart design, featuring bench seating, pegs, an overhead shelf, and under-seat shoe storage—but the real showstopper is its bold red hue.

“My favorite element of the built-in is the color. The brick color has just the right amount of earthiness,” says Fletcher.

The family loves to entertain, often hosting large groups of friends and family. To accommodate their social lifestyle, Fletcher designed a hospitality hub. Removing a bathroom door, she altered the room’s layout, allowing space for this guest suite to come to life.

With the new dimensions secured, she covered the walls in printed wallpaper inspired by the great local outdoors. The bold pattern set the tone for the rest of the design as she pulled colors, patterns, and themes to complement it.

For the primary bedroom, the theme was warmth: a warm bed, warm home, warm soul, all channeled through muted terracotta walls. But before it could truly transform, the team had to iron out a few oddball details.

“This room used to have a few odd details, including transom windows to the adjoining bathroom and an exterior door to a deck. We removed those elements, which allowed for a better furniture layout and a cozier feeling,” says Fletcher.

The room features a traditional four-poster bed in a nearly black finish, which carries over to the lighting details and furniture hardware, bringing out the warmth and depth of the surrounding rich red hues.

For the primary bath, the team capitalized on the luxury of the sloped-side views, framing a classic 400-pound slipper tub with a nearly floor-to-ceiling window featuring vintage pine trim. Truly the focal point of the design, Fletcher finished the window treatment with woven shades to tie in the natural wood details of the home and stand up to moisture. Natural grain runs through the room’s doors and vanity, topped with soft white Calacatta marble, channeling the classic ski chalet aesthetic. Gold fixtures extend from the vanity to the lighting, towel rack, and tub, for a touch of glamour.

Boasting the project’s biggest splurge, a custom bunk-trundle bed, the kid’s room combines playful hues with forest-inspired details and maximum sleepover potential. “We wanted to maximize sleeping spots in this home,” Fletcher explains. “The Casa Kids bunk bed in the kid's room was a splurge, but it can sleep 5!”

Alden Fletcher first fell in love with design while working as an art teacher and refurbishing furniture in her downtime. In 2020, she grew that passion into a full-service design firm focused on bringing functionality to storied designs and creating ultra-personalized interiors.

#6835. Kendall Jenner Thinks Some Things Are Better Naked

The founder’s latest 818 Tequila campaign takes a stripped-back approach.

According to Kendall Jenner, some things are better naked. Buttered pasta, for one.

It’s not exactly the answer I expected when I posed the question to the model during our recent Zoom conversation, but it makes perfect sense in the context of her latest project. Jenner is gearing up to launch “Nothing Extra,” a new campaign for her tequila brand, 818, built around the idea that sometimes, less really is more.

And the visuals certainly back up this sentiment. Filmed at Flamingo Estate, they have all the makings of a countryside daydream: lush greenery, baskets brimming with fruit, and Jenner wandering through the garden in a breezy dress. That romantic, almost storybook-like setting, she tells me, was no accident. Jenner says the original concept leaned more heavily into humor and sex appeal, but she ultimately wanted something softer.

“I wanted it to feel a little bit more based in nature, rather than too sexy by any means,” she explains.

But tequila and pasta are only the beginning. Below, Jenner discusses what she’s learned about feeling comfortable in her own skin, her love of maximalist interiors, and the family member she trusts most to make her a cocktail.

We actually went through a few different ideas. Considering the message we are trying to convey with the tequila being nothing added, nothing extra, it’s just naked. When that idea came through, I loved the kitschiness of it, considering the tequila is technically naked. So we loved it. We had a few other ideas, but for some reason, we always kept coming back to this one. I think it’s a fun way to bring people in, but also a fun way to tell the story of the tequila.

I wanted to keep the focus on the tequila because I built this business to focus on the product and to make a great product while also being able to have fun with it. I think there’s always a fine line for me of a little too much or a little too sexy. Within the right circumstances, it’s so fun to go there. But for this, I’ve always wanted the brand to be taken seriously, and I felt like this was a fun way to be able to do both.

We actually had a lot more humor originally. I kind of sat with it for a while and came back to the team and felt like too much comedy and too much naked might not work. I wanted to take the humor down a little bit. So when rethinking it, I kind of came up with this little bit more whimsical, little bit more airy, fresh feeling. Considering we are talking about something that’s crafted with just three ingredients and there’s nothing extra, I wanted it to feel a little bit more based in nature, rather than too sexy by any means. I really liked the idea of being in nature and being somewhere as beautiful as Flamingo Estate and being within the trees and amongst the plants and with the fruits and the vegetables. Tequila is alcohol, at the end of the day, but we wanted to just have a little bit fresher of a feeling considering it is fresh.

It was ultimately originally imagined. That’s kind of why we settled on Flamingo Estate, because it was part of the references, their landscape and all those things. I feel like our team did a really good job of taking my thoughts and bringing them into reality, which was really nice.

Obviously, I think environment plays into that, but it’s a lot of self-work. Not to go there and be all mental health-y, but it really is so much self-work through experiences and through reminding myself of the things that I’m comfortable with. I love setting boundaries, and I love holding myself to them. I feel best when I’m in a great environment with great people, but then also when I feel like my mind is ultimately right. I’m sure this goes for everyone, but I think I’ve really found my own little remedies and ways to get myself there.

I know people say it all the time, but you’re really nothing without a great team, and I feel that so greatly here. I still am surprised by my team every single day, but I’m also really inspired by how small we’ve remained with how much we’ve built. I’ve loved that process as well. I feel like it’s a little family, and I love that I can text every single person on the team, or get on a Zoom with them once a week and feel like we have this level of communication where we get so much done with just our great little crew. It’s been really cool to build this from the ground up with essentially the same team since day one and to be here now five years later and be able to all feel it together.

Oh, my God, I feel like a lot. A lot has changed. I’d say the biggest thing for me [is] the way I handle myself within stressful situations, or bad situations, or good situations. I think I’ve really grown into myself in that way. I’ve always been someone who’s really remained true to myself. Even as a kid, I never gave in to peer pressure. I’ve always kind of had my own little path. And the ways I’ve been able to stick to that and remain honest with myself, while also being open to learning and open to changing and for the better, if you will, my journey with life and always hoping to keep my mind right, I think has only taught me so much about myself. Learning me as well has been really fun and exciting.

My mom, for sure. She’s the most fun and has a great time when it comes to alcohol, and she’s just really good at making a good cocktail. She has a great bar at her house. We do these little series on YouTube where we’ll take a classic cocktail and we’ll make it an 818 way, so we’ll change the alcohol out. Instead of whiskey, for example, we’ll make it tequila. We’ve made really good Tequila Old Fashioneds and things like that.

I live by less is more. Within my style, I always live by that. I think doing too much can get tricky for me. But to answer your question and maybe contradict myself, where more is more is sometimes in my homes. I love a house that feels lived in and full of things that you’ve collected over time. I love special collectibles. I love found things. I love knickknacks. So I think that more is more there. I stand by it there, for sure. I featured my mountain house in Architectural Digest, and that was definitely a situation where I was like, more is more. But then when thinking of a home probably in California, I’d think, okay, less is more here. So it really just depends, I guess, situation by situation.

Something that’s better naked? Oh, my God, I’m trying to think...who doesn’t love a buttered pasta? [It’s] the simplest of pastas. I’m about to make myself that right now, actually.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6830. The Best Burgundy Shoes to Wear With All Your Favorite Fall Outfits

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From office-ready loafers to weekend sneakers, these rich maroon pairs go with everything.

Few colors are as synonymous with fall as burgundy. Something about this moody tone instantly elevates everything it touches, whether it’s a dress, a wool cardigan, a sleek handbag, or a pair of shoes. And speaking of shoes, we’ve scoured the internet to find the best styles to wear now with your favorite autumn pieces.

Everything from boots to ballet flats, Mary Janes, and even sneakers is found in the perfect shade of maroon. Think of this wine shade as a neutral in disguise. It flatters black, navy, olive, camel, charcoal, and cream with equal ease, and it’s the rare color that moves effortlessly from the office to dinner to a weekend farmers market without a second thought.

Ahead, we’ve rounded up the burgundy styles worth your attention this fall, from loafers and boots to heels and flats that earn a place in your rotation.

We didn’t know we needed polka dot mesh Mary Janes in this rich burgundy shade until we came across these Sézane stunners. They’re the kind of shoe you can trust on any occasion, and compliments tend to follow wherever they go. On cooler days, pair them with your favorite cashmere socks.

A classic penny loafer with a slightly tapered silhouette, this pair is ready to carry you from busy workdays or school days to laid-back weekends alike. It’s a timeless staple that swaps the traditional black or brown for a fresh burgundy, giving everyday fall pieces a new perspective.

Unlike most ballet flats, these patent leather pairs feature a cute drawstring peep-toe, an elasticized back, and flexible soles for easy walking. We love the timeless silhouette and glossy finish, which instantly make them feel more party-ready.

Materials: Upper: 100% Cow Leather, Lining: 100% Goat Leather

There’s something undeniably chic about burgundy sneakers, and this Jimmy Choo pair is exhibit A. The rich, wine-toned finish dresses up denim and dresses down tailored trousers, making them an easy way to add polish to a weekend uniform.

These sleek burgundy pumps instantly elevate any ensemble, denim included. They’re the go-tos you can count on whether you’re headed to dinner, a coffee date, or just running errands around town.

Despite their familiar silhouette, these Le Monde Beryl mules bring a fresh perspective to fall dressing. A rounded toe and slightly curved heel add a cool factor to the overall look, and the bordeaux shade is undeniably chic on its own.

With a chic kitten heel and a sharp, structured silhouette, including an ultra-pointed toe, these black cherry leather boots lean into every mood and occasion.

Luxury speaks volumes in these stunning stilettos, crafted from silky burgundy satin. The open toe and slender heel create an elongated line, making them a showstopping choice for holiday parties and special evenings out.

If your feet can’t take stilettos, consider a tall platform heel instead. This burgundy option features a mega block heel, a rounded toe, and a delicate ankle strap, combining for a sophisticated pair with retro flair.

Designed in collaboration with Alison Roman, this snakeskin mule is her go-to hosting shoe, and we can see why. The polished silhouette says party-ready, while the kitten heel keeps things comfortable and understated. Extra points for the burgundy snakeskin python print!

Materials: Python-embossed Italian calf leather

These burgundy wedges with a V-shaped vamp and a soft matte finish are an easy way to add a little height and a lot of polish to your fall wardrobe.

Inspired by retro running shoes, the Nikoletta sneakers land at the intersection of sporty and cool. The crinkled patent stripes contrast beautifully with the soft leather, while the red logo-embossed tongue adds another bold statement.

#6829. MobLand Season 2, Episode 8 Takes a Devastating Turn

Conrad’s grip on reality is beginning to slip.

Last week I wrote a giddy hagiography of Frankie Cooper. It’s embarrassing how much I like him. I really should grow up, be a man, maintain some critical distance, and write in wry asides like a real English journalist.

But I can’t help it. He’s such an irresistibly murderous bastard that he makes me want to kill a cat just to see how it feels. (The lawyers have advised me to clarify that I don’t actually want to kill a cat; no cats were harmed in the making of this opinion.)

Well, Frankie, it’s been lovely. But there’s a new psychopath in town. Of course the Medusa had a vengeful son by the name of “Mad Dog”! Just out of prison for murder, he’s seen CCTV footage of Eddie, who was there when his daddy was killed, and he’s coming to the Cotswolds.

“I’ll find them, Daddy,” says Kelly, pouring a bottle of whisky over his father’s coffin.

But all that’s for episode 9. We’ve got the admin of episode 8 to sort through first.

Remember Harry’s words at the end of episode 7? “Is that Michael Cooper? At 3 a.m. you’re gonna get clapped.”

Well, Harry did indeed have Frankie Cooper’s boy killed. But not quite so easily as we were first led to believe. He wanted to fuck with Frankie in the process. Harry knew that Michael would call his dad, who has Newgate Prison sewn up tighter than a dodgy tailor’s pocket. Frankie therefore arranged for Michael to be moved to the solitary wing, where he would be safe.

But as Harry explains to Kevin: “What Frankie’s forgot is that a bent screw is a bent screw… So if the CM’s [custodial manager] gonna take a tenner, what’s he gonna do for a twenty? Or in this case, 200 grand in a black bag in the dead of night on a pissing-down Thursday.”

Harry then pays a couple of hired goons to string Michael up in his cell. Frankie is not going to be happy.

It’s time we gave Emmett J. Scanlan his flowers . He’s been ticking over in the background, doing a solid, likable job as the Harrigan dogsbody Paul. But he is changing now and emerging like a butterfly into the show’s comic sideline. There’s a real touch of the Baldricks about him, with a stroke of Hamlet’s gravedigger—the loyal dogsbody who gets lumbered with the shitty jobs and stays funny in spite of it all.

The poor sod thinks he’s in for the noose after the Sicily debacle of episode 7. He’s begging for his life, so desperate that he thinks VIP tickets to Rod Stewart might save him. But no. They were never going to kill Paul. It was merely a simple lampoon. They want him to cut up Donnie’s stinking body in a shipping container.

So Paul strips down to his leopard-print pants and, swinging his hips to the Rolling Stones’ “Mr Charm,” sets about chopping Donnie into vacuum-packable “bricks.” It’s all very Tom Cruise in Risky Business, but with more blood and less hairbrush. Long live Paul.

Eddie’s sinking deeper into the soup—and it’s all of his own doing. If only he weren’t—in Kevin’s words—such a “lazy, spoilt, entitled little cunt.” Maeve finds him in the sauna. He tells her their “situation” isn’t going to work.

“Maeve, I fear our relationship is sailing into choppy water. You’re 80. I’m barely legal.”

Maeve feigns surprise. “This approach, this ploy. It’s depraved. And deeply optimistic.”

Conrad, Eddie explains, is going to “piss his kegs.” Suddenly, so is Eddie, but not out of laughter. “Please don’t tell him; I’ll do anything.”

She agrees, but only if Eddie apologizes. On his knees. To her phone camera. It’s all fun and games for Maeve. I suspect we haven’t seen the last of that video.

Jan agrees to meet Harry. She wants to come clean, to pour out her heart. She tries to explain that she had a brief affair with a man who was using her to get to Harry and Conrad. But Harry already knows.

“That’s my job,” he tells her. “To anticipate danger. Control outcomes. Minimize unwanted consequences.”

She asks him about killing. About the nightmares. He says he doesn’t have any. You get the feeling Harry is all out of nightmares. Then he offers to help Jan and Gina run. Forever.

“I can make that happen. Tonight,” he tells her. “You never have to see me again.”

“What about you?” she asks. “Does no part of you want that?”

For a moment, Harry almost seems human. Almost.

It’s clear now: Conrad is losing his marbles. He wanders into the woods, calling out for his dead dogs. Then he stops, surrounded by swirling mists and squawking crows, and seems to realize just how far he’s slipping. He summons Kevin to the Cotswolds, to which the latter reluctantly submits. Conrad tells his son he’s seen a doctor. He wants to talk succession plans. Kevin, astonished at being asked such a question, reels furiously through the options.

First, Eddie: “A lazy, entitled psychopath who I wouldn’t trust to light the barbecue.”

Then Seraphina: “She would make a world-class businesswoman, but a kingpin? I don’t think she’s ever even touched a gun.”

And then, just as Kevin is about to pitch himself, Conrad reveals what’s really going on. “I see the dead,” he hisses with a haunted, hounded look, the air of the ancient mariner. “They come to me at night. I see them in the woods. All the bodies, in the clothes they died in, their wounds fresh. Standing there, staring at me. They’re in my head, they’re in my soul.”

It’s all very King Lear. They embrace and it’s lovely.

Kevin goes to get the old man a brandy to calm his nerves. When he returns, the fear is gone from Conrad’s eyes. In its place: pure steel. And a shotgun pointed at Kevin’s head.

“If you breathe a word of this to Bella, I’ll kill you both,” he growls.

Conrad is another man again. As though the whole confession were just a performance. But it wasn’t. It was a glimpse of the terrifying disintegration of a man who can no longer distinguish between his different selves.

Kevin can’t believe it. “Pull the trigger then,” he shouts snottily.

“Fuck off now, Kevin,” Conrad says. And, like a beaten dog (though of course, Conrad would never treat a dog in this way), off Kevin fucks.

Well, we’ve spent most of this episode wondering about Frankie’s response to the murder of his son. But here we are. Harry calls Frankie. He wants to reach a treaty. He’s killed Frankie’s son, and now he hopes Frankie will back down.

Here we see a side to Frankie we haven’t seen before: crying, blubbering, a human man in real pain. He’s looking through old photographs of Michael.

“He’s dead, Harry,” he says through snot and tears. “Why did you have to do that? I can’t explain it. The pain.”

“It’s the game,” says Harry, heavy as stone.

To which Frankie replies: “Anyway, I’m sure you understand. After all, Harry, you’re a father too. Ain’tcha?”

It’s a less-than-veiled threat; Frankie’s message: this war isn’t over until the babies are all dead. He’s going after Gina.

And so we end the episode in truly miserable style. A montage of sadness, soundtracked by Johnny Cash’s cover of “Ain’t No Grave”:

The Harrigans have made a lot of enemies over the past eighteen episodes. And now, at last, the dead are joining the queue.

#6827. 9 Curb Appeal Trends That Will Take Over in 2027, According to Designers

These are the exterior elements that are getting a major refresh.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Curb appeal is more than having a pretty house.

“It's not just about how a home looks, but how it lives,” says Danielle Faustini, landscape designer and owner of Helix Green Designs. Homeowners and designers alike are stepping up their game when it comes to creating an appealing facade—in some cases, the shift is quite literal, as stairs are becoming an “it” feature.

The latest U.S. Houzz Outdoor Trends study is seeing a shift toward natural elements like wood cladding, beige exteriors, and native plants.

"Professionals on Houzz have shared that homeowners are looking for exteriors that feel grounded and connected to their surroundings," says Annie Thornton, Houzz outdoor trends expert. “Warm beiges and wood siding blend naturally with stone, native plantings and other organic landscape materials, and they suit a wide range of architectural styles. Our studies show that people are moving away from the stark white and cool gray palettes of recent years toward something that feels more timeless, a look that adds a sense of history without tipping into nostalgia.”

Simple upgrades, like a fresh coat of paint on the front door, are also pivoting into colorways anchored in the past. Designer Alexis Warren is seeing a shift toward darker, moodier front doors in shades of green, deep purple, and near-black, like Farrow & Ball’s Paean Black.

Here are 10 curb-appeal-boosting design trends for the year ahead to make your house the envy of the neighborhood.

Introducing a sculptural element—like an outdoor artwork or creatively placed flora—sends a message that your house has a point of view.

“It elevates the sense of arrival and separates your home from other homes in the vicinity,” says designer Lisa Erdmann. To ensure your home truly stands out, look for one-of-a-kind pieces.

For the home pictured above, Robert J. Bell, founder of landscape architecture firm Bell Design, commissioned artisans to create a copper light inspired by a feather. “More than the end of minimalism, the new focus on sculptural shapes brings back a sense of craft, and the expressive and sensory nature that a simple black metal and glass fixture cannot possess,” he says.

To contrast against the patina, Bell chose purple bougainvillea and a sunny yellow paint on the door.

It’s all about adding warmth and texture, and that’s one of the reasons that beige exterior colors have more than doubled in popularity in the last two years, jumping from 10 to 24 percent, according to Houzz.

Think of a palette that extends into the landscape—warm neutrals such as tan, soft cream, khaki, sand, and greige, says Thornton, who also notes that natural wood and wood-look cladding, sandy stucco, and stone are a welcome change.

Adding paneling can mimic the warmth of wood without worrying about upkeep, points out Francisca Alonso, cofounder and CEO of AV Architects + Builders. This trick also helps soften the front elevation. “We like to combine minimalistic modern details with warm tones to make it more inviting and less commercial-looking,” Alonso adds.

A picket fence may be just what your house needs, but that doesn’t mean you have to resort to the predictable white paint.

“Adding a low-profile picket fence is one of the best ways to gently delineate a property line without in any way compromising sightlines of the home,” says Joelle Nesen, principal designer at Maison Inc. “The picket fence has the kind of classical lines that work well with an historic home like this Craftsman in Portland. By not defaulting to the expected white and leaving the fence a natural wood, the picket fence, with its carefully spaced slats that are almost see-through, allows it to blend in with the landscaping and trees that surround the home.”

When you choose gravel over grass or pavers, it’s all about an understated but classy look.

“Gravel and other permeable aggregates like crushed shells are some of my favorite materials for creating more informal outdoor living spaces,” says Faustini. “It is a beautifully tactile and sensory material. Who doesn't love the soft crunch underfoot? And it really softens a space, helping blur the line between outdoor living areas and the surrounding landscape.”

The look works especially well with decking and patinated light fixtures.

Adding native plants around your house feels cohesive, relaxed, and rooted in place, Thornton says.

Rachael Grochowski, founder and principal of RHG Architecture + Design, adds that she expects to see an increase in native landscapes, meadow-type gardens, as well as an integration of clover into lawns.

“The trend to embrace natural landscapes is not only about supporting environment regeneration, but it also supports our wellbeing by instigating wonder, beauty, and randomness,” she says.

Curb appeal is all about first impressions, and homeowners are not taking this feature lightly. Houzz reports that searches for “front walkway” have more than quadrupled (up 357 percent) in the first three quarters of 2026, compared with the same period last year.

Step design, in particular, is one feature that is growing in popularity.

When Sarah Crawford, associate designer with Courtney B. Smith Design, renovated her home, she added steps to her 1920s bungalow to literally and figuratively elevate the experience. The home initially had just one step from the sidewalk to the front door. By adding a series of stairs to gently guide the way to the door, she added a quiet sense of elegance in keeping with the wood-shingled exterior. Key to the transformation was selecting materials that reflect the character and style of the original home, in this case reclaimed granite. Scale and balance were essential to adding a series of stairs to gently guide visitors to the door.

“Steps should never be narrower than the doorway,” Crawford says. “In fact, the wider the steps, the more inviting the approach.”

For a different approach to the front entrance, consider stone pathways, especially those with a casual look, like grass pavers.

Interior designer Darci Hether likes large-format bluestone pavers, which offer a clean, timeless look. “The generous scale of the stones creates a sense of openness, while the greenery between them softens the overall appearance and adds a beautiful, natural element,” she explains.

Erdmann encourages her clients to forgo safe neutrals and treat the front entry door as a place for personality.

“Whether it is hot pink, lipstick red, cobalt blue, or Benjamin Moore’s color of the year, Webster Green—make a statement!” she says. “Don’t match the door to the shutters and choose something fun. A front door is the easiest, lowest-risk place to experiment since it takes one afternoon and a quart of paint.”

Case in point, Elizabeth Cross-Beard, interior designer behind Palm Henri, took to a vibrant peacock blue to accentuate the lines of the above 1925 French manor house. “When paired with natural materials and muted tones, [the paint] brings just enough color to intrigue the eye without disrupting the overall sense of classic grandeur. Unlike other blue tones which can call too much attention to them, this rich color is the front-door sweet spot,” Cross-Beard explains.

Go beyond ho-hum pots that stand sentry at your door. Look for large pieces with personality to anchor the entrance.

“Aged terracotta, weathered stone, or antique urns bring patina and character, helping even a newly built home feel established,” says MK Smith Boykin of Smith Interiors. “With that kind of presence, the planting itself can be quite simple.” The details are especially important when your home has architectural symmetry, like columns.

“Using matching plants only increases the allure of perfect visual balance,” Nesen adds. “The key is that the scale of the planters needs to match the proportions of the door and porch: Selecting pots or containers that are too small will immediately drain the drama and beauty of the front door statement.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6824. The Waterfall Bob Is Autumn’s Most Elegant Hair Trend

Beachy waves are made softer and more polished for the colder seasons.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Glance back at the year so far, and you’ll notice that each season claimed a new bob trend as its own. If spring’s defining crop was the modern graduated bob and summer saw the Riviera bob reign supreme, naturally, applications are open for autumn’s iteration.

Although the likes of Halle Berry and Léa Seydoux have hinted at the return of the French bob in recent weeks, it’s only now, in the aftermath of Paris Fashion Week, that a strong contender has emerged. The waterfall bob was seen not only on the front row (with Naomi Watts taking center stage) but among attendees. Soft, polished, and infinitely versatile, it makes a compelling case for fall 2026’s most elegant crop.

Ahead, everything you need to know about the waterfall bob, and how to wear it now.

Effortlessly tousled, boho-inspired texture isn’t, in itself, new; for many of us, beachy waves are something of a staple for summer. But for the colder months, wavy styles are made subtler, more understated, and, importantly, more refined.

As the name suggests, “the waterfall bob is known for its relaxed, shiny, S-shaped bends, which offer a more polished finish that’s perfect for autumn,” says Laura Elliott, head stylist and educator at Neäl & Wølf. Unlike the classic blunt bob—which is typically a one-length cut with sharp, straight ends—she notes that the “waterfall” effect comes from “soft, cascading layers which create volume.”

Waterfall (blended) layering is essential to achieving movement that appears to flow seamlessly throughout the lengths—and shorter face-framing pieces can also be added. Though, Elliott adds that while the bob uses strategic cutting techniques, the result shouldn’t appear over-styled.

“The trick is to avoid anything too choppy or heavily layered as this can take away from the softness of the cut. You want the layers to fall naturally into one another, creating that waterfall effect when the hair moves. I’d also ask your stylist to keep some weight through the ends, so the bob looks full and healthy rather than too wispy.”

As for length, references for the waterfall bob vary. It’s versatile enough to be tailored to every preference, whether sitting shorter, toward the jaw, or as a midi or longer bob which grazes the collarbones.

According to Elliott, many choose to opt for a bob (or a shorter cut, in general) at the turn of a new season. “Particularly after summer, people look to refresh their hairstyles for the colder months. Not only does it provide a transformation, but it’s also a great way to get rid of any dry, damaged ends caused by summer heat damage while giving hair a healthier, fuller appearance.”

The waterfall bob adds not only versatility but an effortless appeal to the mix, she continues. “Because the layers are soft and blended, the style grows out quite naturally, meaning you don’t need to worry about keeping a perfectly sharp, blunt shape.” That said, regular maintenance is still a consideration; she recommends booking a haircut every six to eight weeks to maintain healthy-looking ends.

For those with colored hair, the waterfall style is also a clever way to enhance the rich brunette shades that often gain popularity throughout autumn; Elliott highlights how chocolate browns and espresso tones can look deeper and more luxurious as the layers catch the light.

Since the waterfall bob is designed to look lived-in and intentionally undone, those with existing texture and naturally wavy hair make a perfect pairing. But it can be adapted to suit almost all hair types, says Elliott.

Those with straight hair can make use of simple styling techniques to create waves (see below), while thick hair types can benefit from the layering, which removes internal weight.

“For those with fine hair, it’s a great way to create the illusion of volume; however, I’d recommend keeping the layers quite minimal,” she warns. “Too many short layers can make fine hair appear thinner, so keeping a fuller base with a few face-framing pieces will help maintain thickness while creating movement.”

If you have wavy hair, often all that’s required is applying a small amount of curl cream to damp hair, before letting it air dry to form its natural texture. Or, Elliott suggests using a diffuser if preferred to encourage waterfall-like waves.

On straight hair, “use a medium-sized round brush when blow-drying to create lift at the roots and gently flick the ends away from the face,” she explains. “Then use a large-barreled curling wand to add a few loose waves throughout the mid-lengths, brushing them out with a wide-toothed comb for a more lived-in finish.”

Adding shine is optional, but recommended to complete the polished look. “This works particularly well on brunette shades during autumn and winter, as the added shine helps enhance the richness and dimension of darker tones, giving the hair that healthy, expensive-looking finish,” Elliott concludes.

#6823. Is Your Home Too Matchy-Matchy? These 5 Designer Tricks Break Up the Blah and Add Instant Character

If your bedroom design began with a furniture set that matches your nightstands and lamps, it may be time to shake things up. Having a space where everything coordinates perfectly may seem like the easiest route to a stylish home, but many times, it can feel more sterile than a true reflection of who you are and what you love.

“A home often looks sterile when everything comes from the same set,” explains Preeti Shah, founder and principal designer at Spotlight Style.

By stepping out of your comfort zone and switching things up a bit, you can create a cohesive look that still brings character and depth to your home. Here are five designer tips to steer you in the right direction.

Just because one of your surfaces is natural white oak doesn't mean everything in the same room needs to follow suit.

“We love mixing dark wood tones with light tones and everything in between,” says Leah Hook, founder and principal designer at Gray Oak Studio.

Repeating wood tones can make a room feel cohesive, but you don't need to use the same tone everywhere. Think outside the box and look for subtle ways to tie the space together, such as repeating a wood tone in an art frame or decorative tray.

Believe it or not, your island does not have to match your kitchen countertops in every possible way.

“Choose a more exciting stone for your island. You can even miter it two inches thick instead of keeping everything the standard 1.25-inch thickness,” explains Gaelle Dudley, founder and creative director at GLDESIGN.

Dining chairs are almost always purchased as a matching set, but sourcing different styles from flea markets or Facebook Marketplace can give your dining room a fun, unexpected look.

“Select just one element from the table that will be repeated in the chairs, and go with something from a different collection or even an entirely different furniture line,” says Pattie Kelly, certified interior decorator and owner of Inspired Home Interiors.

Consider the height of the chairs (so that they still make sense together at the table) and how much room you have to pull them out in (so you’re not running into the wall behind them).

The most interesting homes display a mix of different art styles. Instead of sticking to one color palette or aesthetic, seek out pieces that contrast. For instance, Shah is a fan of contrasting graphic black-and-white drawings with gold-veined abstract canvases in various rooms of the home.

Speaking of artwork, as the space pictured above by Nina Garbiras illustrates, don’t be afraid to mix and match your frame materials. Just keep one element consistent, like the mat size or the frame width, so that it still has a curated (not chaotic) feel.

Just because you’re choosing one type of fixture finish doesn’t mean you have to repeat it everywhere. In fact, your space will look more “expensive” if you match shiny finishes very minimally.

For example, the above space by Vestige Home showcases how well brass and matte-black hardware can play off each other, adding depth, harmony, and visual interest. Choosing to go with one finish throughout this space would’ve become too matchy-matchy very quickly.

#6820. The Best Skin Care Products of 2026, According to ELLE Editors

We tried dozens of new launches to find our Future of Beauty award winners.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

New skin care products are everywhere—the minute you’ve finished trying the latest serum or eye cream, four more just like it seem to pop up in its place. That’s why we enlisted dozens of testers to find the best of this year’s skin care launches. We narrowed down 320 submissions to the 90 most innovative to test—and after vigorously trialing, we can confidently recommend 28, including a cream that helps extend the effects of Botox, a foamy serum packed with exosomes, and a liquid pimple patch. Keep reading for the game-changing Future of Beauty award winners worth adding to your skin care routine.

Bloomeffects Black Tulip RegeneLift Eye Serum

This eye serum may be lightweight, but thanks to ingredients like peptides and bakuchiol, our tester said it instantly smooths and hydrates, leaving the eye area brighter and more lifted. “My favorite part is the metal applicator. It feels cool on the skin, which is perfect for tired mornings,” she said. Another tester loved the milky texture, noting that it absorbed quickly without pilling under makeup.

Dr. Dennis Gross DRx BioRejuvenation Serum

Powered by a precise ratio of amino acids commonly found in collagen, this serum is designed to help firm, tighten, and visibly lift. Our tester applied the serum around her mouth and noticed that area looked progressively smoother over time. She said the silky texture and pleasant scent were an added bonus, making the twice-daily ritual feel a little more indulgent.

Dr. Diamond’s Metacine Liplift Hyaluron Peptide-6 BioFill Lip Treatment

Inspired by the in-office treatments that made Jason Diamond, MD, a favorite among A-listers like Kim Kardashian and Katy Perry, this lifting serum is designed to deliver fuller, smoother lips without the sting of traditional lip plumpers. The peptide-powered formula helps hydrate, soften fine lines, and enhance definition. Our tester said, “It has a lightweight, silky texture that makes it easy to wear on its own or layered over lip color.”

Dr.Reju-All Advanced PDRN Rejuvenating Lip Serum

Your answer to kissably soft, smooth, and supple lips arrives in this rich formula that looks like a gloss, with ingredients that read like your favorite facial serum: skin-barrier-strengthening PDRN, plus lactic acid and hibiscus enzymes for gentle exfoliation. “I love that the formula looks like a regular gloss, even though it’s repairing and soothing my lips underneath the sheen,” our tester shared.

Exoceuticals Exo Elevate Skin Longevity Complex Moisturizing Cream

Instead of a surplus of single-purpose formulas, what your skin needs is a moisturizer that treats everything effectively. Here it is: a formula that combines exosomes, peptides, fatty acids, and more to restore, strengthen, and balance skin. Our tester said: “After a week of using this, I started to really notice an improvement in some of my lines and the overall firmness of my skin. I definitely haven’t used a moisturizer before that had so many active ingredients.”

#6818. Here’s What It Takes to Keep a Pool Running During the Winter, According to Experts

Before you dive in, it’s important to understand the risks and expenses involved.

When most people think of backyard swimming pools, relaxing outdoors in the summertime immediately comes to mind. But some homeowners don’t want the swimming season to end when temperatures drop. Fans of cold-water swimming are starting to consider ways to enjoy their pools throughout the winter as a part of an at-home wellness experience.

“Ten years ago, most people didn’t think winter swims were possible, especially in colder climates,” says Karen Larson, cofounder of Soake Pools. “Times have changed because these days, they’re seeing neighbors, friends, and others do it and post the proof on social media.”

The increasing interest in cold-water experiences is part of a broader wellness trend. In American College of Sports Medicine's 2026 Worldwide Fitness Trends report, cold therapies, including cold plunges, cryotherapy, and cold showers, earned the 17th spot among fitness trends for the year, up from number 20 in its 2025 report.

If you’ve previously closed your swimming pool around Labor Day but are interested in making cold-water swimming a part of your daily wellness routine, there’s a lot to keep in mind. Not every existing pool is a good candidate for keeping open for winter swimming, and it’s important to understand the risks and expenses involved. But if you’re curious, here’s what to consider about keeping a pool open year-round.

Why is cold-water swimming becoming more common? There are numerous purported mental and wellness benefits, from athletic recovery to stress relief and better sleep. A 2025 systematic review and meta-analysis published in PLOS ONE found that cold-water immersion led to a reduction in stress for 12 hours afterward as well as improvements in sleep quality and overall quality of life.

“Winter swimming often creates a time where friends and family can come together without distractions,” adds Larson.

That said, while swimming during the colder months has its perks, there are also several risks to keep in mind. A 2020 review in the International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health found that cold-water immersion can trigger an immediate shock response, leading to involuntary gasping, hyperventilation, and a rapid increase in heart rate and blood pressure. These reactions can make it difficult to control your breathing and increase your risk of drowning. Cold temps can also impair your muscle strength and nerve function, making it challenging to swim safely.

It’s a good idea to acclimate to the cold water gradually and avoid prolonged exposure. You’ll also want to make sure there’s someone else home while you take your cold-water plunge in the pool.

In addition, if you have underlying conditions like heart disease, consult your doctor before all else, as the cold water may place additional strain on your heart.

If you’re trying to consider whether your pool is a good candidate to keep open during the colder months, these are the things to look into.

The cost of keeping a swimming pool open for winter swimming will depend on your location, size, desired water temperature, and the type of heater you use.

“Routine pool maintenance generally remains about the same year-round, although chemical consumption may decrease depending on how frequently the pool is used,” explains Jim Eisch, Jr., vice president of operations at Pinch A Penny Pool Patio Spa. “The biggest expense to consider in the winter is heating the pool.”

For this reason, prospective winter swimmers may want to consider an upgrade to their heating system. Instead of using pricey natural gas heating or a traditional heat pump, Eisch says that inverter technology is one of the most affordable ways to maintain a swimmable pool temperature year-round.

For example, if you live in Florida and have a 10,000-gallon swimming pool, Eisch estimates your annual heating costs will be $2,467 with a natural gas heater, $741 with a traditional heat pump, and $434 for a heat/cool pump that uses inverter technology.

As aforementioned, the goal with wintertime swimming is not to be soaking in bathwater-like temperatures while it’s freezing outside. For safety purposes, winter swims are quick, and the heating system is to keep your chlorinated pool water well above the 19-degree Fahrenheit freezing point.

Unsurprisingly, a standard 14-by-28-foot swimming pool is going to be one of the hardest to use effectively year-round, unless you live in a region with a mild wintertime climate.

“You can technically use a large pool year-round, but it’s difficult to cover all its surface area well enough to hold in the heat, so heating bills can climb fast,” says Larson.

Therefore, smaller sizes like a 10-by-16-foot cocktail pool or plunge pool are better candidates for keeping open. Dedicated winter swimmers should also invest in an easy on-and-off cover—or a motorized auto cover—for added protection.

Regular pool cleaning and maintenance are essential, and this upkeep over the winter is no exception.

“Check the filtration system regularly to make sure it’s circulating smoothly, monitor the water chemistry, and plan for power outages—because there’s a good chance they will happen,” Larson explains. For this reason, you should also invest in a generator that can keep the pump running if the power goes out.

Safe pool access is just as important as any other aspect of the pool’s design. That’s why a nonslip surface, excellent drainage, and ample lighting are key.

“Your design should also provide a clear path from the house to the water,” says Neha Gupta, interior designer and founder at NU Curations.

Especially in the wintertime when ice and snow are a factor in your region, make sure you have a safe and smooth exit plan for getting out of the water, bundled up, and into your warm home without any obstacles. And then, of course, remember to cover the pool back up to keep it protected and ready for your next plunge.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

This essence, inspired by K-beauty toners for the face, is watery in feel. Our tester was a convert: “I hate applying heavy lotions after showering, but this felt like it extended my shower in the best way.”

Like a scented lip balm for the body, this waxy solid scent is designed to glide over your throat, wrists, and anywhere else that you want to smell good. The scent notes include amber, cognac, and black sugar. “It’s so much less clunky than carrying around a spray bottle. I love that the scent is vegan, too,” said our tester.

Jinsoon Camellia Glow Renewing Peeling Balm

Massage this peel onto skin for just 30 seconds, and dead skin begins to accumulate like pills on a sweater. “It completely transformed my hands nearly instantly,” our tester said. “They were noticeably softer and smoother in less than a minute.” Made with cellulose fibers, which bind to dead skin and impurities to lift them off the surface, the peel can be used on any part of the body.

#6814. Rosie Huntington-Whiteley Goes Completely Backless in a Sculptural Gucci Dress

She made a statement at the 2026 LACMA Gala

At the 2026 LACMA Art+Film Gala, Rosie Huntington-Whiteley was all business in the front, party in the back. Courtesy of Gucci, the evening’s sponsor, the piece appeared simple and modest at first glance—with a front defined by a sculptural, exaggerated high neckline and a slim-fit long-sleeve, floor-length silhouette. When Huntington-Whiteley turned, however, the dress was revealed to be (almost) entirely open, with a singular piece of silver hardware creating separation between a low scoop back and a high leg slit.

She matched the shade to her diamond-and emerald earrings, which were on full display thanks to her slicked-back ponytail. She slipped into pointed-toe black pumps and stopped for photos in front of Chris Burden’s famed public art installation, Urban Light, which doubled as a red carpet for the evening.

The Art+Film Gala celebrated its 15th year tonight, supporting the museum’s ongoing efforts to expand its film programming. It honored the work of painter Vija Celmins, known for her photo-realistic work exploring the complexity of nature, as well as Dune filmmaker Denis Villeneuve. Blackpink’s Rosé was tapped for a musical performance, and, like every year, Leonardo DiCaprio and LACMA trustee Eva Chow served as co-chairs.

#6813. 10 Stylish Sofa Covers to Totally Refresh Your Living Room Before the Holidays

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Hide stains, protect upholstery, and add instant polish.

Life at home gets messy with its regular residents, and it gets even crazier when guests come over. Hosting season demands the most of our couches, as they become extra seats for family dinner or makeshift beds for overnight stays. It’s not always feasible to swap out your sofa just because the upholstery has seen better days. That’s where the mighty sofa cover comes in.

Whether you opt for a tightly-fitted silhouette or a looser, blanket-style slipcover, a high-quality sofa cover can totally refresh your space. Just think about it: a dated, yellowing couch suddenly looks fresh and brand new. You can even go bold and experiment with patterned options, without the commitment of buying a whole new piece. Pro tip: patterns are also a great way to hide stains.

There are hundreds of sofa cover options available, so below, our shopping team did the hard part for you. We hunted down nine options for different types of couches, including an editor-tested and approved option. Plus, we chatted with interior designer Tiffany Leigh for tips on styling sofa covers, keeping them clean, and more.

Velvet couches are having a moment, so if you want in on the trend but don't want to toss out your current sofa, enter this sumptuous sofa slipcover. The fabric is extra soft, so you feel like you're sitting on a high-end sofa at a fraction of the price. "Even better: it was so soft against my skin that I fell asleep on it during movie night more times than I'm proud of."

The size of your sofa cover will depend not only on the size of the sofa or couch you want to protect, but also on the properties of the fabric itself. Leigh says that with a stretchy fabric, you may want a cover with measurements slightly smaller than the dimensions of your sofa for a tighter, more fitted look. For sofa covers with corner ties that allow you to tighten or tie bows, the fabric may have a bit less give, so you'll want to allow for a little extra room in the cover size.

Some styles lend themselves to being covered more effectively than others. Leigh says that sofas with thinner or rolled arms tend to look nicer with a slipcover than those without. You can also choose between having a skirt on your sofa cover or showing off the legs of your furniture, depending on your aesthetic preference. “I like when they go right to the floor because a skirt cover is a much more traditional, covered look,” Leigh says. “If you have a little bit more of a modern style, having a slipcover that stops at the legs can be a fresher, kind of more updated take.”

Natural fabrics, such as chenille or linen, are typically a great choice when selecting a sofa cover fabric. For family rooms or homes with pets, indoor/outdoor fabrics like Crypton that have been treated to repel dirt and debris are safer bets for those wanting something stain-resistant. The stretchiness of the fabric also plays a role in selecting a sofa cover. "If you wanted something a little bit more durable, canvas or cotton would be a really great material on there," Leigh says.

It's always a good idea to pay attention to the care instructions for whatever cover you buy, as fabric care really depends on the individual material. For example, a heavier cotton material can likely withstand more washing. “I would say only wash when you really need to, just like with a really nice sweater,” Leigh says. “Don't wash it every time you wear it because washing breaks down fibers. So, it could reduce the longevity of your sofa cover. Most are designed to be removed and washed. So if and when you feel like 'oh, you know what, it's looking a little bit dingy, it's time,' that's the time to do it.” Additionally, it's essential to consider the size of your cover, as larger ones may necessitate the use of a commercial washing machine.

Additionally, the cover may wrinkle over time, especially with linen, which softens with each wash. “Linen does get a little bit wrinkly, but I think that's kind of the charm of a slipcovered sofa, having that kind of rumpled, you know, 'someone just took a nap here' look,” Leigh says.

“An oatmeal linen slipcover is that very quintessential, relaxed coastal style that you see a lot of slipcovers used in, and I think it's a classic choice that you'll never go wrong with,” Leigh says. Don't run away from patterns either, though—Leigh is seeing a rise in patterned upholstery in the design industry, and it's a chance to push the envelope and have fun with your design choices. “A cover can be a great way to, you know, try out a pattern without necessarily committing to it forever, because if in five years you say, ‘okay, this pattern was fun, but I'm ready for something different,’ you can just change the slipcover as opposed to having to get an all-new sofa,” Leigh says.

At House Beautiful, we are experts in reviewing and buying anything for the home, from mattresses for back pain to reclining sofas. For this story in particular, we asked our staff members to share their favorite sofa cover (if they own one) and spent hours combing the internet, reading hundreds of reviews about the best ones currently available on the market. While we haven't personally tested every product on this list, we've thoroughly outlined the pros and cons of each sofa cover to provide you with all the necessary details before you make a purchase.

For further insight into how to best place sofa covers in your home, we consulted interior designer Tiffany Leigh. Leigh runs her eponymous firm in Ontario, Canada, where her team helps clients bring their dream homes and cottages to life. Since 2019, they have designed approachable and livable interiors that feel welcoming and elevated, focusing on quality craftsmanship, emotional connection, and natural elements.

#6810. Nicole Kidman Celebrates the Art of Ballet in a Voluminous Chanel Tutu

She made her LACMA Gala debut in a messy high ponytail

Every year, the Los Angeles County Museum of Art welcomes the art, film, fashion, and entertainment industries to its annual fundraising event. Since its inception back in 2011, the Art+Film Gala, as it is known, has become a staple fixture on the fall calendar, seeing everyone from models to actors to philanthropists to musicians to athletes return time and time again. But this year, the starry festivities also saw a new face or two—including the one and only Nicole Kidman.

Making her LACMA debut, Kidman showed up to the event wearing a voluminous Chanel dress from Matthieu Blazy’s Spring/Summer 2027 collection. On top, the taupe-and-cream piece featured a long-sleeve corset bodice, with a raw-edge neckline that contrasted the structured boning below. From the hips, layers and layers of semi-sheer tulle cut at different lengths created a high-low tutu skirt that, even when still, gave the illusion of movement.

Before heading inside, the Babygirl actor—with a messy high ponytail and open-toe heels—stopped for photos by Chris Burden’s Urban Light art installation, which doubled as a red carpet for the evening. She was joined by the likes of Alex Consani, Kim Kardashian, and Paris Hilton.

LACMA’s annual gala was founded to support the museum’s efforts to expand its programming, making film more central to its exhibitions and education. It has placed a focus on the intersection of art and film, and takes a moment each year to celebrate individuals for their contributions to the visual and cinematic arts. Tonight, it honored the work of painter Vija Celmins, known for her photo-realistic explorations of nature, as well as Dune filmmaker Denis Villeneuve. Blackpink’s Rosé was tapped for a musical performance, and, for the 15th year, Leonardo DiCaprio and LACMA trustee Eva Chow served as co-chairs.

#6809. Kim Kardashian’s Beverly Hills Marital Villa Lists for $8.5 Million—Plus 4 More Luxury Homes for Sale

This week’s standout luxury listings include the Notting Hill blue-door townhouse, Diana Jenkins’s Hidden Hills mansion, and a Connecticut estate by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé.

Even if you keep up with the Kardashians, you might not recognize this Beverly Hills house, which was one of Kim Kardashian’s first real estate purchases. The reality star bought the gated home 16 years ago for $3.4 million, and it served as the setting when basketball player Kris Humphries proposed to her in 2011. But the residence has gotten a major overhaul in the years since Kardashian sold it following the short-lived marriage, and now the Tuscan-style villa is on the market for nearly $8.5 million.

Other listings on our radar this week include another reality TV–linked estate: A former Real Housewife of Beverly Hills is parting with her 13,200-square-foot Hidden Hills house. On the East Coast, a striking Connecticut home by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé is also on the market. And across the pond, the memorable blue-door townhouse from ‘90s rom-com Notting Hill in London is also for sale. Check them all out below.

One of Kim Kardashian’s earliest real estate purchases was this gated property in Beverly Hills, which she acquired in 2010 for $3.4 million. The Tuscan-style villa quickly became a familiar backdrop on her family’s E! reality show, Keeping Up with the Kardashians, and was even the site of Kris Humphries’s (televised) 2011 proposal to her. In the aftermath of their 72-day marriage, Kardashian sold the half-acre estate, and its new owners tapped fast-rising designer Sebastian Zuchowicki to oversee a complete overhaul. Now on the market for just under $8.5 million with Jack Harris and Michael Fahimian of Beverly Hills Estates, the five-bedroom home has a double-height entry foyer, custom kitchen cabinetry, and a primary suite with a fireplace, two walk-in closets, and a soaking tub.

Entrepreneur Diana Jenkins spent much of her time on The Real Housewives of Beverly Hills nesting in her sprawling Hidden Hills mansion with her newborn daughter, Eliyanah. Now, four years since leaving the Bravo reality series, the native Bosnian is returning to her home country, per Realtor.com—and listing the 13,200-square-foot mansion for $23 million with Sandeep Dhariwal of Sotheby’s International Realty—Beverly Hills Brokerage. Set on 4.4 acres, the seven-bedroom house was built in 2019 and comes with a plethora of perks, among them an infinity-edge pool, barbecue, fitness studio, and home cinema.

It’s perhaps the most famous house in one of London’s most charming neighborhoods, so beloved that crowds are known to gather around it like the English version of Carrie Bradshaw’s West Village apartment. The blue-doored townhouse where Hugh Grant’s fictional bookseller lived and wooed Julia Roberts’s paparazzi-plagued Hollywood actress in 1999’s Notting Hill is now on the market. And the 2,750-square-foot house can be yours for roughly $10 million.

With its organic silhouette, cantilevered roofline, and clever use of space, Leuthold House by Allan Gelbin bears the hallmarks of his mentor, legendary architect Frank Lloyd Wright. Completed in 1966 on five rolling acres in New Canaan, the five-bedroom house spans roughly 5,700 square feet and underwent a restoration by Hobbs Inc. and JMK Architects in 2014. The interiors feature long expanses of glass and mahogany, while the grounds come with a spring-fed pond, a pool, a one-bedroom guest house, and 150 specimen trees. Listed for $6.2 million with John Engel and Melissa Engel of Douglas Elliman, the two-story house is on the market for the first time in 29 years.

One of the art world’s most famous couples once lived and worked in a gated townhouse a block from Washington Square Park. Jackson Pollock and Lee Krasner didn’t own the MacDougal Alley home—a brick-faced carriage house built in the 1870s—but rather stayed there in the winter of 1950 due to the generosity of its owner, a fellow celebrated artist and their friend Alfonso Ossorio. Now, Ossorio’s 1,500-square-foot residence is on the market for the first time since 1988, per the New York Post. The asking price for the two-bedroom, two-bath home is nearly $8 million.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.
