I applied the chubby pencil against the natural direction of hair growth, then used the spoolie on the opposite end to shape the brows. It created a thick, natural-looking brow with a fluffy feathered effect that was even better than a tint because of the added texture.”

Laura Geller Anti-Hot Flash Setting Spray

Whether it’s because of a sweaty day or a hot flash, this spray keeps makeup in place for up to 18 hours and gives a light matte finish that doesn’t leave your skin feeling Saran-wrapped. Our tester said, “This is worth it because it truly keeps my makeup in place after a humid commute to work, all day in the office, and even throughout the night. It is light, non-greasy, and one spray goes a long way.”

It’s not a cream; it’s not a powder; it’s a cream-owder? Whatever it is, our tester liked it, marveling over this blush’s initial mousse cream texture that transforms with a light touch into a pigmented powder. “The product is great because it’s super pigmented but buildable. It blended right into my skin and gave me a slightly sun-kissed look. The texture makes it different than any other blushes I’ve tried.”

Many eye shadows tend to crease or fade after a few hours. With skin care ingredients like hyaluronic acid and ceramides, along with jojoba esters to help with hold, this one combines longwear power with a luxurious texture. “This was a super pigmented formula. The texture is velvety, which is really nice for an eyeshadow.”

Tilt Beauty The Easy Way Hydrating Satin Lipstick

An oversized grip and ergonomic design won this lipstick a “Certified Ease of Use” designation from the Arthritis Foundation. On top of that, the formula is silky and pigmented. Our tester called it a great lipstick with lasting color: “It’s super easy to use, glides smoothly onto the lips, and best of all, the top is magnetic and snaps on easily—especially convenient when you’re on the go.”

Not merely a foundation, this product melts into the skin like a serum and offers some additional protection benefits too, by being noncomedogenic and non-acnegenic. It wards off bacteria and breakouts with purslane and lactobacillus ferment. “When applying the rest of my makeup routine over top, the foundation not only remained fresh but did not formulaically clash with anything else I used. It doesn’t leave the skin oily, but just slightly glowy, and lasted well into the evening,” our tester said.

Urban Decay Lip Toy Staining Brush Lip Liner

The product compelled our tester to announce her love in all caps. It looks like a regular lip liner but also imparts a pretty, enduring stain. She did note that the thin brush applicator takes a little time to get the hang of, but after you do, it “stays the whole night!”

Victoria Beckham Colour Wash Bronze Water Tint

Steer clear of the typical pitfalls of bronzer with this product, which goes on like a K-beauty essence. Dot it along the areas of the face that you want to look a little more sun-kissed and watch it deliver a pretty, non-orange warmth. Our tester said, “It left me with a true ‘just left the beach’ shade of a tan all day long and blended very easily into my skin.”

Westman Atelier Suprême Bio-Complex Liquid Foundation

This just might be the first foundation formula with exosomes and peptides for skin renewal, in addition to amino-coated powders that replace skin-clogging silicones. Our tester went in skeptical and came out a convert. “I’ve eschewed foundation for most of my adult life. I’ve also struggled to find a match for my pale skin that didn’t look either fake-tan or ghostly. This was such a nice surprise! It was a great color match and smoothed out my freckly, uneven skin tone without settling into laugh lines. And it was so buildable—even after two applications, I still looked like me, rather than me in a mask.”

Fenty Beauty by Rihanna Pro Filt’r Fluid Flex Natural Matte Longwear Foundation

With even more diverse skin tones, specifically more neutral shades, this formula also looks more skin-like and less matte. After taking it on a trial run, our tester simply said, “In Fenty we trust.”

#6805. This Breezy Los Angeles House Has “a Little Funk to It”

Designer Mike Moser creates a Santa Monica home that spans decades, styles, and influences—without sacrificing fun.

#6804. This 100-Year-Old Louisiana Home Got a New Plot of Land and a Colorful New Chapter

The expansive property also includes a pool house and carriage house.

Long before it became the backdrop for family dinners, afternoon swims, and spirited tennis matches, this century-old Colonial in Baton Rouge, Louisiana, had already lived a rather extraordinary life. In the late 1920s, the house was dismantled and relocated from Prairieville to its current address, where its distinctive red clay roof tiles—salvaged from Louisiana State University—have presided over a landscape of majestic live oaks ever since. Decades later, a young girl growing up down the street would admire the house, never imagining that one day she’d raise a family of her own beneath that very roof.

When she and her husband finally purchased the property, they knew that turning a beloved neighborhood landmark into a forever home for themselves and their three daughters would require a little imagination—and a lot of careful planning. Enter interior designer Anne Underwood, who was tasked with bringing the historic residence into the present while preserving the quirks that made it so beloved. Over several years, the project grew from a renovation of the original four-bedroom home into a sprawling family compound, complete with a carriage house, pool house, and landscaped grounds designed to make an ordinary weekend feel like a vacation.

Inside, Underwood embraced the home’s traditional bones without letting them stiffen the home (and the family’s) sunny disposition. Lemon-yellow checkerboard floors mingle with antique furnishings (an element Underwood says is a must for creating “a lived-in, collected-over-time feel that I find crucial to making a true home”), hand-painted finishes, and exuberant textiles. A whimsical chandelier welcomes guests into the living room, while a tucked-away banquette has become an unofficial headquarters for homework, puzzles, and art projects.

Outside, the property’s newest additions—including a pool house and carriage house—offer an equally playful proposition. Conceived as the estate’s crown jewel, the pool house features pale wood walls, a colorful handwoven light fixture, and a monumental wood-burning pizza oven. The homeowners’ relatives jokingly dubbed the expanding property their own private resort, a description Underwood happily embraces.

“So much was poured into this project to make it unique, joyful, and really functional for the clients,” says Underwood. “The warmth and care of each detailed layer creates a sense of ease, which is what facilitates the joyfulness that plays out inside and outside these walls.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne UnderwoodArchitect: LRK ArchitectsContractor: Bernhardt Normand ConstructionStyling: Lucy BammanLandscape Architect: CARBOLocation: Baton Rouge, LouisianaThe Space: A four-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom main house, with a carriage house and pool house.

With a simpler architectural structure, the pool house was a place where Underwood leaned into the family’s playful energy. “We selected the wall stain to look like the inside of a shell and wanted the furnishings to feel layered, colorful, vibrant—like a vacation but also very durable for wet swimsuits and sweaty tennis players!” she adds.

The first selection for the pool house was also its most exuberant: a 12-light PET Lamp fixture, with each colorful pendant and cord individually customized and positioned beneath the soaring vaulted ceiling. “Its colorful, playful nature was exactly what we wanted and is very effective for balancing light throughout the space with high vaulted ceilings,” says Underwood. Below, a generous sectional upholstered in indoor-outdoor fabric creates an inviting place to retreat after a swim.

With vibrant Moroccan mosaic flooring, blue zellige shower tile, and a vintage wooden mirror, the pool house bathroom could easily belong to a boutique hotel on some far-flung island. Wood siding and thoughtfully chosen antique details lend the space a relaxed, resort-inspired character that feels right at home beside the pool.

Tradition gets a playful twist in the home’s primary gathering space, where a hand-knotted rug and classic upholstered furnishings mingle with an exuberant chandelier by Stray Dog Designs.

“This house lacks a foyer, so we wanted the living room to be welcoming with plenty of seating and layers of color and accessories,” says Underwood. “While this is the more ‘formal’ living space, we selected a bright, whimsical light fixture to let guests know that these homeowners don’t take themselves (or their interiors) too seriously.”

A passageway between the living room and the home’s more casual quarters, the dining room needed to make an impression without feeling overly precious. Schumacher’s Weeping Pine wallpaper wraps the space in pattern, while pale blue trim, upholstered chairs, and vintage sconces sourced from Round Top lend the formal setting an unexpected sense of ease.

With the adjoining breakfast room to consider, Underwood gave the kitchen a quieter disposition. Existing cabinetry received a custom pale green stain, expertly applied by a local faux-finish artist, while paneled appliances and tonal counter stools create a seamless effect. The yellow-and-white checkerboard flooring continues underfoot, connecting the two spaces.

A powder room is as good a place as any to indulge a dramatic streak. “We wanted a bold escape for guests in the powder bath, and this incredible grasscloth wallpaper was the jumping-off point,” says Underwood. She complemented the Aux Abris pattern’s lush, atmospheric tone with a wood-grained antique chest repurposed as a marble-topped vanity. Vintage Murano glass sconces and a striped inlay mirror add a final dose of eccentricity.

In the office, Underwood leaned into the appeal of a well-appointed study, furnishing this hardworking space almost entirely with vintage and antique finds. Custom cabinetry painted in Benjamin Moore’s Templeton Gray conceals printers, files, and other less-than-picturesque necessities, while mohair-upholstered armchairs make the space equally suited to work and contemplation.

As it turns out, the inspiration for the primary suite was waiting just outside the window. “I started with the idea of bringing materials—like the patinated copper on all exterior lanterns and gutters—from the exterior to the interior,” says Underwood. “While the walls are painted a green/blue color reminiscent of the patina of weathered copper, the accents in the room are warm shades of camel and copper.”

A Gustavian antique secretary and dimensional papier-maché art from Gusto complete the room's quietly collected atmosphere.

The generously proportioned soaking tub in the primary bathroom has already lived several lives, first as a favorite bathing spot for the family’s three young daughters, and now as a welcome retreat for their parents. Underwood carried the bedroom’s blue-green palette into the bath, introducing blush-patterned wallpaper, graphic stone tile, and black accents to balance the room’s softer flourishes.

In daughter Emily’s room, Underwood chose to embrace the sloped ceiling, outfitting it with custom bed canopies that create an enveloping, almost storybook atmosphere. Layers of patterned bedding, including bespoke eiderdowns made in the UK, heighten the cozy effect, while chunky upholstered ottomans offer a welcome counterpoint to the room’s more feminine details.

Making sense of the home’s quirky upstairs architecture required some clever spatial maneuvering. Underwood divided one large bathroom into two separate spaces, each with its own dressing vanity. In daughter Olivia’s bath, Salvesen Graham floral wallpaper covers the sloped walls and ceiling, visually softening the room’s unusual angles. “The pattern play in the tile, wallpaper, and fabrics keeps the energy moving throughout the space,” she adds.

In the carriage house, Underwood continued the layered approach with velvet, chenille, and a generously proportioned sectional, creating a retreat where overnight guests can comfortably settle in for an afternoon—or an entire weekend.

The carriage house kitchen offers guests all the comforts of home, with a nostalgic twist. Cabinetry painted Benjamin Moore’s Mistletoe features vintage-inspired vent detailing, while a retro SMEG refrigerator reinforces the room’s charming, slightly old-fashioned character.

Underwood preserved the home’s original red clay roof tiles while refreshing its facade using Benjamin Moore’s White Dove on the siding and Kennebunkport Green on the shutters. An expanded bluestone sidewalk gives the front door prominence while mature live oaks lend the property a sense of permanence that no renovation could replicate.

Anne Underwood is the founder of Underwood Interiors. Known for her thoughtful approach to creating layered, Anne creates livable spaces that balance timeless tradition with a fresh, playful sensibility. With an appreciation for antiques, vintage furnishings, and artisanal craftsmanship, her interiors feel collected over time rather than decorated all at once. Her work celebrates color, pattern, and architectural character, with an emphasis on thoughtful details and everyday functionality. Whether reimagining a historic residence or designing a new space from the ground up, she believes the most successful interiors reflect the personalities of the people who call them home.

#6801. The 83 Most Innovative Beauty Products of 2026

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As Mariah Carey once sang, “It’s time.” Each year, there are hundreds of new beauty products that promise to illuminate, brighten, slough, moisturize, hydrate, densify, and drench every inch of our bodies, from head to toe. But how good are they actually? And how special are they? Are the promises real? To find out, we put hundreds of products to the test.

After testing and combing through hundreds of entries for our 2026 Future of Beauty Awards, we found true innovators and standouts in skin care, hair care, body care, and color cosmetics, all rated highly by our editor testers. There are perfect 10s in every category, and we read many effusive reviews—from an NAD+ moisturizer that made someone a believer, to a top coat that made one tester’s nails glisten like diamonds, to one of the first sunscreens to use the new SPF filter, to a moisturizer meant to combat the effects of GLP-1s. See all 83 of our favorites below.

Bloomeffects Black Tulip RegeneLift Eye Serum

This eye serum may be lightweight, but thanks to ingredients like peptides and bakuchiol, our tester said it instantly smooths and hydrates, leaving the eye area brighter and more lifted. “My favorite part is the metal applicator. It feels cool on the skin, which is perfect for tired mornings,” she said. Another tester loved the milky texture, noting that it absorbed quickly without pilling under makeup.

Dr. Dennis Gross DRx BioRejuvenation Serum

Powered by a precise ratio of amino acids commonly found in collagen, this serum is designed to help firm, tighten, and visibly lift. Our tester applied the serum around her mouth and noticed that area looked progressively smoother over time. She said the silky texture and pleasant scent were an added bonus, making the twice-daily ritual feel a little more indulgent.

Dr. Diamond’s Metacine Liplift Hyaluron Peptide-6 BioFill Lip Treatment

Inspired by the in-office treatments that made Jason Diamond, MD, a favorite among A-listers like Kim Kardashian and Katy Perry, this lifting serum is designed to deliver fuller, smoother lips without the sting of traditional lip plumpers. The peptide-powered formula helps hydrate, soften fine lines, and enhance definition. Our tester said, “It has a lightweight, silky texture that makes it easy to wear on its own or layered over lip color.”

Dr.Reju-All Advanced PDRN Rejuvenating Lip Serum

Your answer to kissably soft, smooth, and supple lips arrives in this rich formula that looks like a gloss, with ingredients that read like your favorite facial serum: skin-barrier-strengthening PDRN, plus lactic acid and hibiscus enzymes for gentle exfoliation. “I love that the formula looks like a regular gloss, even though it’s repairing and soothing my lips underneath the sheen,” our tester shared.

Exoceuticals Exo Elevate Skin Longevity Complex Moisturizing Cream

Instead of a surplus of single-purpose formulas, what your skin needs is a moisturizer that treats everything effectively. Here it is: a formula that combines exosomes, peptides, fatty acids, and more to restore, strengthen, and balance skin. Our tester said: “After a week of using this, I started to really notice an improvement in some of my lines and the overall firmness of my skin. I definitely haven’t used a moisturizer before that had so many active ingredients.”

Expanding its Soy Cleansing empire, Fresh’s new balm has a texture that starts as jelly, becomes an oil, and then liquefies as you rinse it off. Soy protein is the star ingredient, adding moisture and boosting elasticity. Our tester felt that “the jelly did a great job of cleansing and removing my foundation while leaving it feeling clean and fresh, but not dry.” She also liked the tub format, noting, “I use a lot of facial cleansing oils, and this format felt innovative and easy to transport, which is not always the case with oils.”

“Gwyneth, I was not familiar with your game!” our tester raved. “Before testing this product, I read that it would lessen the appearance of my fine lines within a month. I was skeptical, but it really works!” This cream contains plant peptides and a precursor to NAD+, a powerful coenzyme that many celebrities use to biohack, as it purportedly helps stimulate mitochondria. “I have tried so many face creams over the years, but this is the only cream I think has actually made a difference.”

“My under-eyes looked about 20 percent brighter as a result of using the product,” enthused our tester after just a week of consistent use. The silver applicator dispenses one cool dose of two different types of vitamin C and hyaluronic acid to smooth, moisturize, and deliver radiance to the under-eye area.

La Roche-Posay Hyalu B5 Suractivated Rich Cream Jar

“Replumping” power is one of the most intriguing aspects of this moisturizer. In a study of GLP-1 users who used both this cream and its sister serum, they reported smoother and more radiant skin, perhaps thanks to the combination of multiple hyaluronic acids and ectoin, an ingredient that prevents water loss. Our tester said, “I liked using this cream first thing in the morning. It absorbs right away and provides quick moisture, which I found worked well under makeup.”

Manyo Glutathione 7 Dark Spot Serum Patch

Going beyond an under-eye or even a pimple patch, this is a patch—shaped like clouds, hearts, or stars, natch—that can go anywhere on your face to hydrate and reduce dark spots using glutathione and niacinamide. Our tester said, “My under-eyes are slightly less dark. But I enjoyed the cooling effect of these more than anything.”

People sometimes associate luxe face balms with granola-ish or sensuous formulations, rather than real science. But this one corrals powerful ingredients like vitamin C, resveratrol, and fisetin, a flavonoid that recent studies have shown can help clear out aged or dormant fibroblasts. “I really liked the product. I expected it to seize up or react strangely with my melange of other products, but in fact it seemed to lock them in to help them better absorb. It actually made my already oil-slicked skin feel less greasy. The next day, my naturally dry skin felt and looked hydrated.”

Medik8 Exo-PDRN Prismatic+ Vegan PDRN Serum With Triple Exosome Complex

PDRN and exosome treatments are both popular in Korea, and this serum is a vegan-friendly version of them, aimed at helping rejuvenate and moisturize skin. “I got a lot of sun over the summer, and this has helped bring my skin back to life! It has made it plumper and super hydrated,” our tester said.

For those who prefer their pimple patches on the subtle side, the reigning patch brand has developed a new invisible bandage. Our tester is a frequent pimple patch wearer and liked that this clear version discouraged skin picking and sealed out bacteria, so that her zits could flatten in just a few hours.

Olay Regenerist Lifting and Sculpting Face and Neck Treatment

A face sculptor and moisturizer in one, this rollerball dispenses a mix of collagen peptides and NAD+. The cooling applicator allows you to glide it across your neck, décolletage, and the lymph nodes for a sculpting effect. “I primarily used this on my neck and jawline and areas I don’t pay enough attention to. My skin looked plumper and smoother after use,” our tester said.

Like a shot of espresso for your face, this tingling cream mask has caffeine to depuff and peptides to add radiance. “The Caffeine Reset definitely feels like an elevated addition to my routine,” said our tester. “The formula felt cooling and comfortable on my sensitive skin, and I loved the packaging. The depuffing results weren’t dramatic for me, but I loved how it felt and think it’s the kind of product I’d especially reach for after a late night or early morning.”

This botanical-infused mineral sunscreen has an SPF-plus formula that visibly calms redness while protecting the skin. Our tester did want to flag that this is a capital-M mineral sunscreen, meaning that it has a thicker texture and requires a bit of rubbing. But it’s worth it, she said, because it provides sun protection that lasts longer than the formulas she usually uses.

Is it possible for a sunscreen to make you look glowy without being greasy? As it turns out, yes. This one comes in two shades and has a natural finish. Every swipe of this hydrating sunscreen gives skin a cast-free finish and has a water-burst-like texture, according to our tester, who loved how moisturizing it felt.

Dior Prestige La Crème Texture Essentielle

A combination of powerful, rare botanicals and a new “micro-filler” technology makes this cream especially luxurious. It contains peptides from Dior’s proprietary Rose de Granville, a hardy, resilient variety grown on the French cliffs. The brand’s micro-filler technology also glosses over texture and fine lines so that skin looks extra smooth. “Putting this on my chest made a weird shoulder crinkle disappear, so I can only imagine the possibilities that it could have for my face,” our tester said.

You may know Upneeq for its prescription eye drops that help open low-lying lids. Now, it has also made an over-the-counter eye cream to firm and brighten with vitamin C and peptides (though it does not help your eyes appear more open). “I love the way this cream feels going on,” our tester raved. “It’s so smooth, like butter, and absorbs easily.”

Our tester couldn’t stop marveling at how good her skin looked after using this cream. “It’s smooth and firmer, and I really see fewer fine lines. But my favorite thing about this cream is the way it melts in—I feel like I am taking a watering can to my face.” The formula is intensely hydrating, but its real breakthrough is that it’s made with Myrixin, a flavonoid complex with six different plant extracts that boasts Botox-like effects and helps smooth deep-set wrinkles.

Skorr Skin care Revive Exosome EGF Booster

SeneGence GLP-Complex with SenePlex+ and Exosomes

Biodance Caviar PDRN Liposome Bubble Booster

It’s easy to use and on top of that, it smells great and rinses out well. It’s also lightweight and can be applied generously without feeling oversaturated. It feels like exactly what my hair needs and something I could get addicted to using.” Apply it before you shampoo to repair damage and split ends thanks to the brand’s signature bond-building technology, which helps rebuild damaged hair from the inside out.

If you’re in the market for a slick-back aid that doesn’t leave a greasy film (or require multiple showers to get out), this stick is what you’ve been looking for. “I was really impressed with how little residue it left and the immediate result,” our tester said. She used it on a bun during a humid day and felt that it added shine, nixed flyaways, and kept her hair in place. It contains shea and mkani butters to seal in moisture, while cranberry extract and oil help smooth and add shine.

Don’t let the word “balm” trip you up—this is an in-shower mask that you leave in for three minutes after shampooing, in lieu of conditioner. Our tester worried it could be greasy, but she was pleasantly surprised: “My hair was quickly silky, shiny, and less frizzy.” She thought it worked better than her typical conditioner, with a bonus that it “smells amazing.”

I’ve been using this shampoo twice a week and my hair feels very clean and free of build-up. I love the scent, too. Even my hair dresser remarked on how shiny my hair is right now.”

Imagine a product that works so well coworkers start to comment on how good your hair looks lately. That’s what happened to our tester when she started adding this to her routine: “My hair appears healthier and softer, with people at work even asking what I’ve been using. My ends are a lot less dry.” It makes sense, since the acidic bonding complex works to seal split ends in your hair.

#6800. Why Wallpaper Is the Secret to Making Even the Smallest Bathroom Feel Special

A little pattern can turn into a true design moment.

When you’re working with limited space, it’s almost instinctive to tone down your design details to avoid feeling overwhelmed—but even small spaces need character. Our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston is a master class in excellent design, no matter the room size or purpose. This year’s top recommendation for a chic bath is wallcoverings.

When installed properly, wallcoverings are one of the most reliable ways to add an extra touch of flair to interiors. Here’s how the designers of our 2026 Whole Home used them to bring big personality to small spaces.

For many designers, powder rooms are the perfect opportunity to play. Designer Mark Kennamer, founder and principal designer of his eponymous Birmingham, Alabama–based firm, used wallcoverings to add bespoke character to a nearly monochromatic powder-pink scheme. In the guest bath, the Zoffany mauve moiré wallcovering lends a subtle shimmer and dimension, while its wood-like grain adds an unexpected, welcome layer of texture.

Anissa Zajac of House Seven Design outfitted the primary bathroom with a botanical-printed wallcovering by Sanderson that incorporated nearly every hue in her palette while adding a dose of whimsy. The pattern’s vertical lines play off the texture of the fluted vanity cabinets, making a bold statement without straying too far from the design scheme.

In the gallery bath, the team at R&R Interior Design 365 took inspiration from Charleston’s famed cobblestone roads. They opted for a blue-brick wallpaper by Perennials to channel the perfect level of city flair while keeping the feel warm, intimate, and inviting.

#6796. Melitta Baumeister Is the Designer Keeping New York Weird

The bold designer is braving the New York fashion landscape with unique shapes and high concepts.

Only at a Melitta Baumeister presentation in New York would you find the artist Kembra Pfahler playing a tuba, Paola Castro doing backflips, and Colleen Litchfield sculpting clay on the floor with sneakers gliding by on motorized platforms with chunky tires. “I really loved the idea of just doing flips in your living room,” the designer tells ELLE of her event last month. Sitting in her Greenpoint atelier in Brooklyn, Baumeister is wearing one of her signature black pointy baseball caps covered in pleated fabric. It’s become such a trademark for her that the people who know her best barely recognize her without it.

But that’s not all she’s known for. Since launching her own fashion line over a decade ago, Lady Gaga and Rihanna have worn her work, and the label has become a favorite of fashion insiders, with over-the-top conceptual shows, directional collections sold at places like Dover Street Market, and sporty collaborations. Her latest foray is a second collaboration with Nike, which involved the aforementioned performance-art-centric presentation to launch her rework of the Tabi-silhouetted Rift. Her take is a chunky, clay-inspired wedge of a shoe with gasp-inducing details like eyeball motifs and puffy logos.

Baumeister’s work has a cult following because it puts a focus on handmade details in an unusual, raw way. But she also has always gravitated towards big, voluminous shapes that take up space in an uncompromising way, with an element of sportiness. At her New York Fashion Week debut, for instance, Paralympian sprinter Scout Bassett opened the show. Sculpted dresses with bell-shaped bottoms, architectural pointed blouses, and surreally satisfying pleated dresses were put on full display with banana-shaped pouches, spiked necklaces, and, of course, that signature hat done in various colors like lemon yellow.

“I think because there’s so much out there, I feel like making statements to show creativity,” Baumeister says. “I feel like that’s why you have to push because there’s just too much boring stuff out there.” The designer describes her house codes as O lines and A lines, always with a voluminous shape. “My approach is always to make a garment kind of feel three-dimensional. There might be a drape from the side view, but it’s all very considered. It comes from hand draping, working with the fabric, and seeing what the fabric can do with its structure.”

Born in a tiny town in the Black Forest in southern Germany, Baumeister went to tailoring school at the age of 16 before studying fashion design at the Pforzheim University of Applied Sciences. She later moved to New York City to study at Parsons in the MFA Fashion Design and Society program and officially founded her line when she graduated in 2014. In 2023, she won the CFDA/Vogue Fashion Fund, and in 2025, she won the National Design Award for Fashion Design.

Earlier this year, the Costume Institute at the Metropolitan Museum of Art acquired her “Venus” dress and exhibited it at the “Costume Art” show. She works alongside her partner, Michal Plata, who also serves as the brand’s art director. The city continues to inspire her: “the energy is so special and so strong here that I feel like it kind of gives me that sort of harshness in design,” she says. “We’re very bold and kind of very daring. I don’t think I would do the same in Germany in my hometown.”

Though many of her pieces are designed in charcoal black, when she’s not working in neutrals, it’s neons. “I really want color to feel strong and bold rather than kind of too soft or too safe,” she says. Color is a storytelling method for her, and the brighter colors also represent energy. Baumeister continues to pull from the earliest days of her brand, like when she created clothing out of poured silicone. “I’m still very inspired by that, because it was not about caring about wearability, it was more like an art piece than clothing.” One of her brand missions is to show that garments don’t have to be traditionally sewn, or conventionally shaped. Another pillar for her is the accessories and footwear. “A bag talks so much about the character of a woman, and the shoe as well, because you can feel more feminine or more masculine or more in a certain way,” she says.

At the moment, she’s busy working on her own line (with a potential return to fashion week on her mind) after finishing her latest Nike collaboration, which just launched. The Nike collaboration includes clothing in addition to the shoes, like sculpted sweatpants accented with nose-shaped keyrings. “I always think shoes or sneakers nowadays look so much that they’re coming out of a machine and no one touched them and they’re just perfect,” she says of the collection. “I wanted to highlight that things are made by hand and there is a lot of hand work also in shoes.”

For the occasion, she actually hand-sculpted the shoes with clay in her atelier to form the final mold. That’s part of the reason why the presentation was so theatrical and involved a live sculptor: “I wanted to show how things are made. I want people to come to the thought that there’s no God-given talent, and you’re not born as a final product. Things have to be built from the ground up. There’s always another thing that you have to work on.”

That’s what makes her work so exciting. Baumeister is still most interested in what fashion can become when it refuses to conform to the typical shape.

Melitta Baumeister Yukon Sculpted Track Pants

#6795. 27 Creative Ways Designers Hide TVs for a Stylish, Screen-Free Environment

These genius tricks keep it accessible yet out of sight.

For years, many designers and design-loving homeowners have felt that having the TV on display is a major faux pas. Even though most of us have one and use it daily, there’s something unappealing about having a big black box against the wall and breaking up the interior flow. It’s become even more of an eyesore now that analog spaces are becoming more popular.

While one solution is to invest in a TV that can disguise itself as artwork, those come at a high price; the other option is to learn how to hide the TV in a way that doesn’t feel obvious or tacky, and thankfully, designers have a lot of ideas.

We’ve also included plenty of inspiring photos and expert tips to guide you along the way. If your goal is to reduce the visual presence of technology in your home, you’re in the right place. Keep reading to find inventive ways to make any room feel screen-free—at least when the TV isn’t in use.

Of course, you can hide your television the same way you'd hide anything else you wouldn't want on full display at all times: tuck it into a cabinet. At least, that's what designer Kylie Buhagiar at Melbourne, Australia-based Golden did. "In the main living room, the TV is discreetly integrated into a wall-hung metal joinery box, with hinged doors that elegantly stack back to reveal the screen when needed, maintaining a clean and uncluttered aesthetic," Buhagiar says. "This thoughtful design transforms the joinery into a sculptural piece of furniture, allowing the space to effortlessly transition from a main living room to a calm sitting area, enhancing the home's rich yet comfortable atmosphere."

The whole point of concealing the TV is to hide it, but sometimes, the mechanism used to hide the screen just draws more attention to the area. Gillian Segal, an interior designer based in Vancouver, British Columbia, came up with a clever solution that basically looks like the wall. "This room functioned as both a formal living space and informal family room (complete with movie nights and lots of kids' TV shows), so we wanted a design that felt both elegant and approachable," Segal explains. "We concealed the TV behind a very simple millwork door system that folds open and closed. It was a cost-effective solution that allowed other elements in the room to shine."

It’s no secret that many designers find the Samsung Frame TV to be beautiful, but designer Ariel Okin takes it a step further by adding a literal frame to the Frame. This hidden-TV tactic ensures that it matches other elements in the space, such as the fireplace screen, thus hiding it in plain sight.

If you don’t want to think about your TV when it’s off, you can cleverly camouflage it. In this living room, designer Emily Del Bello painted a shadowy accent wall black and mounted the TV there—you wouldn't even notice it at first glance.

In a room as traditional and cohesive as this, you probably don't want a big black rectangle interrupting your aesthetic, so do what designer Lynne Uhalt did and hide your TV in a closet. This guest room is heavily inspired by European interiors and architecture, but Uhalt still wanted the guests to be able to watch TV and relax in bed. As a design-friendly solution, she put the TV in one of the closets, which still boasts enough room to enough to hold clothes.

Designer John Wooden maximized limited square footage in his clients' cottage by cleverly hiding several items, including placing their TV in a beautiful credenza. One of the simplest methods to hide your TV is by incorporating it into furniture that already complements the space. This wooden mid-century piece fits perfectly into the room, making it an ideal spot for a hidden TV.

In a maximalist, highly colorful space like this, designer Shazalynn Cavin-Winfrey couldn't see how a TV could work in the room, so she decided to hide it in the green cabinet in the back. However, the designer took it a step further and covered the glass with fabric that pulls hues from the rest of the room.

Everick Brown designed a statement wall encased in custom steel with walnut insets to create a “floating credenza” for a hidden TV. A console table behind the sofa helps separate the two living room zones.

Designed to look like abstract artwork, Kristin Fine hid the TV behind modern paneling above the fireplace. It suits the neutral tones nicely and maintains the refined and grown-up atmosphere while also ensuring that it's family-friendly.

This living room sitting area revolves around the corner fireplace. To ensure that the TV didn't take up too much valuable visual space, designer Denise McGaha invested in a Samsung Frame and disguised it as a photograph that complements the blue and grey color scheme.

This outdoor living room, designed by Amanda Lindroth, is the perfect place to entertain and unwind. Look closely at the pagoda-shaped cabinet above the fireplace, and you'll notice that it opens to reveal a hidden TV. It was based on a design from St. Michael's Mount castle in England.

What do you do in a busy kitchen where the children love TV and the mom doesn't—but she does want to keep an eye on said kids? Enter the Samsung Frame, once again making it all work in family homes with competing interests and preferences, like in April Tomlin Interiors designer Sarah Rogers's kitchen here.

A bi-fold panel painting by Stuart Coleman Budd hides a TV, but the goal was to be transparent. “Bronze hinges honestly express that it’s a movable screen—that this is true kinetic art,” says architect Ken Pursley of Pursley Dixon Architecture.

This formal living room, designed by Mark Cunningham, doubles as a more relaxed and casual hangout room. To ensure that it could do both well, Cunningham built a hidden TV into the back wall shelf that pops up when the occupants want to unwind with a good movie.

Don’t hide it, just make it one with your wall. This gallery wall of various-sized framed prints blends in with the Samsung Frame TV, which has a wood frame and a rotating display of art images. “None of my clients' guests realize it's a TV when they first walk into the room,” says Natalie Myer of Veneer Designs.

Custom paneling over a fireplace elevates the television area in this bedroom lounge zone by Studio McGee. Flush with the fireplace facade and further disguised by a custom mirror, the hidden TV definitely will not be ruining the chic decor scheme here.

Related Story: Shea McGee Just Dropped a Cottagecore Home Collab With a Major Fashion Brand

Designer CeCe Barfield Thompson hid a TV behind a retractable antique mirror for maximum glamour. “In an oak-paneled Manhattan library, I designed an antique mirror to hide the wall-mounted television,” Thompson says. “The mirror's lower panels retract like a garage door to reveal an entertainment system behind. This mechanism allowed us to create a room that was incredibly functional, without sacrificing an ounce of beauty!”

Related Story: This Manhattan Apartment Showcases an Abundance of Antiques and Contemporary Art

Here's another example of custom cabinetry with a hidden TV. A sliding door flush with the wood accent wall makes this media room designed by Heidi Caillier extra discreet and pretty. After all, the custom upholstery, drapery, and grasscloth wallpaper are too good to get lost in the entertainment system shuffle.

Designer Nina Farmer took the most elegant approach possible—hiding the TV in a custom mirrored cabinet atop the mantel. “The living room needed a concealed TV due to the formality of the architecture,” she says. “Hand-blown mirror was used on the doors so that the enclosure fit with the original 1850s marble mantel. It has concealed hinges and no pull, so when it’s closed, you would never know what’s behind it.”

If you can’t outright conceal your screen, the key is to avoid the “black hole” effect when it’s off. “I always try to blend them into the architecture of the house,” says designer Eche Martinez. “We recently completed a project in Belvedere, CA, where the homeowners were hesitant about installing a TV in their living room. To solve this, we decided that the best way to divert attention from the TV when it was not in use was to have an oversized, freestanding piece of art right next to it. Clients loved it, and above all, the piece looks great in the room."

#6793. John Cho’s Latest Role Took Him Back to the Life He Almost Lived

In his latest movie, the Harold & Kumar star saw something in the mirror: the man he almost was.

One of John Cho’s strongest childhood memories involves wearing a cowboy hat. Decades ago, just after the now 54-year-old actor had arrived from South Korea with his family, there was something called (to the best of Cho’s memory) “Western Heritage Day.” While it sounds like a bad hashtag cooked up by a far-right podcaster, the way the Harold & Kumar star describes it, it was just dress-up fun.

“All the kids had to come to school in a cowboy hat and boots. And my Korean mother was like, What?” Cho tells me. “I don’t know how, but she scrambled and got us a ten-gallon hat and boots.”

Years later, on the set of Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother, Cho’s faint memory of rolling up to school all yee-haw and yee-howdy came flooding back. “When we did the fitting for Hwan, I was like, Oh, jeez. I’m doing this. This could have been me.”

For close to 30 years, John Cho has been one of the most consistently interesting—and reliable—actors in Hollywood. Since his 1997 debut in the bizarre indie comedy Shopping for Fangs (codirected by future Fast & Furious helmer Justin Lin), Cho has led a storied career in which he’s been finely tuned to the spirit of whatever story he was telling. Moviegoers first knew him in American Pie as a hormonal teenager who coined “MILF” and Frenched a photo of Jennifer Coolidge. Audiences have since seen him be serious (Searching), somber (Columbus), swoon worthy (the short-lived Selfie), and slick with swagger (Netflix’s Cowboy Bebop). With Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother, Cho was determined to show off a different side of himself.

In the acclaimed action thriller by Bassam Tariq, Mahershala Ali stars as Latif, a hitman, widower, and devout Muslim desperate for breast milk to feed his infant child. Among the forces working against Latif is Hwan (Cho), a U.S. Army veteran and pastor whose ministry is armed to the teeth with Bibles and rifles—and they’re locked and loaded on Latif’s family and community.

“I saw it almost as a coming-of-age action story,” Cho observes. “A father who has to grow up in the absence of his wife, and his daughter who has to grow up in the absence of her mother, and how they find one another in that grief where the stakes are as high as they could be. It is an American story. It is filled with people trying to practice their faith as honestly as they can, and it’s hard sometimes.”

The chance to play a red-blooded American of Korean descent allowed the actor to imagine a life he might have had. Like Hwan, Cho lived in Houston before his family settled in Los Angeles. His father was a minister in the Church of Christ, and he encouraged John and his younger brother to assimilate into American culture to survive. Cho’s portrayal of Hwan is an actor wearing the boots of a man he almost was. “The exercise for me was: How could I, John Cho, who lived in Houston, have become that person?” he says. “All kinds of things happened that led me to where I am now, but how could I become that person? It was a fantasy for me as an actor to do all these things. I find [Hwan] to be so psychologically interesting.”

Below, Cho unloads on his latest turn as a villain for Bassam Tariq’s acclaimed festival feature, how he looks back on #StarringJohnCho ten years later, and future projects that may or may not include a long-awaited sequel to a millennial comedy classic.

This interview has been edited for clarity.

Take us to the beginning of Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother. Bassam Tariq told me before that he wanted to have you involved from the start. How did you come aboard?

There were three elements. The script, the filmmaker, and Mahershala Ali. I read the script first. It made me return to my experience as a first-time father. That feeling of breast milk as the most precious resource on earth—I was like, This is getting me to an anxious place. It was an incredibly fulfilling read. Then I watched Bassam’s first movie, Mogul Mowgli, and was so impressed. Then the question was: Can I hold my own against Mahershala? I’m such an admirer of his. My fears were allayed when we met. I knew he was going to take care of me. He’s a real leader and made sure I was gonna do my best work.

We just talked. My personal malfunction is that I like to talk in abstract all the time. It frustrates people in my family. Bassam’s like that too. I was like, Okay, I have a kindred spirit. His script led us to a wide-ranging discussion. He had to take a leap with me, because I don’t think any of us knew what that character was going to be based on the script. There were blanks we had to fill in together. I wasn’t sure how it was going to work, but I trusted my partners to make sure everything felt okay.

Talk to me about becoming Hwan. What drew him to you as an actor?

One thing Bassam and I agreed on was that I didn’t want this portrayal of this right-wing preacher to be a commentary on anyone. There’s a world in which we play him as a crook or a charlatan. For this to work, I wanted to play him authentically to what he’s saying. He really believes this. I was excited to explore how someone gets there. I’ve seen so many kinds of people walking down political paths I wasn’t even aware of. I’m like, How does someone get there? I have been thinking about it in my personal life a lot, [and this movie] was a chance to explore all that.

How did your childhood in Houston, raised by a minister father, help you understand Hwan and other ethnic-minority conservatives?

You’re right to assume, but I don’t see any crossover between Hwan’s beliefs and the Christianity I grew up with. There’s little crossover, except maybe their fervent belief that they’re doing the right thing. I knew people in the churches I grew up [who had conviction] in what they were doing. I wanted to bring that into [my performance].

I’ve always known that there’s more variation in people of color than most people understand. If you watch CNN, you just see voting blocs. It’s more fractured than that, particularly for Asian Americans, because the political situations in their home countries can inform how they lean. So it’s always been obvious to me, and I thought it was cool that it was clear to Bassam. He wanted to shine a light on it.

I know Hwan isn’t the first time you’ve played an antagonist. I point to your guest role on How I Met Your Mother as an early indicator that—

It showed your range for treachery! But after years of playing good guys, fathers, and romantic leads, what do you hope people see in you after wearing cowboy hats and preaching about God with guns?

I feel privileged to have had as many shots at this as I’ve had and to have a variety within that is really delightful to me. It’s hard to get the opportunity to do something different. I hope people appreciate it. That’s all.

You said just now that you played Hwan as someone who believes so strongly in what he preaches. By the end of the film, it looks like Hwan himself doesn’t know what he believes in anymore. What’s your take on Hwan at the very end?

I’m not sure what the day after is like for Hwan. I think we’ve all had this experience of having our worlds shook. Even when we receive information that what we believe is wrong, we tend to go back to what we feel comfortable. I don’t know whether Hwan was changed permanently. But he could have been. He saw something extraordinary.

Ten years ago, you were the face of #StarringJohnCho. How do you look back at that moment? What do you think it did for your career and for the industry?

It’s hard to understand what it did for me. It’s not like people sent me scripts. It was a clever, intelligent way to have a conversation I thought we needed to be having. Look at this poster. Why is this bizarre? Is it strange to you? Why? It’s hard to have a conversation like that these days in a way that’s not preachy. I thought it was a brilliant thing, I’m glad we had that conversation, and I’m glad to have been part of that.

Do you think the conversation around representation has moved forward, or has it regressed? I wonder if #StarringJohnCho could have happened now if it hadn’t happened then.

Back then, I thought we were past the monoculture. Looking back now, that seems so much closer to the monoculture than now. Every year, I think it’s impossible to have a big discussion, because our information diet is so much more fractured than it was six or 12 months ago. My inclination is that it’s just harder to have a single conversation with a lot of people now. But who knows? [Hashtag creator] Will Yu was someone who had digital literacy and knew how to talk to people. There’s gotta be someone around the corner who can do that. The idea is still a worthwhile one to bring up. We need the next Will Yu. Maybe we need Will Yu to answer again.

About five years ago, you starred in Netflix’s Cowboy Bebop, a huge live-action adaptation of an influential anime. How do you feel about how that show was received? Was there ever talk of a season 2?

There was talk of a season 2. I’m struggling to remember what decisions were made about the direction. But I look back on it fondly. We didn’t get season 2, but I wish we had. I felt like I got the melody down. I’d like to go off melody and play with it a little more. I wish we could have done that. We came at it from a very venerant place. We really liked and respected the material, and we tried to honor that as best we could. I don’t have regrets except that we didn’t get to do more.

More recently, you debuted on Korean television in the series Tempest last year. After a long career as a Korean American in Hollywood, what culture shock did you experience working as a Hollywood actor in Korea?

I was so curious what that experience would be like. I, selfishly, just wanted to know what that was and if that was a real path to doing stuff [there]. There are differences that are significant, but the commonalities outweigh the differences. It’s mostly linguistic and cultural stuff. The talent level is so high. I’m optimistic we’ll see more of these coproductions. And personally, having been born in Korea, it was cool. I brought my family. We spent the summer, and it deepened the experience.

Your next project is something called The Off Weeks. What can you tell me about it?

It’s an Apple TV series. It stars Jessica Chastain, Ben Stiller, and Richard Gere. It’s a mystery, a comedy, a thriller. The attraction for me was to work with that cast, and I’m excited for you to see it. It’s bananas. I worked with all those actors, and they were all very different and all thrilling.

It’s been a long time since audiences have seen Harold and Kumar. I know a continuation has been thrown around for a long time. What’s the latest word?

There are people who are trying to give the people what they want. We’ll see if we can do it. It might be a good thing to make a Harold & Kumar 4. That’s all I’ll say.

#6792. The Best Dressed Celebrities at the LACMA Art + Film Gala

From Nicole Kidman in Chanel to Anya Taylor-Joy in vintage Pierre Cardin

Last night, a glamorous cross section of the art, film, fashion, and entertainment industries gathered at Los Angeles County Museum of Art for its annual Art + Film gala, which fundraises for the museum’s film initiatives, exhibitions, acquisitions, and educational programs.

This year’s guests decided to embrace a bit of play with their looks. The event is sponsored by Gucci; so fittingly we saw a lot of the brand on the red carpet. Rosie Huntington-Whiteley, who walked in the Spring 2027 show, embraced drama in a slinky, backless black gown with a slit not at the front, but up the back to reveal just a bit of bum cheek. When Demna spoke about this collection this past fashion month, he said he was most proud of the structural feats of his jersey dresses, light as air but engineered to hang just so. Whiteley walked the show alongside additional LACMA guests including Alex Consani, in a dramatically twisted full-skirted gown, Elsa Hosk, who wore a gray wool gown with a thigh-high slit, and Candice Swanepoel in a dress with full-length silver fringe and slits up to her hips.

Anya Taylor-Joy, styled by Law Roach, pulled from the Pierre Cardin archives, selecting a embroidered silvery-white slip with a ruffled organza evening coat. Kylie Jenner wore a look from couturier of the moment, Ashi Studios, that featured a halter neck gown with an allover deer-like animal print and an oversize tassel at the back. And Julia Garner wore a sculptural black taffeta gown from Loewe, which she paired with oversized earrings from Lisa Eisner.

Perhaps the most fitting look of the evening, Nicole Kidman pulled a much-talked-about dress from Chanel’s Spring 2027 collection by Matthieu Blazy. The look features a bodice designed to resemble burlap and a layered tulle skirt with darker panels added to create the appearance of age and wear. It references Degas’ 1881 sculpture “Little Dancer of Fourteen Years,” and is evocative of what this gala celebrates: Kidman, a movie star, in Chanel, one of the most important French fashion houses, in reference to Degas, one of the most renowned impressionist artists.

#6791. Jennifer Aniston on ‘The Rachel’ Revival, the Bob She Regrets, and LolaVie’s New Hairspray

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Jennifer Aniston may just be hair’s ultimate cultural icon. Her Friends character Rachel Green inspired one of the most coveted hairstyles of the ’90s: “The Rachel,” a voluminous, layered cut that sent countless women to salons in search of hair that looks as effortlessly chic as Aniston’s did on-screen. The irony? She remembers the style as anything but effortless. “I remember how much work that original haircut was!” Aniston tells ELLE exclusively.

Today, hair trends have shifted toward styles that feel more relaxed, lived-in, and less polished—including the modern version of “The Rachel” that features longer and softer layers. There’s also a greater emphasis on hair that looks and feels healthy, with movement and texture taking precedence over a stiffly styled finish. Even Aniston prefers the “undone” approach. “I don’t love anything that makes your hair stiff or crunchy,” she says. That philosophy is at the heart of the latest addition to her hair-care brand, LolaVie: the Weightless Medium Hold Hairspray. “We wanted to create a product that gives you hold without sacrificing the feel of your hair,” she adds.

Powered by adaptive peptides and a trio of tropical fruit-derived actives, the superfine spray sets your style in place while leaving behind touchable softness and a healthy-looking shine. The formula even makes second-day styling a breeze, with 450°F heat protection and 72-hour frizz protection.

In an interview with ELLE ahead of the product’s launch today, Aniston reveals her hair-care routine, her bob era, why she envies Sandra Bullock, and her favorite rom-coms. Read the full conversation below.

I try to keep it pretty simple. I always start with a good shampoo and conditioner, and then choose products depending on what my hair needs that day. If I’m blow-drying, I go for heat protection and a little smoothing. If I’m going for a more natural style, it’s all about hydration and movement.

We wanted to create something that gives you hold without sacrificing the feel of your hair. The Weightless Medium Hold Hairspray helps keep your style in place while still allowing your hair to move and feel soft and touchable.

I’ve always been curious about advances in science and medicine, especially when it comes to taking care of yourself, but I’ve learned it’s important to be discerning about where you get your information. I always do my research and have trusted sources and experts that I turn to. Just because something is trending doesn’t mean it’s right for you—or anybody, for that matter.

There’s so much information and so many new treatments available, which is wonderful, but it can also feel overwhelming. I hope the future is about getting back to the basics: sleep, movement, nutrition, managing stress, and taking care of yourself consistently. Technology can be amazing, but it’s not a replacement for the fundamentals.

I love that people are still inspired by it. The modern versions feel a little more relaxed and lived-in, which I love.

Many times throughout the years. Hair is such a fun way to experiment with how you look and feel, and sometimes you just want a change. I remember a couple of times thinking, A bob would be fun, so I took the leap...and then immediately started to let it grow back out. What can I say? I’ve found what works for me.

Sandy has the thickest head of hair on a woman over 40 that I’ve ever seen in my entire life! I have such hair envy! She is also one of my biggest LolaVie supporters. She’s ordered every product available from day one and continues to do so. I know she loves me, but God knows she doesn’t have to do that.

There are so many good ones! When Harry Met Sally, of course, is the quintessential rom-com. Rob Reiner and Nora Ephron—nobody did it better. The Philadelphia Story is also a classic.

It’s bittersweet. I’m proud of the journey we’ve taken Alex on and very grateful to have been part of telling these stories with such an extraordinary cast and crew. Our writers did an incredible job wrapping up not only Alex’s journey but the other characters we’ve come to love. You’ll just have to wait and see!

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

#6787. Designers Reveal the Fastest Way They’ve Updated a Living Room Under $100

A few small swaps can make your space feel brand new.

A living room is arguably the most popular spot of the house, so why not give it the tender loving care it deserves with a design refresh? Problem is, when it comes to most home projects, it’s easy to convince yourself good things only come to those who wait—or anyone willing to fork over a small fortune.

Fortunately, affordability and efficiency are no longer mutually exclusive. According to the pros, it’s possible to elevate a space swiftly and without spending much money. Want proof? Keep scrolling for the clever (and quick) ways designers have upgraded a living room for $100 or less.

Barbra Fordyce of Ginger Lemon Indigo says plants are “hands-down” one of the best low-cost, high-impact ways to spruce up your space. “Bringing something living into your living room instantly changes the mood, quite literally bringing life, nature and an organic beauty into the room,” explains the designer. While fresh foliage can look good anywhere, she loves how plants can add a “verdant richness” to neutral rooms without throwing off the entire color palette.

That said, houseplants aren’t a one-size-fits-all situation, so choose wisely.

“If you have high ceilings, you want the plant to be proportional to those dimensions,” Fordyce says. “If the plant or tree is too small in a large living room, it will feel dwarfed.”

For the perfect finishing touch, use your leftover budget to buy a beautiful planter. “The container should echo the feeling of your room,” she shares. “A basket, ceramic container or even a vintage wooden crate can all make beautiful ‘pots’ for your plants.”

Though there is such a thing as too many throw pillows, the right ones can make a difference. “A pillow and throw refresh can go a long way in bringing new life to your sofa,” explains Natalie Kaelin, an interior designer in Raleigh, North Carolina.

Of course, there’s no need to buy brand-new lumbar and square cushions on a whim: Simply purchase covers for your existing pillows and swap them out seasonally.

Neutral linen layers can conjure coastal grandmother vibes virtually anywhere, while plush, jewel-toned velvets offer an elegant, autumnal look. Come winter, embrace the holiday spirit with flannel fabric or a plaid repeat.

We might spend a lot of time talking about layered lighting or how to pick the perfect fixture, but we cannot stress it enough: Pay attention to your bulbs.

“Daylight LED bulbs are incredibly white, so they put out light akin to doctors’ offices and hospitals,” says Elizabeth Cross-Beard of Palm Henri in Baltimore. “A warmer temperate bulb gives a home a candle-light, cozy feel.” As a general rule of thumb, she recommends buying lightbulbs with a temperature of 2700K and avoiding anything above 4000K.

Cross-Beard says switching your bulbs can effectively “redesign” your space, and it’s shockingly inexpensive. “Depending on how many lights you have, it can cost as little as $50,” she adds.

If you want to upgrade your space without spending a cent, Blair Burton says to start with your furniture.

“Sometimes simply pulling furniture away from the walls and creating a more comfortable conversation area can completely change how a room feels,” the Austin-based designer explains.

After all, just because an armchair has been in the corner of your living room for years doesn’t mean it needs to go there. By doing a room rearrangement, you might end up using it more when it’s in front of a warm, sun-drenched window. Maybe you’ll even realize you need to pick up an ottoman to create the ultimate cozy space.

Another $0 hack? Burton also encourages her clients to “shop their homes.” “It’s practical, of course, but it's also about honoring the things they've collected and loved over the years,” she explains. “Sometimes finding an unexpected new home for a favorite piece is one of the most satisfying discoveries in a project.”

“Adding artwork, whether it's a gallery wall, a single large-scale piece, or something in between, makes a huge visual and personal impact in a living room,” says Molly Torres Portnof of DATE Interiors in New York.

Retailers like Minted and Society6 offer well-priced prints, but you can also reframe photographs and posters you already own. As long as you pay attention to scale and proportion, the ceiling’s really the limit. “Rules are meant to be broken,” Portnof says. “If it feels right and makes you happy, you're on the right track.”

If your living room is blessed with beautiful built-ins, you can fill them with treasures you already own, or buy a few new pieces for well under $100.

“Take the time to create vignettes,” advises Tennessee designer Katie Kiser. “More often than not, I find my clients have all the pieces for a well-choreographed space, but they're styled improperly.”

For an instant yet affordable upgrade, head over to your local antique store to pick up some art books, jars, or a trinket tray.

#6784. Your Houseplants Need a New Care Routine for Winter—Here Are 4 Easy Habits to Start

Give your indoor greenery a fighting chance until spring.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

With colder weather drawing our attention indoors, many of us swap outdoor garden maintenance for caring for our favorite houseplants. But while your spider plant, peace lily, or monstera may be safely tucked away inside, their needs change significantly during autumn and winter.

Reduced daylight, cooler temperatures, and drier indoor air can all affect the health of houseplants, meaning the care routine that worked perfectly in summer could actually do more harm than good during the colder months.

It turns out many of us are unknowingly making a few common mistakes that can leave indoor plants struggling through winter. Here are the habits to avoid, plus the simple swaps that will help keep your houseplants healthy until spring.

You might assume that moving your houseplant closer to a bright window is an excellent idea in winter to allow it to drink in as much sunlight as possible. And while this isn't necessarily a bad move, it just requires a little more thought.

“It’s always a good idea to move them closer to a bright window as darker days hit, but make sure to avoid cold drafts and radiators,” Mark Sage, B&Q's horticulture expert, tells us. “Cold drafts and dry air from radiators can all take their toll on indoor plants, leading to yellowing leaves, rotten roots or even plant fatality.”

Similarly, Sage suggests thinking about where the sun actually shines in your home throughout autumn and winter.

“Where natural light may be limited, consider investing in a plant light to give an extra boost, while rotating larger plants daily can help avoid leggy, awkward growth as they stretch out looking for the sunshine,” he suggests.

When houseplants start to look a little worse for wear and growth begins to slow over winter, it's tempting to step up your care routine. You might find yourself watering more often, tending to leaves more regularly, or even feeding plants in an attempt to encourage new growth.

However, Sage warns that this is often the wrong approach. In fact, during autumn and winter, “sometimes the best thing you can do for your plants is leave them alone!”

He explains: “In a slower growth period, plants generally need less water and feeding, and winter certainly isn’t the time for regular repotting.”

Instead, Sage recommends letting the soil dry out between waterings to help prevent root rot and giving plants time to rest. “It's worth giving them a boost of plant feed ahead of the winter months, but a little goes a long way,” he says. “Give plants their final dose at the end of autumn.”

During summer, when houseplants are actively growing, it's common to spread them throughout your home so you can enjoy a touch of greenery in different rooms. However, according to Sage, this can work against them once temperatures drop.

Instead, he recommends bringing your plants together during autumn and winter to help recreate the conditions many tropical houseplants naturally prefer.

“Give your houseplants a helping hand through winter by clustering them together to create a more humid microclimate, as many of these plants originally come from much hotter parts of the world,” he recommends. “Grouping several varieties close together can help maintain humidity around them, which can be particularly useful when central heating makes indoor air drier.”

While watering may have been your main plant-care task during summer, it's a mistake to neglect cleaning your houseplants over autumn and winter. Just like the rest of your home, plants can gather dust, and that build-up can make it harder for them to make the most of limited daylight.

“During winter, leaves can gather a layer of dust,” says Sage. “Give them a wipe down every one to two weeks with a microfibre cloth to support photosynthesis, and to help them benefit from even the smallest burst of sunlight.”

In fact, Sage suggests combining cleaning and watering occasionally by giving your plants a gentle shower.

“Use lukewarm water to boost humidity and get rid of any pests, but keep an eye on the temperature as if it is too cold you can shock the roots, but too warm and you can scald the leaves.”

#6783. A Guide to Cruelty-Free Skin Care

How to spot non-animal-tested formulas and the cleansers, serums, and moisturizers to start with.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Every skin care product should be cruelty-free, but in the world of beauty regulations, rules in the U.S. neither require brands to conduct animal testing to prove a product is safe nor prohibit the practice. Brands have proudly volunteered to be “cruelty-free” certified by organizations like Cruelty Free International (Leaping Bunny) and PETA, and a growing number of countries and states have banned or restricted animal testing. You can easily identify cruelty-free certified brands by their labeling (look for the bunny). And just because it’s not tested on animals doesn’t mean it’s any less effective or safe. To jump-start your not-animal-tested lineup or to introduce a new addition to your already existing range, here are some of the best cruelty-free skin care products.

This cream cleanser foams into a rich lather, gently clears away the day with the help of Japanese rice powder, and leaves behind hyaluronic acid and red algae so skin feels clean but still hydrated.

The Tri-ceramide complex in Byoma’s multitasking mask will help restore broken-down skin barriers and reinforce those already in good shape. The peel-off treatment reduces water loss, boosts collagen production, and includes calming cica to soothe redness and irritation.

If there’s a one-and-done moisturizer to add to your routine, make it this one. The lightweight Cloud Cushion cream is loaded with brightening and texture-smoothing niacinamide, complexion-evening peptides, multiple moisturizers (squalane and snow mushroom), and ceramides to keep skin from losing moisture.

For skin in need of advanced fine-line smoothing, a texture reboot, and serious free-radical-fighting, this potent antioxidant serum—which also has moisturizing hyaluronic acid and glycerin—is the answer.

A trio of proteins works in tandem with plant-derived exosomes and growth factors to smooth fine lines, improve elasticity, and boost collagen. If refining texture is the goal, this helps with that, too.

Retinol can be tricky with sensitive skin, but this firming moisturizer pairs the ingredient with ginseng ceramides that soothe any irritation, keep skin moisturized so the retinol doesn’t dry it out, and support the skin barrier. Copper peptides in the formula help skin look bouncier.

This fan-favorite sunscreen is also cruelty-free. The skin protector goes on completely clear, has a high SPF and antioxidants, and doubles as a makeup primer.

As the name suggests, Medicube’s Zero Pore Pads use a combination of alpha hydroxy acids and beta hydroxy acids to refine, unclog, and help pores look smaller (or nonexistent). The pads also have hyaluronic acid, soothing allantoin, and the anti-inflammatory panthenol—a combo that makes it a gentler choice for sensitive skin.

Part makeup, part eye treatment, DRMTLGY’s eye corrector is formulated in four tints for light coverage, has UV-blocking zinc oxide, and has moisturizing snow mushroom to keep the delicate under-eye area hydrated and plump, not puffy.

Another double-duty skin care-meets-makeup combo is this peptide-laced bronzing serum. Tiny capsules carrying the buildable tint are suspended in the serum, which is infused with smoothing bio-peptides and cacao extract to improve elasticity.

If you’re building a cruelty-free skin care regimen, don’t forget about the lips. Mango butter and argan oil are the hero ingredients in Versed’s lip mask, which delivers a smooth, moisturized, non-sticky finish.

#6782. Three Ways to Master the Noir Aesthetic

Dior’s American Psycho pieces bring a dark cinematic edge to modern menswear.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Film noir has given menswear some of its most enduring images: the broad-shouldered suit, the long overcoat, the shirt with its collar open just enough to suggest trouble. The clothes are sharp, the colors run dark, and the men wearing them tend to look like they've got somewhere important to be. Even when they don't.

You don't need a private investigator's wardrobe to borrow from the genre. A well-cut suit will get you most of the way there, while a graphic T-shirt or a statement bag can bring the look into the present. Dior's American Psycho T-shirt and Book Tote offer a particularly good entry point, drawing on the cover art of Bret Easton Ellis's novel for a dose of dark literary and cinematic attitude. Here's how to make the aesthetic work in three different ways.

The suit is the natural starting point. Classic noir leads tend to wear clothes with presence: broad shoulders, defined waists, full-length trousers, and jackets with enough structure to hold their shape. You can take those proportions as inspiration without dressing like you've wandered off a 1940s soundstage.

Start with a charcoal or black suit, a white or pale blue shirt, and black leather shoes. A long wool overcoat adds another layer and gives the silhouette a little drama. Pay attention to fit, particularly through the shoulders and trouser leg. The goal is a clean line from top to bottom, not a suit so tight you can't sit through dinner.

For a more relaxed interpretation, replace the dress shirt with a fine-gauge black knit or wear the jacket with dark, straight-leg trousers. The result retains the polish of classic Hollywood while feeling appropriate for a modern wardrobe. Save the fedora for a particularly committed evening.

Film noir has always had a strong visual identity. A graphic T-shirt lets you reference that world without committing to a full suit. Dior's black American Psycho tee, which features embroidered artwork based on the first-edition cover of Bret Easton Ellis's notorious novel, brings a literary reference to a familiar menswear staple.

The trick is to keep the rest of the outfit simple. Wear the shirt with charcoal trousers, black jeans, or a pair of dark, pleated wool pants. Add a structured blazer if you want to sharpen things up, or wear it on its own with leather boots for a more casual look. Either way, let the graphic take center stage.

There's a good bit of irony in wearing American Psycho on your chest. Patrick Bateman, the novel's image-obsessed protagonist, treats clothes as a measure of status and a way to distinguish himself from everyone around him. The reference has some bite, particularly when paired with understated basics rather than an outfit that tries to do too much. You get a little cinematic attitude without looking like you're headed to a costume party.

A bag can change the character of an outfit, especially when the rest of the clothes are fairly restrained. The Large Book Tote features the same book-cover artwork as the matching tee. It has a practical shape, with room for a 15-inch laptop and everyday essentials, and can be carried by hand or over the shoulder.

The graphic gives the bag a personality of its own, so there's no need to build an entire outfit around it. Carry it with a long charcoal overcoat, a dark crewneck sweater, straight-leg trousers, and polished loafers. The combination nods to noir's shadowy palette while the bag adds a more contemporary point of interest. You don't need to dress like a private eye to make the reference land. A few well-chosen pieces will do the job.

Start with a dark suit, tailored trousers, a long overcoat, and polished leather shoes. For a more casual take, pair a graphic T-shirt with dark denim or pleated trousers. Keep the silhouette sharp and the palette restrained.

Mix classic tailoring with contemporary pieces. A fine-gauge knit can replace a dress shirt, while a graphic tee or statement bag adds personality to an otherwise understated outfit. The goal is to borrow from film noir's visual language without dressing for a period drama.

Black, charcoal, gray, navy, and white form the foundation of the look. High contrast, such as a pale shirt against a dark suit, is especially effective. You can also introduce muted brown or olive for a softer take that still feels true to the aesthetic.

Film noir style tends toward dark palettes, sharp tailoring, and an understated, slightly brooding mood. Old Hollywood style is a broader category that includes everything from formal tuxedos to relaxed studio-era tailoring. The two overlap, but film noir offers a more specific and shadowy approach to dressing.
