#6781. 33 Bedroom Plants That Bring a Calming Touch to Your Space

Do you have one of these in your room yet?

Want to improve your sleep naturally? Try adding a few plants to your bedroom. Beyond offering charm and a cozy atmosphere, plants have been linked to mental and physical benefits, including sleep. Research suggests that being around greenery can lift your mood, ease stress, and promote a greater sense of well-being. Bringing a touch of nature into your sleeping space may be a simple way to add those positive qualities to the room.

Plants may also help calm the mind and move the body from an alert state toward a more restful one, potentially supporting better sleep. A past study linked indoor greenery with more positive emotions and fewer negative ones, which may help you fall asleep faster. The findings point to a broader connection between greenery and well-being, including its possible role in a restful bedroom. It’s easy to see why biophilic design—an approach that evokes nature indoors—has become a favorite among designers.

Ahead, find the best plants for your bedroom. We made sure the options would thrive in all types of lights, but especially in low light, so that whether you’re a beginner or an experienced green thumb, you will find the right verdant friend to grow.

The Aspidistra elatior thrives in low to bright shade, making it perfect for bedrooms that don’t get too much direct light. A fun fact: This plant historically was referred to as the “bar room” plant or “beer plant” because it could survive smoky, low-light conditions in Victorian-era taverns and halls. It is non-toxic to humans, cats, and dogs so if you have pets this is a great option.

Native to the rainforests of Brazil, the Maranta leuconeura is now a common indoor houseplant. During the day, the oval leaves lie flat to soak up the light and at night, the leaves fold upward like praying hands, hence the name.

For a trailing plant that thrives in bright, indirect light get a wax plant. It appreciates higher humidity levels so if you live in an older home with fluctuating humidity, this is the verdant plant baby for you.

For beginner and intermediate plant owners, the nerve plant is a great option because they thrive in most indoor conditions, remain compact, and are safe for pets. They will signal to you what they need so you can water and choose the soil for it accordingly.

Pilea peperomioides, or the Chinese money plant, is a cheerful-looking, easy-to-propagate plant that is incredibly low-maintenance. It's a great plant for a sunny bedroom. All plant parents need to do is place it in bright, indirect sunlight and water only when the top inch of soil feels dry.

Aglaonema tolerates very low light levels, so it's ideal if you don't have large windows in your bedroom. Water it when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The snake plant doesn't mind low light—and because it's a succulent, it only needs to be watered every week or two.

Purported to bring good luck, the money tree has a nice, modern vibe—plus, it's not fussy and grows quickly. Give it medium light, water it every week or two, and it'll be happy.

The pothos plant tolerates any light level from low to high. (That means it's super-easy to keep alive!) Just don't water it until the top few inches of soil feel dry to the touch—overwatering is the only real way to kill this plant.

Lavender has long been considered beneficial for relaxation and sleep. Although most research has studied the use of lavender essential oils specifically, having a live lavender plant in your bedroom probably can't hurt. It needs bright light, so make sure it's on a sunny windowsill or use a grow light. Water when the top inch or two feels dry to the touch.

This handsome vining plant is a great choice for new plant parents. Its glossy, heart-shaped leaves are beautiful as they spill over the edges of nightstands or bookshelves–and it's tolerant of low light levels. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

This adorable fern is one of the best to raise indoors. Its thick, waxy leaves retain water better than many other ferns that have finer fronds—and it won't leave a mess by dropping foliage every day. Give it bright indirect light and water when the top inch of soil feels dry.

This fun plant looks like an old-fashioned ric rac trim. It will grow like crazy in bright indirect light—and because it's a succulent, it needs watering only every week to 10 days.

This social media darling is striking and easy to grow—it just needs bright indirect light. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The peace lily will adapt well to low light levels, but it needs bright indirect light to bloom. Water when the top inch or two feel dry.

This cute palm that looks just like a messy ponytail likes bright indirect light. And the bulbous base stores water, so it needs a drink only every few weeks when the soil is totally dry.

This handsome plant boasts a beautiful upright form, so it makes an eye-catching statement in the bedroom. Give it bright indirect light, and water when the top few inches of soil are dry. As an outdoor landscape plant, it will flower, though that's unusual indoors.

If you're looking for low-maintenance houseplants, this is the way to go! It will tolerate low light and is often grown in water. In water, change it weekly; in soil, water so that it stays slightly moist.

This cute tabletop palm will add loads of style to your bedroom. It prefers bright indirect light, but it will tolerate medium light better than many other types of palms. Water when it's slightly dry to the touch.

#6778. Marshals Is Ruining the Dutton Family Legacy

The Yellowstone spinoff wants to move on from its Kevin Costner-sized shadow, but it can’t.

Ever since John Dutton (Kevin Costner) died, the Yellowstone-verse has sought to do away with everything he stood for. Without the patriarch in charge, it’s dawned on his children, Kayce (Luke Grimes) and Beth (Kelly Reilly), that the man was too violent and too stubborn. So, Kayce sold the ranch and resolved to put his violent life behind in the spinoff, Marshals, while Beth moved to Texas in Dutton Ranch to try ranching her own way.

It’s not really working out. Beth and her husband Rip (Cole Hauser) basically just moved the whole Dutton operation to Texas. And as much as Kayce’s plan to turn his last remaining acres of the Yellowstone Ranch into an equine therapy center would follow through on his plan for peace, he’s too busy continuing his father’s cycle of violence by running into danger as a member of a team of U.S. Marshals every week. Why? Well, because peace for the Duttons just isn’t good TV.

Still, Marshals tries its best to weave the framework into season 2. The latest round of episodes to kick off the second season deals heavily with post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), and the various ways the Marshals team handles their crazy, violent lives. Mostly, it’s by bottling it up and never speaking about it. After killing a man in the season 1 finale, Kayce’s son Tate (Brecken Merrill) says a line in season 2, episode 3 that could have been plucked directly from a Dutton family quote book: “What happened stays where it is.”

But try as they might, the Dutton children are becoming their father anyway. By now in the task force spinoff, Kayce’s likely killed more men on the ranch than his father ever did. And though he’s not branding people like cattle anymore—which still haunts Kayce’s dreams in season 2, episode 4—even John Dutton gave up that practice after Yellowstone season 3.

Most likely, the Marshals team just wrote themselves into a trap. They might think they’re providing Kayce good backstory by tackling his inner quest for a peaceful life, but when he heads to work to kill criminals every morning, you can’t really believe the guy. Is he trying at all? And since he’s clearly not, maybe we should focus on that instead...?

So, at the end of episode 3, Kayce demands that Tate break his family’s generational cycle and take his ass to therapy. “It’s the last place my father would’ve taken me, and the last place I would’ve gone myself,” he says.

Two episodes have passed since that moment, and we haven’t seen Tate actually go yet. Plus, Kayce hasn’t exactly set a good example. Despite ordering his son to open up, Kayce didn’t give us any hints that he’s decided to see a therapist himself. And that equine center? We’ll be lucky if we ever see it until Marshals is over for good.

#6776. Three Clues That Suggest Callum Turner Could Already Be the Next James Bond

The final screen tests are seemingly finished, but we may already have our answer.

It's been over five years since the release of No Time to Die, Daniel Craig's final outing as James Bond, and the speculation surrounding his successor has hit a fever pitch.

Recent reports suggested that the final screen tests have already happened. It's widely thought that Jack Lowden, Callum Turner, and Josh O'Connor are the three actors vying for the role—and it's anyone's guess who will get their hands on the hallowed tux. Heck, if The Hollywood Reporter’s recent dispatch is to be believed, it might even be Robert Pattinson or Nicholas Hoult.

However! You don't have to be a super spy to see that there are some clues pointing to a specific candidate—namely, one Callum Turner. Below, we've broken down the evidence.

Though he's currently filming the remake of Possession alongside Margaret Qualley and in pre-production for The Comebacker, 2027 isn't looking especially busy for Turner, one of Britain’s most in-demand young actors. We can assume that Bond 26 will go into production next year, so a wide-open schedule would prepare Turner for the intense, high-commitment shooting schedule that Amazon will likely stick to.

Turner recently stepped down as a director of Twentytwo Films Ltd., the production company that he founded last year with his now-wife Dua Lipa. Given that the final screen tests also supposedly took place in the last few days, do we think this could be a result of some complicated contract negotiations? Or maybe he's just freeing himself up even more?

This isn’t hard evidence, exactly, but an argument in Turner's favor. One thing that people noted about Daniel Craig when he was first announced as Bond was that he wasn't exactly a blockbuster actor, and his lineup mostly consisted of strong, independent films, really only including one that signaled he'd make a brilliant Bond: Layer Cake.

Craig then, indisputably, went on to be one of the best-loved 007s to date, and we think that Turner's range over the past ten years makes him a pretty chameleonic, versatile candidate for the role. Across the likes of Green Room, Rose of Nevada, and Rosebush Pruning, we've really seen Turner take on all sorts of mystery, thriller, and action films. With Denis Villeneuve at the helm, there's no way that Bond 26 will be a simple spy story, and Turner's ability to take on all kinds of genre and tone makes him the perfect partner for whatever Villeneuve's vision will be.

#6774. The Tommy Hilfiger Gifts Worth Giving

A little alpine spirit goes a long way, especially when it comes wrapped and ready to give.

Finding a great holiday gift means knowing what someone will actually love receiving—and ideally, using long after the decorations come down. For the friend who looks forward to a weekend in the mountains, that might mean a cozy knit or a warm accessory. For the frequent traveler, a polished wallet or a toiletry bag could be just the thing. And sometimes, a little fragrance is all it takes to make a gift feel special.

Tommy Hilfiger has been putting its own spin on American classics since 1985, drawing on the easy confidence of East Coast prep and the sporty influences that have long defined its look. This heritage feels right at home in the brand’s alpine campaign, where familiar staples take on a winter-ready appeal. The result is a gift selection that moves from cozy cold-weather favorites to thoughtful little luxuries, with plenty of Tommy’s signature style.

A cable-knit sweater is a gift she’ll reach for all winter. This lightweight cotton version has a relaxed, easy fit, classic crew neck, and ribbed trim. The traditional knit gives it a timeless appeal, while the soft pink shade adds a fresh touch. It’s an easy choice for everything from a weekend in the mountains to a casual day around town.

For the friend who always feels a chill, this knit hood is a fun alternative to the usual winter hat. The design features a monogram logo for a signature touch and offers extra coverage for cold-weather walks, snowy weekends, and everything in between.

Gloves are easy to overlook when shopping for gifts, which makes a good pair all the more welcome. This wool-blend knit version is a gift with staying power, adding a classic finishing touch to a cold-weather look. Tuck them into a gift box for someone planning a mountain escape, or give them to the friend who never leaves home without checking the temperature first.

This trifold wallet gets its personality from a buckle accent and monogram detail, giving the compact design a polished finish. It’s a nice option for someone who likes a bit of branding without carrying a logo-heavy bag, and its smaller profile makes room for the rest of the essentials.

There’s something especially nice about unwrapping a new fragrance during the holidays. Tommy Hilfiger’s Freedom comes in a generous 3.4-ounce bottle, making it a gift with staying power.

This monogram-print toiletry bag from Tommy Hilfiger gives skin care, makeup, and other travel necessities a dedicated place, while the allover pattern makes it easy to spot in an overstuffed suitcase. Consider it a small upgrade for the friend who always has another trip in mind.

#6772. SNL’s You Can See Everything Parody with Druski Resurrects a Classic Eddie Murphy Bit

Back in 1984, Eddie Murphy had fun with John Howard Griffin’s 1961 book Black Like Me with a Saturday Night Live sketch titled White Like Me. In it, Murphy caked on makeup to pass as a white man, flipping his world upside down. It’s still pretty funny to watch after all these years, even if putting on makeup to resemble another race for a joke is walking a tightrope. So it’s damn impressive that Saturday Night Live just got away with it again, with Druski.

On the most recent Saturday Night Live, guest star Druski was at the center of one of the night’s best sketches (and maybe the only one worth watching), which was also the inevitable parody of You Can See Everything, titled Dru Can See Everything.

The premise is simple: What if, before Nathan Fielder, Druski first filmed the final days of freedom for Elizabeth Holmes? Cast members Ben Marshall and an incredible Jane Wickline co-star and elevate the sketch from a mildly funny “haha” to “holy shit they’re actually committed here.” Clocking in just four minutes long, the sketch runs through the highlights of You Can See Everything’s first hour: Elizabeth (Wickline) and her partner Billy (Marshall) living at home, cooking eggs, showing off the controversial Theranos machine, and even Billy’s barenaked shower. But the one change is swapping out Fielder for Druski. And it makes a huge difference.

You can see everything (the sketch) below.

While Fielder’s social awkwardness and determination to ensnare a charlatan without falling victim himself is part of the movie’s drama, SNL knows that Druski would just call out Holmes and Evans’s bullshit straight up. No hesitation. “I just want people to see me as an ordinary person. Can you do that, Druski?” asks Wickline’s Elizabeth Holmes.

Says Druski, “Based on what I’ve seen so far, I’mma go ahead and say no. Y’all got some of the weirdest, freakiest vibe I’ve ever seen.” Grabbing the bowl of eggs, he then turns to the camera: “Whatever they said this bitch did, she done did that sh-t.” (Remember the eggs scene is like, ten minutes into the movie.)

Funny as Druski is, the real MVPs are Wickline and Marshall, even if all their jokes are about the size of Elizabeth and Billy’s saucer-pan eyes. The now spoofed-to-death “I’m always real” scene gets its SNL roast too, with Druski asking literally point-blank, “Are you trying to f-ck me?” Then, when Elizabeth shows off Theranos’s supposedly miraculous machine, Druski doesn’t hold back. “Listen white girl, I’mma say this with all love, right? This the most Temu ass piece of sh-t I done ever seen in my life.” Cut to “Billy” and his weird lizard.

(By the way, we know Saturday Night Live’s production design is top-tier, but it’s frankly amazing they were able to build a virtually precise replica of Holmes’s oceanfront rental and recreate the lighting conditions to make the shots look passably real. Remember that they shoot these things in under a week. I demand a behind-the-scenes video of how they pulled this one off.)

The sketch peaks at the end, when Druski emulates Fielder’s method for staging reenactments by putting on White Chicks-style makeup (to better understand Elizabeth, of course). There’s no getting around it: the man looks freaky. Uncanny. He’s like Fiona from Shrek in a J. Crew sweater. Which makes the joke’s reveal a funny jump scare. Post-midterms, it’s honestly awesome that this kind of woke comedy is “back” (if it actually ever left). I’m not smart enough to suss out if whiteface is actually acceptable. I just know Druski and the SNL team killed it this week.

#6771. Artist Quentin Jones Launches a Surrealist Jewelry Collection

With Nocturne, the House of Quentin Jones founder brings the playful, sculptural language of her homewares to a collection of wearable art.

Above: Artist Quentin Jones wears pieces from her new jewelry collection while posing in front of tapestries Circe’s Garden and Western Moon.

British mixed-media artist Quentin Jones has quite the varied resume—one that spans artistic disciplines and collaborators as varied as Chanel, Apple, and Miley Cyrus. Now based in Brooklyn, her practice blends filmmaking, photography, collage, and painting.

Last year, Jones turned her eye towards interiors, launching a homeware line and design studio called House of Quentin Jones. The brand debuted with a collection of tapestries and ceramic sculptures that are both architectural and playful, leaning into Jones’s moody, dreamy look. Now, after a year of HOQJ, Jones is releasing new styles—and adding jewelry to the mix.

Nocturne, a collection of sculptural jewelry, translates Jones’s eclectic design language into wearable art. She pulled motifs like eyes, hands, and celestial forms from her tapestries and ceramics and incorporated them into the pieces. The collection features earrings, pendants, chains, and cuffs that balance black and white, pearl and onyx, organic and sculptural shapes.

“I’m interested in jewelry as sculpture,” Jones tells ELLE Decor. “A cuff wraps around an arm, a collar frames the neck, a tassel moves. The body becomes part of the composition. I want the pieces to have a presence of their own, while leaving room for the personality of the person wearing them.”

As she enters this new category, Jones is also beefing up her existing offerings. She’s adding new quilted tapestries to the studio’s repertoire and iterating on previous designs. With these new pieces, she says, “The palette has become softer and earthier—ivory, sand, and burgundy—but the compositions still have that surreal, graphic quality that runs through my work.”

But she isn’t stopping there. Jones has big dreams for the future of her eponymous studio, a “House” built larger with each new release. Next up, she’d like to expand the accessories category further, hoping to add bags, scarves, and other wearable pieces to her lineup.

“I’ve spent years working between art, film, and fashion,” she says. “I’m building towards a collection of things that can accompany you through your life—from what hangs on your wall to what you choose to wear.”

#6769. Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce Re-Created One of Their First Public Moments After the Chiefs’ Win

Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce made a rare move following the Kansas City Chiefs’ win over the New York Jets yesterday. The newlyweds left Arrowhead Stadium together, re-creating one of their first public moments as a couple by driving off in a vintage Cadillac Coupe DeVille. Onlookers filmed the happy couple waving to the crowd as they departed.

When Swift attended her first-ever Chiefs game in September 2023, the pair did the same thing, leaving in Kelce’s 1970 Chevrolet Chevelle 408 Stroker convertible. They seem to have a thing for the classic cars, and even raffled off a 1970 Chevelle at their wedding, an apparent homage to the 2023 moment.

The singer attended Kelce’s game against the Jets with her parents and her friend Karlie Kloss. It marked Swift’s first appearance in years with Kloss, who also attended the singer’s wedding in July.

Swift usually leaves Arrowhead Stadium undetected. Back in October 2025, she told BBC Radio 2’s Greg James that her greatest skill as a spy would be “entering and exiting the building without being seen. Just put me in a garbage can, roll me, I don’t care. Honestly, I can fit in like, a purse. Sometimes I just can’t deal with it [the paparazzi and attention] and in those times, I won’t deal with it. I’m just digging tunnels under every building I go into, airlifting in through the skylight.”

In January 2025, Parade spoke with Ty Richmond, president of event services at Allied Universal, who detailed how intricate plans can be to get VIPs in and out of football games. “When you have a very high-profile individual [coming to an event], the logistics of that person getting into the venue and getting into the specific location where they need to sit is a job and a process in itself,” he said, adding that for someone at Swift’s level of fame, thousands of security professionals could be involved in days of preparation.

“If, for instance, there are fans that are known to be targeting [a star] and/or have been a threat in the past because they’re fixated on a talent or a personality, those are all very specialized, very confidential issues that have to be understood and mitigated properly,” he added.

#6765. 7 Ways to Wear the Jeans-and-a-Blazer Look for Fall and the Most Versatile Styles to Shop Now

It’s a longstanding partnership for good reason.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The idea of a uniform isn’t new. In fact, that’s kind of the point: it prioritizes a sense of timelessness that defies trends. The winning partnership that emerged from recent street style sightings, however, felt newly relevant—not a flash in the pan coupling, but one that has what you might call an unshakable bond: jeans and blazers.

The beauty of this particular uniform is that it doesn’t come prepackaged. Instead, you can pick from a plethora of different jeans—are you a barrel-leg, capri, or boot-cut kind of person?—and then opt for the blazer that best suits its proportions.

Keep scrolling for the seven combinations that have been cherry-picked for the insouciant energy that’s part and parcel of the jeans-and-blazer look.

Military jackets were spotted across the street style scene during SS27 shows. Combining the statement piece with casual denim works to balance out the look that will work well into winter.

Here, a pair of ’70s-inspired, front-pocket jeans are the perfect match for a double-breasted, cropped blazer. Plus, choosing a straight-leg silhouette helps to accentuate the padded shoulders of the jacket, allowing a simple look to transform into a statement.

A smarter pair of baggy jeans—either because of a darker wash or lack of distressing at the knees—is the perfect partner for a suede jacket, which requires no introduction after its dominance on the pavements of fashion month.

For a super-polished take on the combination that’s suitable for work (and whatever comes after), a scarf blazer paired with straight-leg jeans is the ultimate power couple.

Like capri pants, Bermudas are still a viable option in autumnal temperatures. As Gabriella Karefa-Johnson shows, you’ll want to wear your pair with a nearly knee-high boot, especially when it contrasts with a patchworked top half that limits the amount of bare skin exposed. Smart.

The best way to remove the slouch factor from cuffed jeans? Add a single-breasted pinstripe blazer with one button fastened.

The curvaceous nature of a barrel-leg jean strikes a brilliant contrast with a blazer that’s cropped to either the waist or hipbone, especially if, like this streetstyler, you keep the rest of your look pared-back enough to let its sculptural lines sing.

#6763. A Kitchen in Bloom: Inside Miele’s Lush Botanical Oasis Where Art Meets Design

Step inside Art Basel as visionaries Tiffany Howell and Asher Young merge cutting-edge technology with untamed botanical romance, staging an immersive sensory landscape you don’t just see, but feel.

Miele crafts appliances for enduring performance, but they also curate experiences that engage all the senses. Imagine the intuitive precision of a sleek induction cooktop that subtly adjusts temperature on its own; the soft, inviting glow of a French door refrigerator that reveals a curated bounty within ample space; the alluring aroma of a built-in coffee system greeting you each morning; and the calming, almost meditative hum of a steam at work. Each of these choreographed moments elevate Miele appliances beyond a functional tool into something you fundamentally feel. By blending groundbreaking innovation and sustainable features with timeless, minimalist design, Miele frees you to embrace the pure joy of cooking and to savor the intimate, everyday moments that make a house a true home.

For this year’s Art Basel, the ultimate intersection of culture and creativity, Miele brings together two visionaries to redefine the culinary space. Tiffany Howell of Night Palm Design is passionate about creating cinematic homes that tell a deeply personal story. Asher Young is a multidisciplinary artist known for merging art with technology to create immersive spectacle. Together, they have imagined a sensory kitchen built entirely within a lush botanical garden setting. Here, the wildness of the natural world frames the precise German engineering. It is a space where the smooth, handleless silhouettes and cool touch of Miele's Obsidian Black glass stand in striking relief against their organic surroundings, harnessing technology for a design that is both tactile and state-of-the-art. “We used the sleek, matte black Miele appliances as the driving force to bring this to life,” says Howell of the striking visual contrast between nature and cutting-edge machines. “They’re like the perfect little black dress—timeless, elegant, and grounding the entire room.” A deep respect for the environment is also reflected in Miele engineering, which uses innovative technology that preserves ingredients longer—ensuring that the natural world isn't just an aesthetic inspiration, but a core priority for the brand.

Howell’s signature nostalgic romance blends with Asher’s analytical thinking and knack for creating physical, interactive experiences to make the space come alive. Young's touch ensures the installation feels genuinely dynamic, translating the unseen power of Miele’s technology into a captivating physical experience. “How can we create a space that rebalances and also is a place that you can actually engage with? So you can smell the flowers while you're washing your hand. You can feel the cool breeze as you open the fridge,” explains Young. “All these elements is not just for the sake of a visual presentation, but actually to experience and be inside.” The result is an experiential reminder that kitchens should always reflect your personality and passions. “We both said let’s bring our shared language together,” he recalls. The finished design is the undeniable heartbeat of the garden, a space with one foot rooted elegantly in the past and the other stepping boldly into the future.

“A kitchen expresses how you live, what you love and desire, and it’s also where your most cherished memories are made,” says Howell. As the Miami sun sets, the botanical space seamlessly transitions into an elegant, moody enclave for evening entertaining, inviting you to linger just a little bit longer. And in a Miele kitchen, those memories continue into the next day, beginning the moment the fragrant aroma of freshly brewed coffee announces the limitless possibilities of a brand-new morning.

Experience the convergence of culinary art, design, and innovation firsthand. Explore the full Miele collection and begin designing a kitchen that awakens every sense.

#6761. Shooting Matt Damon in the Desert and Leaning Into Discomfort

It was a beautiful day to be indoors. In barren Lancaster, California, the temperature had hit the mid-90s, and the feels-like figure in my weather app topped triple digits. We were professional. We were stoic. But at a certain point, you just give in to the heat of the desert. At a certain point, you wilt a little.

Matt Damon, mercifully, never did. Damon may be the least-debated pick in GQ Men of the Year history—he’s still riding the billion-dollar wave of The Odyssey, one of the great cinematic accomplishments this century. Damon was also, by some distance, the most unbothered man on that desert set, and all while being the only one wearing heavenly black tailoring (a peak-lapel Norton & Sons jacket, and glorious mohair Celine pants). It was Bourne-esque. Odyssean, even! Three decades into being a film star, Damon has familiarity with endurance, both creative and literal. In his cover story, he walks our Frazier Tharpe through the physical cost of making Christopher Nolan’s epic, up to and including a night in Scotland on which he didn’t sleep at all. The piece is a portrait of a movie star who still treats the job as a job, most of all when it is inconvenient, unfair, or uncomfortable.

Discomfort has been something of a theme for me this year. I will confess that I knew 2026 would be an administrative ordeal from the moment I accepted this new role at GQ. There was a move from London to New York (including a cat, whose opinion was not consulted). There was a home to find. And there was the discovery that the land of the free has impressive disdain for a grown man with no credit score. None of it was enjoyable. All of it, I think, is good for me.

I have the good fortune to work with many brilliant editors and writers. A recurring note I like to give is: Where is the tension? Without tension, there’s no story. I am not a confrontational person, but I find confrontation on the page irresistible. I want you to stumble, in every issue of GQ, onto something uncomfortable, or uncomfortably new. A magazine that agrees with you from cover to cover is a magazine that you have already read. And I would hate to do that to you.

This issue does not let you off lightly. After some life advice from an honest-to-God astronaut who went deeper into space than anyone that’s ever lived, you’ll read about Chris Rock’s unvarnished thoughts on divorce, discomfort, and reclamation, and Andrew Garfield’s very real anxieties in playing Sam Altman, the complicated man who’s been described as the Oppenheimer of our time. From there, we hang out with the men risking the unknown to get hot on peptides, which is, for better or worse, the defining male-vanity story of 2026.

A few months ago I started putting my email address in these letters (adam@gq.com). You’ve been generous. You’ve been thoughtful. A handful of you have been unhinged. But one note showed up way more than I thought it would: NO POLITICS, PLEASE.

I understand the impulse. I know These Modern Times are stressful, and wouldn’t it be nice if GQ were a place of apolitical tranquility? Alas. GQ has always taken men seriously as full human beings, and full human beings can handle tough conversations, opposing viewpoints, and a little gray area. There’s nothing noble about steering clear of the uncomfortable.

Which brings me to our special package on Cornell, anchored by a dispatch from campus by Savannah Sobrevilla. It is about an alleged sexual assault. It is also about the men on the sidelines of sexual assault. Some of the young men we meet through the story are students who are earnestly and inelegantly trying to grapple with how serious the alleged crimes are, with the crude and cruel culture that surrounds them, and with what role, exactly, they play in exacerbating that culture—or could play in diminishing it. It’s not easy reading, but not everything should be. ✦
